Bibliographic: АЛМАТЫКІТАП БАСПАСЫ, 2020 (RU). Set ISBN 978-601-01-4193-3; part ISBNs P1 978-601-01-3863-6, P2 978-601-01-3862-9, P3 978-601-01-3861-2, P4 978-601-01-3860-5; © Акпаева et al., text, 2019. 2023 reprints: set 978-602-02-1178-7 (RU, files 1248–1251, 2020 part ISBNs retained), set 978-602-02-1182-4 (KZ, files 1267–1270, with its own part ISBNs P1 978-601-01-3895-7, P2 978-601-01-3894-0, P3 978-601-01-3893-3, P4 978-601-01-3892-6). Pagination: P1 152 pp (2019: 148), P2 144, P3 160, P4 144.

**Difference verdict (TOC-compared for all four parts, front/back matter compared for Part 1):** the lesson order and printed page of the lessons are the same as the 2019 edition, with two small TOC-level exceptions found at citation check: in Part 1 the 2019 joint entry "39–40. Проверь себя" (p.140) is split into L39 Check yourself (p.140) and L40 Generalisation (p.142); in Part 4 the 4A tail shifts by one lesson — the revised L139 is "Numerical and letter expressions for solving problems" (p.36), the fraction-comparison lessons are L140–141 (pp.39, 43), generalisation is L142 (p.46) and the check is L143 (p.50), where the 2019 printing has the comparisons at L139–140, generalisation at L141, checks at L142 and L143. The revision is otherwise confined to: (i) Part 1 grows by 4 pages in the end matter (Glossary moves from p.142 to p.146; extra platform/usage pages); (ii) section headings in the TOCs gain the wording "Сквозная тема «…»"; (iii) the pupil letters and "how to use" spreads now point to the electronic version on the OPiQ.KZ / TOPiQ.KZ platform and the 2023 KZ letter likewise; (iv) new set ISBNs. No maths content differs materially — a learner with either edition meets the same course; the content entries G4-S1…S4 above therefore serve both editions (read with the two numbering exceptions just noted), and this entry records the edition difference itself. The Uyghur Part 1 (file 1257, imprint 2019) is already printed in the expanded 152-page layout with its glossary at p.146, i.e. the Uyghur printing follows the revised layout ahead of the RU 2020 imprint date.

ISBN 978-601-01-3828-5" — the same ISBN 978-601-01-3828-5 is printed in №2, №3 and №4 (shared-ISBN quirk), and the KZ numbers likewise share ISBN 978-601-01-3890-2. The KZ №3 verso (file 884) correctly says №3, 92 бет.

Structure and companion verdict: keyed lesson-by-lesson to the student's book of the same part number — **match confirmed** (№1 opens with "1. Повторение пройденного в 3 классе" and follows the Part 1 lesson titles in order). Each lesson offers two write-in tasks (Task 1: consolidate the lesson's skill, often on squared grids; Task 2: apply the maths in a life situation) and an "Я могу" checklist in which the pupil writes what they can now do (the front matter models it with an example: "Записывать задачу кратко / Решать по действиям / Составлять выражение по задаче"). KZ and RU numbers carry the same tasks in the two languages. No new theory is introduced. Defects: KZ №4 (file 885) has 87 PDF pages (84 printed + OPiQ advertising end-pages) and its colophon page was not captured in the scan — bibliographic data for KZ №4 rests on its cover/title page and the shared set ISBN.

## Kazakhstan Maths — Band C (Grades 5–6) — Reading Draft

Band: 24 files, 4,208 PDF pages. All files inspected at cover, title/imprint page and
contents page(s) from the printed book itself (rendered page images; the PDFs are
image-only scans carrying only an OKULYK watermark text layer). Recorded titles in
set-table.json were treated as untrusted; everything below is as printed.

### The author-spelling question (set by the task) — SETTLED

The record spells the Mektep author two ways: **Абылкасымова** and **Әбилқасымова /
Әбілқасымова**. The imprints prove these are **one and the same person and series**:

- Kazakh-language imprints print **Әбілқасымова А. Е.** (cover of 125/126/182/183:
  "А. Е. Әбілқасымова, Т. П. Кучер, З. Ә. Жұмағұлова").
- Russian-language imprints print **Абылкасымова А. Е.** for the same books.
- Every colophon, in every language, gives the identical full name:
  **Абылкасымова Алма Есимбековна / Әбілқасымова Алма Есимбековна**, with the same
  co-authors **Кучер Татьяна Павловна** and **Жұмағұлова Зауре Абылкеновна**,
  same publisher (Мектеп, Almaty), same years (Grade 5: 2017; Grade 6: 2018),
  and parallel ISBN blocks that differ only in the language-edition digits
  (e.g. G5 Pt 1: Kazakh ISBN 978-601-07-0846-4 vs Russian 978-601-07-0847-1).
- Әбілқасымова is simply the Kazakh Cyrillic spelling of the surname; Абылкасымова
  is its Russian Cyrillic spelling. Not different books, not different authors.

The same pattern holds for the Atamura series: Алдамуратова Т. А. with
Байшоланова Қ. С. and Байшоланов Е. С. in both languages.

### Clustering rule applied

(a) Same edition + language + part in several files = one holding, verified by
contents pages and interior pages, not covers alone.
(b) Kazakh / Russian / Uyghur / Uzbek editions of the same series + part = the same
work in parallel language editions: content filed ONCE per part, all language
editions listed, with interior spot-checks across languages (done for every part —
section numbers, worked examples and exercise numbering are identical across
languages; only the language of instruction and the ISBN/print-run differ).
(c) Different years/editions filed separately — in this band each grade has exactly
two competing series (Atamura vs Mektep), each in one edition only.
(d) The two "Абылкасымова Т." files (1557, 1558) are NOT textbooks at all — see
Work 5 below: they are the electronic teacher supplement to the Mektep Grade 6
book, in Russian and Kazakh. Filed as their own component with a companion verdict.

**Result: 24 files → 5 distinct works** (4 two-part student textbooks + 1 two-language
teacher supplement). No file in the band is a re-scan duplicate of another file:
every apparent "duplicate" in the record turned out to be a *different language
edition* with its own ISBN. Page-count differences are explained under each work.

### WORK 1 — Aldamuratova, Baysholanova, Baysholanov, *Mathematics*, Grade 5 (Atamura, 2017)

**As printed (Kazakh edition):** Т. А. Алдамуратова, Қ. С. Байшоланова, Е. С. Байшоланов,
*Математика*, textbook for Grade 5 of the general-education school, in 2 parts,
Almaty: Atamura, 2017. Prepared to the Model programme of updated content for
Grades 5–6 and approved by the Ministry of Education and Science of the Republic
of Kazakhstan. Full names from the colophons: Алдамуратова Тұрсынкұл
Алдамуратқызы, Байшоланова Қарлығаш Советқызы, Байшоланов Еркін Советұлы.
Reviewer named in the Kazakh imprint: Әбдімәжит Қоңырбайұлы Өтеш (director of
Almaty Physics–Mathematics Lyceum No. 178); the Russian imprint adds a second
reviewer, Симакина Галина Николаевна. Part 1: 224 pp, ISBN 978-601-306-735-3
(set 978-601-306-734-6), print run 50,000, signed to print 22.05.2017. Part 2:
192 pp, ISBN 978-601-306-736-0 (same set), run 50,000, signed to print
06.06.2017. Russian editions: Алдамуратова spelling of the first surname in
Russian Cyrillic is the same (Т. А. Алдамуратова); Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-306-725-4,
Pt 2 978-601-306-726-1, set 978-601-306-724-7, run 30,000 each. Held files: 123,
124 (KZ); 146, 147 (RU). In all four scans PDF page = printed page throughout
(title leaf = p. 1, imprint/symbols leaf = p. 2, first numbered text page = 3);
Part 1 closes with contents (pp. 222–223) and colophon (p. 224), Part 2 with
contents (pp. 188–190) and a colophon spread over pp. 191–192. Content is filed
once per part from the Kazakh edition; the Russian edition was spot-checked at
its cover, imprint, full contents and at §1.1, §2.8, §3.14, §4.1, §6.1 and the
year-revision block: section numbers, worked examples and exercise numbering
are identical at every check (e.g. §2.8 key facts НОД(18, 45) = 9 and
НОД(90, 252) = 18; §3.14 opens with the same 315-watch problem in both
languages; Pt 2 ex. 1363–1365 are the same tasks). **Pagination is NOT
identical across the languages:** in Part 1 the Russian setting runs ahead of
the Kazakh by 1 page at §1.1 (p. 4 vs p. 5) growing to 4–5 pages by Ch. III
(§3.13 at RU p. 203 vs KZ p. 207), because the Russian foreword takes one page
against the Kazakh two and the Russian type is slightly tighter; in Part 2 the
two settings track within a page (§3.15 RU p. 11 vs KZ p. 10; §6.2 RU p. 118 vs
KZ p. 117; §9.1 RU p. 173 vs KZ p. 174). The Russian editions also carry an
alphabetical index of terms (Pt 1 pp. 218–220; Pt 2 p. 187 ff.) that the Kazakh
editions do not have. All page numbers below are the Kazakh edition's.

**How the book teaches (both parts).** The two parts are one continuous book:
nine chapters (Ch. III is split across the part boundary) and one continuous
exercise numbering (Pt 1: ex. 1–665; Pt 2: ex. 666–1365). Each § is built the
same way: a short lead-in problem or "prepare yourself" task (marked with a
book icon), the theory in short numbered subsections with boxed rules stated in
words and in letter form, a worked "check yourself" (Өзіңді өзің тексер) strip
in which the expected pupil answer is printed and reasoned aloud, then
exercises in three tiers — A (first level, compulsory), B (second level),
C (third level, creative) — plus blue-numbered revision exercises, `*` harder
tasks, `°` ingenuity tasks, framed logic tasks and ▲-marked answers printed at
the end of the §. Every § closes with a "Тақырыптың түйіні" (topic summary)
box. Recurring extras: history boxes (number systems, the sieve of
Eratosthenes, Gauss's pairing method, aliquot fractions, the history of
percent and of the degree), "learn this!" additional-reading sections
(үреніп алыңдар) that are explicitly outside the assessed course, an
independent-work sheet on equations inside Ch. I, microcalculator key-sequence
instructions in the percent chapter, and a chapter-revision exercise block at
the end of every chapter. The decimal separator is a comma throughout.

#### Part 1 (printed pp. 3–224)

**Chapter 1 — Natural numbers and zero (pp. 5–70).**
- §1.1 Natural numbers and zero (p. 5): counting numbers; 0 is not natural;
  a multi-digit natural is the sum of its place values (35 749 worked both
  ways); place units 1, 10, 100, 10 000…; the class table up to trillions
  (15 places: units / thousands / millions / milliards / trillions); the
  decimal system is positional — the same digit 8 means units in 348, tens
  in 582, hundreds in 867.
- §1.2 The coordinate ray (p. 10): built step by step — ray, origin O (the
  counting start), unit segment, direction; the coordinate of a point,
  A(3); choosing the unit freely (a scale); opening pursuit problem (cat
  7 m/s after a dog at 10 m/s) solved on the ray; scales in the exercises
  (unit = 40 m, unit = 60 km on a three-ray map from Astana).
- §1.3 Comparing natural numbers; double inequality (p. 15): by position on
  the ray; by number of digits; digit-by-digit from the highest place;
  reading 9 < 10 and 15 > 7 aloud; a natural between two others written as
  a double inequality (18 < 25 < 40) and read from the middle number
  outwards; strict vs non-strict wording noted for exercises.
- History of number systems and of writing numbers (pp. 19–21, extra
  reading): finger counting, Egyptian signs, Roman numerals I, V, X, L, C,
  D, M with the additive/subtractive rules, old Slavic letter numerals,
  Babylonian base-60 (1 h = 60 min), Indian digits via the Arabs, Adelard,
  Magnitsky's *Arithmetika* (1703), 5-, 12-, 20- and 60-bases.
- §1.4 Adding and subtracting natural numbers (p. 21): column addition and
  subtraction by places; addition and subtraction as shifts right/left on
  the coordinate ray; the names of the components (a + b = c, a − b = c with
  a > b or a = b); subtraction as the inverse of addition.
- §1.5 Multiplying and dividing natural numbers; the basic property of the
  quotient (p. 26): multiplication as a sum of equal addends (3 · 7 on the
  ray); a product with a zero factor is 0 (used at once for equations of
  the form 16(x − 3) = 0); division defined through multiplication;
  multiplying or dividing both dividend and divisor by the same natural
  leaves the quotient unchanged (60 : 15 = 120 : 30 = 20 : 5); **division
  by zero is impossible** (boxed); checking by multiplication/division;
  written multi-digit multiplication and long division, with a footnote
  that foreign textbooks set the division out differently.
- History of the operation signs, = and inequality signs (pp. 30–31, extra
  reading): Widman 1489 (+, −), Oughtred 1631 (×), Leibniz (· and ÷),
  Recorde 1557 (=), Harriot (>, <).
- §1.6 Properties of the arithmetic operations (p. 31): commutative and
  associative laws of addition shown on the ray and in letter form;
  a table of the multiplication laws — commutative, associative,
  distributive over addition and over subtraction ((a − b) · c = ac − bc,
  a ≥ b) — each used for convenient calculation (e.g. (100 + 4) · 12).
- "Learn this!" — the Gauss pairing method (pp. 36–38, extra reading):
  1 + 2 + … + 100 = 5050 by pairing to 101, then test-style tasks.
- §1.7 Order of operations on natural numbers (p. 39): first-level
  operations (+, −) vs second-level (·, :); left to right within a level;
  second level before first; brackets first, keeping the same order inside
  them; worked chains with the steps numbered above the line.
- §1.8 Numerical and letter expressions (p. 41): a numerical expression and
  its value; a letter expression; the value of a letter expression for
  given letter values; the coefficient (in 8x the coefficient is 8; a
  coefficient 1 is not written; the × sign is dropped before a letter and
  before a bracket); a letter is a variable; an expression built from a
  story is a mathematical model of it; reading expressions aloud (ex. 80).
- §1.9 Simplifying expressions (p. 46): simplify = same value, fewer
  operations; regrouping by the commutative/associative laws; the
  distributive law forwards (expanding) and backwards — taking a common
  factor out of brackets (7x + 8x = (7 + 8) · x = 15x); like addends
  combined; the coefficient of a simplified product written first, letters
  in alphabetical order (3n · 4k · 6m = 72kmn).
- Independent work No. 1 (p. 51): three balance-picture tasks to be turned
  into equations and solved (melon + 2 kg against 10 kg, etc.).
- §1.10 Equations (p. 51): an equality containing a letter-marked unknown
  is an equation; its root; solving by the unknown-component rules
  (unknown addend / minuend / subtrahend / factor / dividend / divisor),
  each worked example ending with a check by substitution; word problems
  solved in the fixed steps — choose the unknown (one quantity in one
  unit), write the model, solve, answer, check — with table and segment
  (bar) diagram models (ages problem; 37 berry bushes split by two
  conditions).
- §1.11 Formulas; calculating by formula (p. 58): a rule written with
  letters is a formula. Rectangle perimeter P = 2(a + b), square P = 4a;
  rectangle area S = ab, square S = a²; distance s = v · t with
  v = s : t and t = s : v; cost C = a · n; cuboid volume V = abc, cube
  V = a³; all quantities in the same unit before substituting; a closing
  formula table. (Triangle area is **not** stated here — it appears only
  inside exercises 126/135*, by completing the triangle to a rectangle.)
- §1.12 Number sequences (p. 64): a sequence as an ordered list; its terms
  a₁, a₂, a₃, … (aₙ); constant sequences; finding terms from an nth-term
  formula (xₙ = (n + 3) · 2; cₙ = n² + 1) and guessing the formula from the
  terms (1, 4, 9, 16, 25… → n²); recursive descriptions in the exercises.
- Chapter 1 revision exercises (pp. 67–70, ex. 151–166).

**Chapter 2 — Divisibility of natural numbers (pp. 71–126).**
- §2.1 Divisors and multiples of a natural number (p. 71): a divisor
  divides without remainder; 1 divides every natural; the largest divisor
  of a number is the number itself; divisors found in pairs whose product
  is the number (24: 1·24, 2·12, 3·8, 4·6), written as a set
  B(15) = {1, 3, 5, 15}; a multiple is divisible by the number; the
  smallest multiple is the number itself; multiples are infinitely many,
  E(14) = {14, 28, 42, …}.
- Extra reading (p. 77): finding a natural root of an equation by trying
  divisor pairs (x(x + 6) = 40 from the divisor pairs of 40).
- §2.2 Prime and composite numbers (p. 78): naturals split by divisor
  count into primes (exactly two divisors: 1 and itself), composites
  (more than two) and the number 1 (one divisor — neither); there are
  infinitely many primes; twin primes in a side box; the table of primes
  under 1 000 is printed on the book's flyleaf (see Defects).
- The sieve of Eratosthenes (pp. 81–83, extra reading): crossing-out
  construction to 40, Euclid on the infinity of primes, Chebyshev and
  Vinogradov named.
- §2.3 Basic properties of divisibility (p. 83): if one factor is
  divisible by d, so is the product; if each addend is divisible by d, so
  is the sum; the same for a difference — each shown by writing the
  numbers as d · (factor) and used to decide divisibility without
  dividing.
- §2.4 Tests for divisibility by 2, 5 and 10 (p. 87): even digits
  0, 2, 4, 6, 8 and odd digits; a number ending in an even digit is even
  and divisible by 2; ending in 0 or 5 → divisible by 5; ending in 0 →
  divisible by 10; every number divisible by 10 is divisible by both 2
  and 5.
- §2.5 Tests for divisibility by 3 and 9 (p. 92): discovered from a
  digit-sum table, then *derived* from the expanded form
  (100a + 10b + c = 99a + 9b + (a + b + c)): digit sum divisible by 3 (by
  9) ⇔ the number is; combined tests noted alongside (divisible by 2 and
  3 → by 6; by 3 and 5 → by 15; by 5 and 9 → by 45). No tests for 4, 25
  or 11 are given.
- §2.6 Powers of a number (p. 96): aⁿ as a product of n equal factors
  (n > 1); base, exponent, value of the power; a² is the number's square,
  a³ its cube; a¹ = a; powers of 10 and writing a number with them
  (2375 = 2 · 10³ + 3 · 10² + 7 · 10 + 5); raising to a power is a
  third-level operation and is done before the others in a bracket-free
  expression.
- §2.7 Writing composite numbers as a product of prime factors (p. 102):
  two methods — splitting a product step by step (84 = 4 · 21 =
  2 · 2 · 3 · 7) and the vertical-bar ladder (420 → 2 · 2 · 3 · 5 · 7),
  always taking the smallest prime divisor; the factorisation is unique
  apart from order; repeated primes collected with powers
  (36 = 2² · 3², 100 = 2² · 5²); reading a number's divisors off its
  factorisation in the exercises.
- §2.8 The greatest common divisor; coprime numbers (p. 107): GCD
  (ЕҮОБ) by listing divisors, then by prime factorisation — the common
  prime factors each to the *smaller* exponent; if the smaller number
  divides the larger it is itself the GCD; numbers whose only common
  divisor is 1 are coprime (өзара жай), e.g. 25 and 12.
- §2.9 The least common multiple (p. 114): LCM (ЕКОЕ) by listing
  multiples, then by factorisation — the larger number's factorisation
  topped up with the missing primes of the other (all primes to the
  *larger* exponent); for coprime numbers LCM = their product; if one
  number divides the other, LCM = the larger; ex. 310's table (a × b,
  GCD, LCM side by side) leads pupils to complete GCD(a, b) · LCM(a, b) =
  a · b as a fill-in equality — it is not boxed as a theorem.
- Chapter 2 revision exercises (pp. 119–121, ex. 316–327).
- §2.10 Motion on a river (pp. 121–126, extra reading, marked "for
  additional study"): downstream speed = own speed + current speed,
  upstream = own − current; recovering the two speeds from the pair:
  current = (downstream − upstream) : 2, own = (downstream + upstream)
  : 2; a full exercise set (ex. 328–348) follows.

**Chapter 3 — Common fractions and operations on them (pp. 127–221).**
- §3.1 The common fraction; reading and writing fractions (p. 127): a
  share (үлес) of a whole; a/b — a (the numerator) says how many shares
  are taken, b (the denominator) into how many equal shares the whole is
  split; a may be 0 or any natural, b any natural; reading ("three
  eighths"); the fraction bar is the division sign — the quotient of two
  naturals is written as a fraction (12 : 4 = 12/4), and any natural as a
  fraction with any denominator (3 = 3/1 = 12/4). Fractions of quantities
  are worked from the first exercises (1/5 of the competitors, 120 trees'
  1/5), ahead of the systematic §3.14 in Part 2.
- §3.2 The basic property of a fraction; reducing fractions (p. 135):
  multiplying or dividing numerator and denominator by the same natural
  gives an equal fraction (seen on a re-divided circle, 3/4 = 6/8);
  reducing = dividing both by a common divisor ≠ 1; a fraction whose
  numerator and denominator are coprime cannot be reduced. Three taught
  methods, with the verdict that dividing by the GCD at once is the most
  efficient: (I) by the GCD (42/63 = 2/3), (II) step by step using the
  divisibility tests (180/315 → /5 → /9), (III) by prime factorisation,
  cancelling common factors (60/225 = 4/15); cancelling is also practised
  inside fractional expressions whose numerator/denominator are sums,
  differences and products (ex. 388–394).
- §3.3 Proper and improper fractions (p. 142): proper (numerator <
  denominator) is less than 1; improper (numerator ≥ denominator) is
  equal to or greater than 1; a/a = 1; a natural written as an improper
  fraction with a chosen denominator (4 = 32/8 = 60/15).
- §3.4 Mixed numbers (p. 147): from sharing 7 apples among 3 children —
  2⅓, a whole part plus a fractional part, read "two whole one third";
  improper → mixed by division with remainder (23/6 = 3⅚); mixed →
  improper by whole × denominator + numerator (5⅜ = 43/8); a natural as
  a mixed number (9 = 8 7/7 = 8 5/5).
- §3.5 Fractions and mixed numbers on the coordinate ray (p. 153): the
  plotting algorithm — divide the unit segment into b equal parts, count
  off a of them; A(5/9), C(2¾).
- §3.6 Reducing fractions and mixed numbers to a common denominator
  (p. 160): the additional factor (толықтауыш көбейткіш), written as a
  small superscript at the numerator; the least common denominator is
  the LCM of the denominators (1/4 and 2/3 → 3/12 and 8/12); the
  three-step algorithm is tabulated; mixed numbers are converted through
  their fractional parts only.
- §3.7 Comparing fractions and mixed numbers (p. 167): same denominators
  — the larger numerator wins; same numerators — the *smaller*
  denominator gives the larger fraction; different both — reduce to the
  LCD, **or** cross-multiply (a/b vs c/d by ad vs bc, stated in full);
  the larger fraction lies further right on the ray; mixed numbers by
  whole part first, then fractional part.
- §3.8 Adding and subtracting common fractions (p. 175): like
  denominators from pictures, a/c + b/c = (a + b)/c and
  a/c − b/c = (a − b)/c (a ≥ b); unlike denominators via the LCD;
  subtracting a fraction from a natural (3 − 2/5 two ways, through
  15/5 or through 2⅘); the commutative and associative laws hold for
  fraction addition.
- §3.9 Adding and subtracting mixed numbers (p. 183): wholes and
  fractional parts separately (unlike denominators: LCD first); carrying
  when the fractional sum is improper (2⅑ + 5⁴⁄₉ = 7⁵⁄₉); borrowing one
  whole when the fractional part is too small; a natural minus a mixed
  number by writing the natural as a mixed number (9 − 6³⁄₁₀ = 2⁷⁄₁₀).
- §3.10 Aliquot (unit) fractions (pp. 190–193, extra reading): an
  aliquot fraction has numerator 1; the ancient Egyptians used only
  these ("real fractions"); the largest is 1/2; writing a fraction as a
  sum of aliquot fractions with different denominators
  (2/7 = 1/6 + 1/14 + 1/21; the Ahmes papyrus table entry
  2/13 = 1/8 + 1/52 + 1/104).
- History of fractions (pp. 194–195, extra reading): unit, systematic
  (Babylonian base-60: 1 min = 1/60 h) and general fractions; Egyptian
  hieroglyphs; Indian writing without the bar; al-Khwarizmi, al-Hassar
  (the bar, XII c.), Leonardo Pisano, Planudes ("numerator/denominator"),
  Archimedes.
- §3.11 Multiplying fractions and mixed numbers (p. 195): fraction ×
  natural as repeated addition, (a/b) · n = a·n/b; fraction × fraction
  from the area of a 7/8 dm by 3/5 dm rectangle on a grid,
  (a/b)·(c/d) = ac/bd; **cancel before multiplying**; powers of fractions
  ((5/7)² = 25/49); mixed numbers multiplied through improper fractions;
  a fraction as a coefficient (⁵⁄₈x); a zero factor gives 0.
- §3.12 Reciprocal numbers (p. 204): two numbers whose product is 1;
  the reciprocal of a/b is b/a — swap numerator and denominator; of a
  mixed number, after writing it improper; of a natural n, the fraction
  1/n; 1 is its own reciprocal; equations (a/b)x = 1 in the exercises,
  and ex. 604's true/false set states that zero has no reciprocal.
- §3.13 Dividing fractions and mixed numbers (p. 207): the rule is
  *derived* — from the rectangle of area 9/25 dm² and side 2/5 dm,
  x = (9/25) : (2/5) is solved by multiplying both sides of
  (2/5)x = 9/25 by the reciprocal 5/2; rule: a/b : c/d = a/b · d/c;
  mixed ÷ mixed through improper fractions; natural ÷ fraction
  n : a/b = nb/a and fraction ÷ natural a/b : n = a/(bn), the first also
  shown on the ray (4 : 2/3 = 6); a number cannot be divided by zero.
- Applying arithmetic operations to fractions and mixed numbers
  (pp. 216–220, ex. 637–665): the order of operations is the same as for
  naturals; long mixed expressions, complex (multi-storey) fractions in
  the C tier (ex. 627, 659). The part ends (p. 221) with two unnumbered
  lead-in problems — finding a fraction of a number (72 · 3/8 = 27
  pages) and a number from its fraction (27 : 3/8 = 72 pages) — which
  Part 2 §3.14 then takes up systematically.

**Honest negatives, Pt 1:** no decimal fractions and no percent (both are
Part 2); no negative numbers; no ratio or proportion; geometry is only the
formulas inside §1.11 (perimeter, rectangle/square area, cuboid/cube volume)
plus the ray — no angles, no circle; no statistics or diagrams; no mean;
triangle area is never stated as a rule (used only inside starred exercises);
no divisibility tests for 4, 25, 6 or 11 as stated rules (6/15/45 appear only
as combined-test remarks inside §2.5).

**Defects noticed (Pt 1, Kazakh scan):** the scan is clean and fully legible —
the decorative-font mojibake of the Mektep scans does **not** occur here. Every
page carries the OKULYK.KZ site header and the ministerial-order footer (Order
No. 217 of 17 May 2019) — scan overlays, not book content. The PDFs open
directly on the title page: **no cover or flyleaf pages are held**, so the
table of primes under 1 000 that §2.2 (p. 78) tells pupils to use ("printed on
the book's flyleaf") is not in the holding. No missing or duplicated body
pages. Inside the book itself: the closing guidance page (p. 221) teaches the
two fraction-of-a-number problem types only *after* the final exercise block
whose C-tier tasks already use them, and §3.1's exercises likewise use
fraction-of-a-quantity problems six sections before the method is stated
(the systematic treatment is §3.14, in Part 2).

#### Part 2 (printed pp. 3–190; contents pp. 188–190, colophon pp. 191–192)

**Chapter 3 (continued) — Common fractions.**
- §3.14 Finding a fraction of a number; finding a number from its
  fraction (p. 3): fraction of a number — first as (315 : 7) · 3 on a
  segment diagram, then as the rule "multiply the number by the
  fraction" (315 · 3/7 = 135; also for a mixed multiplier,
  5⅓ · 1⅛ = 6); number from its fraction — "divide the number by the
  fraction" (70 : 2/5 = 175), each type also solved by an equation
  ((5/7)x = 15 → x = 21).
- §3.15 Problems on joint work (p. 10): the whole job — or the whole
  distance, for meeting motion — is taken as 1; each worker's rate is
  1/(his time); rates add; together-time = 1/(combined rate) (two pumps
  fill a cistern in 15 min; a boat downstream on the "whole distance = 1"
  model in 4 h). Exercises extend to three workers, a third pipe
  emptying the pool (ex. 700), and the classic eating-together problems
  from Magnitsky (ex. 703*, 713).

**Chapter 4 — Decimal fractions and operations on them (pp. 16–91).**
- §4.1 The decimal fraction; reading and writing decimals (p. 16):
  fractions with denominators 10, 100, 1000… written without the
  denominator (4 17/100 = 4,17); the comma separates the whole part from
  the fractional part; new places to the right of the units — tenths,
  hundredths, thousandths, ten-thousandths — each 10 times smaller than
  the previous (1/10 = 0,1 etc.); reading: whole part, the word "whole"
  (бүтін), then the fractional part with its last place named
  (35,418 = "35 whole 418 thousandths"); the book notes that Kazakhstan
  uses the comma while e.g. England and the USA use a point; writing
  rule — as many digits after the comma as zeros in the denominator,
  padding with zeros on the left (4 9/1000 = 4,009); expanded form
  (2,817 = 2 + 0,8 + 0,01 + 0,007); trailing zeros may be appended or
  dropped (0,7 = 0,70 = 0,700; 0,300 = 0,3); a natural as a decimal
  (7 = 7,0); quantities written as decimals (5 m 8 dm = 5,8 m;
  14 kg 5 g = 14,005 kg).
- §4.2 Converting a decimal to a common fraction and a common fraction
  to a decimal (p. 24): decimal → common in three steps (whole part,
  digits after the comma over 1 with as many zeros, reduce;
  0,401 = 401/1000, 5,07 = 5 7/100). Common → decimal **only when
  possible**: an irreducible fraction converts exactly when its
  denominator's prime factorisation contains only 2s and 5s
  (10 = 2 · 5, 100 = 2² · 5², 1000 = 2³ · 5³) — then bring the
  denominator to 10/100/1000 by the basic property; 2/15 and 5/6 are
  shown *not* convertible, and the book says such work stays in common
  fractions. Memorised conversions boxed: 1/2 = 0,5; 1/4 = 0,25;
  1/5 = 0,2; 3/4 = 0,75; 1/10 = 0,1; 1/20 = 0,05; 1/25 = 0,04.
  **No long-division conversion and no periodic decimals are taught
  here** (a numerator ÷ denominator conversion appears only inside
  §4.7's exercises for friendly denominators: 1/2 = 0,5, 3/4 = 0,75,
  3/5 = 0,6).
- §4.3 Decimals on the coordinate ray; comparing decimals (p. 30):
  plotting 2,57 by successive tenths/hundredths refinement of the unit
  segment; equal decimals are the same point (0,4 = 0,40); comparing by
  ray position, then place by place from the highest place (whole parts
  first, then tenths, hundredths…), padding to equal length with zeros.
- History of decimals (p. 36, extra reading): al-Kashi's *Key of
  Arithmetic* (1427) and the vertical-bar separator, Stevin (1585),
  Napier and Kepler (the comma), Magnitsky (1703), the point in the USA
  (1,5 written 1.5 there).
- §4.4 Adding and subtracting decimals (p. 37): first through common
  fractions (3,46 + 5,271 = 3460/1000 + 5271/1000), then the column rule
  — equalise the number of decimal places, write comma under comma, add
  or subtract place by place from the smallest place; the laws of
  addition/subtraction carry over, each named and exemplified; with
  practice the padding zeros may be kept in mind only.
- §4.5 Multiplying a decimal by a natural number (p. 45): first through
  a common fraction (4,23 · 5 = 423/100 · 5 = 21,15), then the rule —
  multiply ignoring the comma, then separate as many decimal places as
  the decimal factor has; padding on the left when the product has too
  few digits (0,009 · 8 = 0,072); the multiplication laws hold.
- §4.6 Multiplying decimals (p. 51): from the area 6,15 cm × 4,3 cm
  through common fractions; rule — ignore both commas, separate
  (places of the first + places of the second) digits from the right,
  padding with zeros if needed (0,134 · 0,02 = 0,00268); decimal squares
  and cubes in the exercise tables (a² for a = 0,1…0,4); "a decimal part
  of a quantity" problems (0,5 of 7 kg; price up/down by 0,25 / 0,08 of
  itself, ex. 858) prepare percent without naming it.
- §4.7 Dividing a decimal by a natural number (p. 57): long division as
  for naturals; the comma goes into the quotient as soon as the whole
  part is exhausted; a remainder is continued by appending zeros
  (41,56 : 8 = 5,195; 2,34 : 5 = 0,468 — whole part of the quotient 0
  when the dividend's whole part is smaller); every quotient is checked
  by multiplication; if the division runs on, converting the decimal to
  a common fraction first is advised (0,7 : 3 = 7/30).
- §4.8 Dividing a decimal by a decimal (p. 65): by the basic property of
  the quotient — shift the comma in both numbers right by the divisor's
  decimal places (5,52 : 1,2 = 55,2 : 12 = 4,6), then divide by a
  natural; if the dividend has fewer places, append zeros; side
  observations: dividing by a decimal > 1 shrinks, by a decimal < 1
  grows; a "learn this!" box (p. 69) teaches cancelling a fraction whose
  numerator and denominator are products of decimals by first scaling
  both to naturals ((8,7 · 5,1 · 0,75)/(1,7 · 1,5 · 2,9) = 9/2).
- §4.9 Multiplying and dividing a decimal by 10, 100, 1000… and by 0,1;
  0,01; 0,001… (p. 72): comma-shift rules in all four directions (×10…
  right, ÷10… left, ×0,1… left, ÷0,1… right), padding with zeros where
  the digits run out (3,4 · 1000 = 3400); the equivalence is made
  explicit: multiplying by 0,1; 0,01… and dividing by 10; 100… give the
  same result, and vice versa.
- §4.10 Operations with decimal and common fractions together (p. 78) —
  in form the Chapter 4 revision block (ex. 956–1004): convert to one
  kind and compute; mixed word problems throughout.
- §4.11 Rounding decimals (p. 82): a rounded value is an approximate
  value, written with ≈ and read "approximately equal"; rounding by
  deficiency and by excess pictured on the ray (34,18 lies between 34,1
  and 34,2, nearer 34,2); the rule — look at the first dropped digit:
  0, 1, 2, 3 or 4 → the kept part is unchanged (round down); 5, 6, 7, 8
  or 9 → the last kept digit grows by 1 (round up); applied to naturals
  (to tens…thousands, in the exercises) and to decimals (to tenths,
  hundredths, thousandths); a trailing zero that marks the rounding
  place is kept (15,921 ≈ 16,0); ex. 999 has pupils round common
  fractions (2/3, 6/7, 2/13, 5/19) to thousandths — the one place a
  non-terminating decimal is met, handled by dividing and rounding
  without any period notation.
- History of calculating tools (p. 89, extra reading): finger counting,
  the abacus, the Russian counting frame, Leibniz's arithmometer
  (1673), Chebyshev's improved arithmometer (1878), the microcalculator.
- §4.12 Sequences made of fractions (p. 90): exercises only — fraction
  and decimal sequences continued, completed and given by nth-term
  formulas (aₙ = 1/n² worked as a table).

**Chapter 5 — Sets (pp. 92–110).**
- §5.1 A set; elements of a set; representing sets (p. 92): Cantor
  (1845–1918) named as the founder of set theory; a set is a collection
  of objects sharing a stated property; the objects are its elements;
  sets are named with capital Latin letters and written in braces, each
  element once; the set of naturals **N**; membership ∈ / ∉ with their
  readings (7 ∈ N; 0 ∉ N); a set with no elements is empty, ∅; sets are
  finite or infinite by their element count, written n(A) (n(A) = 10 for
  the digit set); two ways of giving a set — listing the elements, or a
  characteristic property (B = {x | x ∈ N, x < 70}); Euler–Venn circles
  with member points inside and non-members outside (Euler, Venn
  pictured).
- §5.2 Subsets (p. 97): B ⊂ A when every element of B is in A (read
  three ways); ⊄; equal sets (X ⊂ Y and Y ⊂ X ⇔ X = Y); the empty set
  and the set itself are subsets of every set (∅ ⊂ F, F ⊂ F); all
  subsets of {a, b, c} are listed (8 of them), and the text states that
  an n-element set has 2ⁿ subsets counting itself and ∅. **No quantifier
  vocabulary** (the Mektep book's "all / there is" box has no counterpart
  here).
- §5.3 Intersection and union of sets (p. 102): ∩ — the set of elements
  belonging to *both*, A ∩ B = {x | x ∈ A and x ∈ B}; disjoint sets have
  A ∩ B = ∅; ∪ — elements belonging to *at least one*,
  A ∪ B = {x | x ∈ A or x ∈ B}; the counting formula is worked inside
  the self-check: n(A ∪ B) = n(A) + n(B) − n(A ∩ B) (6 + 6 − 3 = 9);
  special cases: disjoint → counts add; B ⊂ A → A ∩ B = B, A ∪ B = A.
  Commutativity/associativity of ∪ and ∩ are **not** stated.
- §5.4 Problems on sets (p. 107): two-set counting by Euler–Venn diagram
  with the formula applied numerically (olympiad: 38 + 25 − 20 = 43
  pupils); the exercise set (ex. 1070–1084) includes three-circle
  diagrams to read and complete (ex. 1067, 1084) but no three-set
  formula.
- Chapter 5 revision exercises (p. 109, ex. 1078–1084).

**Chapter 6 — Percent (pp. 111–132).**
- §6.1 Percent (p. 111): one percent is one hundredth of a whole
  (1% = 1/100 = 0,01; 1 cm is 1% of a metre); the whole quantity is
  100%; a 100-square grid picture (9 shaded squares = 9%); percent → a
  common or decimal fraction: drop the % sign and divide by 100
  (70% = 0,7; 300% = 3), with a table of landmarks (100% = 1,
  25% = 1/4 = 0,25, 50% = 1/2, 75% = 3/4, 20% = 1/5, 10% = 0,1,
  15% = 15/100 = 3/20); fraction or number → percent: multiply by 100
  and attach % ((a/b) · 100%; 0,4 = 40%; 2 = 200%); finding 1% of a
  quantity (÷ 100) and the quantity from its 1% (× 100) (ex. 1089);
  the third problem type — what percent one number is of another — is
  **not given its own §**: it is covered here through the
  fraction → percent conversion (radius is 50% of the diameter; a
  square's side is 25% of its perimeter, ex. 1096) and in §6.1's
  exercises generally.
- §6.2 Finding the percent of a number (p. 117): two methods side by
  side — (i) through 1% ((4 : 100) · 85 = 3,4 kg of water in 4 kg of
  apples), and (ii) by the "main percent formula" b = a · P/100:
  express the percent as a common or decimal fraction (whichever is
  more convenient: 20% = 1/5 or 0,2) and multiply; numbers more than
  100% and less than the whole alike (90's 130% = 117; 56's 85% = 47,6);
  microcalculator % key sequences are printed for all three shapes
  (45 × 20%, 90 + 30%, 56 − 15%); price rise/fall problems; a boxed
  additional task (ex. 1135) states the **simple interest formula**
  Sₙ = S(1 + Pn/100) — deposit S at P% a year for n years, interest
  always on the original sum (no compounding) — used in ex. 1136
  (4 800 000 tg at 7% for 2 years) and the revision set.
- History of percent (pp. 123–124, extra reading): percent in V-century
  India and in Babylonian base-60 reckoning; *pro centum* → "cto" → the
  % sign from a misread abbreviation in a 1685 Paris commercial
  arithmetic; Stevin's percent table (1584).
- §6.3 Finding a number from its percent (p. 124): through 1%
  (480 pupils are 60% → 480 : 60 · 100 = 800) and by the formula
  a = b : P/100 — express the percent as a fraction and divide;
  microcalculator sequence (175 ÷ 25%); standard camouflaged problems
  (price after a 10% cut is 72 900 tg — find the price before; 6% loss
  on resale, ex. 1148).
- Chapter 6 revision exercises — "Problems on percent" (pp. 130–132,
  ex. 1171–1184): the three types mixed, successive percent changes
  (+5% then −20%; −14% then +8%), a deposit split between 5% and 4%
  (ex. 1184).

**Chapter 7 — Angles. Polygons (pp. 133–156).**
- §7.1 The angle; the degree measure of an angle (p. 133): an angle is
  two rays from one point; vertex, sides; notation ∠AOB (vertex letter
  in the middle) or one letter ∠E; supplementary rays form a straight
  angle; the degree is 1/180 of a straight angle, so a straight angle
  measures 180°; the bisector (a ray splitting the angle into two equal
  angles, with the Latin etymology); angles at a point add
  (∠ABC = ∠ABD + ∠DBC = 55° + 30° = 85°).
- Short note on the degree (p. 138, extra reading): Babylonian origin;
  1° = 60′, 1′ = 60″; reading 35°20′15″; converting both ways
  (12,30° = 12,5°; 18°15′ = 18,25°).
- §7.2 The protractor; measuring and constructing angles (p. 139): the
  protractor — a semicircle with a 0–180° scale, each division 1°;
  **measuring** (centre on the vertex, one side on the 0 mark, read the
  other side: ∠AOB = 60°); **constructing** a given angle (∠ABC = 120°
  built step by step); exercises ask pupils to *discover* the angle
  sums by measuring — the three angles of a drawn triangle (ex. 1204)
  and of a quadrilateral (ex. 1210, ∠A + ∠B + ∠C + ∠D) — and to find
  which of the four angles at two intersecting lines are equal
  (ex. 1213); neither sum is stated as a theorem.
- §7.3 Comparing angles; kinds of angles; the drawing triangle
  (set square) (p. 143): equal angles by superposition and by equal
  degree measures (marked with equal arcs); the larger measure is the
  larger angle; kinds — right angle = half a straight angle = 90°
  (with the square corner mark, and how to draw one with the set
  square), acute < 90°, obtuse between 90° and 180°, straight 180°,
  full angle 360° (two straight angles around a point, shown on a
  disc divided by a diameter).
- §7.4 Polygons (p. 150): a polygon is a closed broken line whose
  non-adjacent sides do not meet — the self-crossing four-link figure
  KLNP is shown as **not** a polygon; vertices, adjacent vertices,
  sides and angles (as many of each); a polygon lying on one side of
  the line of each of its sides is convex, and only convex polygons are
  studied; equal polygons by superposition; **perimeter** = the sum of
  all sides; a **diagonal** joins two non-adjacent vertices; polygons
  are named by side count (triangle, quadrilateral, pentagon…); a
  triangle has no diagonals; counting diagonals is set as drawing
  exercises (quadrilateral 2, pentagon 5 — ex. 1236), with no n(n − 3)/2
  formula.
- Chapter 7 revision exercises (pp. 155–156, ex. 1248–1254), including
  the quadrilateral angle sum used numerically (A = 50°, B = 120°,
  C = 80° → D measured, sum asked, ex. 1252).

**Chapter 8 — Diagrams (pp. 157–173).**
- §8.1 Circle and disc (p. 157): drawing with compasses; the circle is
  the figure of all points of the plane at one given distance from the
  centre O; radius R (centre to any point of the circle), diameter
  D = 2R (through the centre); the disc is the circle together with
  its interior; equal circles (discs) have equal radii; an **arc** of
  the circle, written ∪AB by its two endpoints (three letters when the
  arc is more than a semicircle); points on / inside / outside the
  disc in the exercises. **No circumference and no area of a disc
  anywhere in the book.**
- History note (p. 162, extra reading): Greek *diametros*, Latin
  *radius*; the round yurt, its shanyrak as a circle in Kazakh building.
- §8.2 The sector of a disc (p. 162): a sector is the part of a disc
  bounded by two radii and an arc; the angles of the sectors making up
  a disc sum to the full 360° (55° + 80° + 225°); finding the unknown
  sector (360° − 110° = 250°).
- §8.3 Ways of presenting statistical data; bar, line, pie and graphic
  diagrams; tables (p. 165): a diagram shows values geometrically.
  (I) **Bar (column) diagram** — equal-width columns on one line at
  equal gaps, built with a chosen scale (rivers: 1 cm = 1000 km;
  Ertis 4200, Esil 2500, Zhaiyk 2400, Syrdaria 2200 km) and read back.
  (II) **Line diagram** — the same values as the lengths of segments
  from one baseline (peak heights, 1500 m = 1 cm). (III) **Pie
  diagram** — the whole (100%) is the full 360°, so 1% corresponds to
  3,6°; sector angles computed from the percents (an alloy: tin 15% →
  54°, copper 60% → 216°, remainder 90°). (IV) **Graphic diagram** — a
  quantity's change as a broken line through plotted points
  (Astana temperature over a day, 15 July 1916: highest 30° at 15 h,
  lowest 16° at 2 h). (V) **Tables** — data in rows with a stated step
  (temperature every 2 hours). Conversion tasks between table, bar, pie
  and graphic forms fill the exercise set (ex. 1291–1301).

**Chapter 9 — Nets of spatial figures (pp. 174–179).**
- §9.1 The rectangular parallelepiped (cuboid) and its net (p. 174):
  6 faces, all rectangles, opposite faces equal; 12 edges, 8 vertices;
  the three edges at one vertex are its length, width and height (its
  measurements); cutting along edges and laying the faces flat gives
  the **net** — a plane figure of the faces in a definite order; the
  cube is the cuboid with all measurements equal (6 square faces); the
  book states a cube has 11 different nets; surface area and volume are
  used from Ch. 1 in the exercises (a cube face of perimeter 32 cm →
  surface 384 cm², volume 512 cm³, ex. 1305) but no surface-area formula
  is stated in this chapter.
- §9.2 Problems on cutting figures; problems on assembling figures
  (p. 178): practical net work only — which edges to cut to obtain a
  given net, which faces meet / are opposite when a marked net is
  folded, building a model from a printed net with glue flaps,
  surface area read off a net on squared paper (ex. 1313–1316).
  (Unlike the Mektep book there is no equal-composed / equal-area
  theory here.)

**Year revision exercises (pp. 180–187, ex. 1317–1365)**, in eight groups:
1. Natural numbers and zero; 2. Divisibility of naturals; 3. Common fractions
and operations on them; 4. Decimal fractions and operations on them;
5. Sets; 6. Percent; 7. Angles and polygons; 8. Pie diagrams and the net of a
cube — then contents (pp. 188–190) and colophon.

**Honest negatives, Pt 2 / whole G5 Atamura:** no negative numbers; no
ratio and proportion as such (percent is the only ratio language, and even
the percent-ratio type has no § of its own); no periodic decimals and no
general common-fraction → decimal conversion by division; no circumference
or disc area (the circle chapter stops at radius/diameter/arc/sector
angles); no triangle classification by sides or angles and no stated
triangle/quadrilateral angle-sum theorems (both are left as measuring
discoveries in exercises); no mean, median or any other statistic beyond
presenting data; no probability; no coordinates beyond the one-dimensional
ray; no compound interest (the bank formula given is simple interest);
quantifier words and the ∪/∩ algebra laws, which the parallel Mektep book
teaches, are absent.

**Defects noticed (Pt 2 and the Russian scans):** the Kazakh Pt 2 scan is
complete and clean, with the same OKULYK overlays as Pt 1 and likewise no
covers/flyleaves. The Kazakh Pt 2 colophon is split over two PDF pages
(pp. 191–192) where the Russian Pt 2 colophon fits on one page (p. 192) —
a layout difference, nothing missing. In the book itself: §8.3's "line
diagram" (segments standing on a baseline for peak heights) is in substance
a second bar-type display, so the bar/line/graphic terminology overlaps;
§9.1 asserts the 11 nets of a cube without showing them; and the percent
chapter's third basic type (what percent one number is of another) is
never gathered into a rule box — a pupil meets it only scattered through
§6.1's exercises and the revision set. The Russian spot-check found no
content discrepancies: identical § structure, worked examples and exercise
numbers in both parts; the differences are the pagination drift recorded
in the headnote, the Russian-only alphabetical index, the extra Russian
reviewer, and the smaller Russian print runs (30,000 against 50,000).

---

### WORK 2 — Abylkasymova, Kucher, Zhumagulova, *Mathematics*, Grade 5 (Mektep, 2017)

**As printed (Kazakh edition):** А. Е. Әбілқасымова, Т. П. Кучер, З. Ә. Жұмағұлова,
*Математика*, textbook for Grade 5 of the general-education school, in 2 parts,
Almaty: Mektep, 2017. Approved by the Ministry of Education and Science of the
Republic of Kazakhstan. Part 1: 144 pp, ISBN 978-601-07-0846-4 (set 978-601-07-0860-0),
print run 100,000. Part 2: 128 pp, ISBN 978-601-07-0875-4 (same set), run 100,000.
Russian editions: Абылкасымова spelling; Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-07-0847-1, Pt 2
978-601-07-0874-7, set 978-601-07-0859-4, run 75,000 each. Uyghur editions (translator
Ya. Sharipov): Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-07-0921-8, Pt 2 978-601-07-0922-5, set
978-601-07-0920-1, run 2,000 each. In the Kazakh scans printed page = PDF page
throughout (cover and imprint are PDF pp. 1–2, contents p. 3, answers at the end).
Held files: 125, 126 (KZ); 148, 149 (RU); 1392, 1352 (Uyghur). Content across the
three languages is identical in structure, worked examples and exercise numbering
(spot-checked at §1, §16, §29 and the answers sections in all three languages).

**How the book teaches (both parts).** Each § opens with a "You will…" goals box,
a short discovery dialogue ("Think!", "Explain!") leading to a boxed rule, then
three exercise tiers — A (compulsory for all), B (medium), C (advanced/research) —
numbered continuously across the whole two-part book (Pt 1: ex. 1–555; Pt 2
continues). Recurring furniture: "Say it correctly" speech boxes (how to read a
formula aloud), "Prepare a report" history boxes (Roman numerals, Harriot/Recorde,
Oughtred/Leibniz, al-Khwarizmi, Descartes, Eratosthenes, Fibonacci, Pythagoras,
Tartaglia), "Getting ready for the new topic" revision boxes before each §, "Maths
in my family life / in my life" applied tasks, self-check questions at each § end,
and a full answers section. Every rule is stated in words and in letter form.

#### Part 1 (printed pp. 4–144)

**Chapter 1 — Natural numbers and zero (pp. 6–48).**
- §1 Writing natural numbers (p. 6): counting numbers, digit vs number, place-value
  classes (units/thousands/millions/billions, 3 places each), expanded form as a
  sum of place values, even/odd by last digit; Roman numerals as a history box with
  the additive/subtractive reading rules; reading real population figures from a
  table of countries.
- §2 The coordinate ray (p. 12): unit segment, origin, coordinate of a point,
  choosing a scale (marking 10/100/300 per unit), old Kazakh length units
  (tutam, qarys, qulam, shaqyrym, yard, foot, mile) in a reference box.
- §3 Comparing natural numbers (p. 17): by counting order, by number of digits,
  digit-by-digit; double inequalities and how to read them in both directions and
  in grammatical cases.
- §4 Arithmetic operations on natural numbers (p. 21): order of operations worked
  step-by-step; convenient calculation — regrouping addends, rounding a subtrahend,
  multiplying by 25/125 via 100/1000; big multi-step expressions.
- §5 Numerical and letter expressions; simplifying (p. 26): using the commutative/
  associative/distributive laws to simplify, coefficient, omitting the × sign,
  collecting like terms (e.g. 44x + 16x = 60x), evaluating expressions for given
  letter values, filling s = vt-type tables.
- §6 Equations (p. 31): root of an equation, equations with several roots or none;
  solving by the unknown-component rules (unknown addend/minuend/subtrahend/factor/
  dividend/divisor), with a check; solving after simplifying one side first
  (17x + 18x = 700 type).
- §7 Formulas; calculating by formula (p. 35): P = a + b + c, S = a·b and its
  rearrangements, s = v·t and rearrangements, cost = price × quantity; motion on a
  river — own speed ± current speed introduced here with diagrams.
- §8 Solving text problems (p. 41): multi-step arithmetic solutions; proportional
  reasoning by table (600 l for 50 m² → x l for 70 m²); the **segment (bar) diagram
  method** for sum-and-ratio problems (melon/watermelon 8 kg problem); composing
  problems from tables (railway fares, utility bills).
- §9 Sequences of natural numbers (p. 46): a sequence as an ordered list built by a
  rule; continue and describe patterns (incl. squares/cubes patterns and Fibonacci
  in the history box); a 5-term sequence "is not enough" discussion.

**Chapter 2 — Divisibility of natural numbers (pp. 50–76).**
- §10 Divisors and multiples; prime and composite numbers (p. 50): divisor/multiple
  definitions and the three speech forms of "divides"; prime = exactly two
  divisors, composite = more; 1 is neither; "there are infinitely many primes";
  table of primes under 1,000 printed on the book's flyleaf (қосарбет).
- §11 Basic properties of divisibility (p. 54): if each term is divisible by d so
  is the sum/difference; if one factor is divisible by d so is the product —
  proved by examples and used to avoid full division.
- §12 Tests for divisibility by 2, 3, 5, 9, 10 (p. 58): each test *derived* from
  place-value expansion in tables before being boxed as a rule; combined tests and
  "find the digit *" tasks; tests for 4 and 25 and for 6/11 are set as
  discover-it-yourself and preparation tasks rather than stated.
- §13 Powers (p. 63): repeated addition → multiplication → power; base, exponent;
  "square" and "cube"; a¹ = a; powers of 10 and the decimal (expanded) notation of
  a number; comparing powers.
- §14 Prime factorisation (p. 67): the vertical-bar ladder method choosing the
  smallest prime divisor each time, factor trees, writing the result with powers;
  reconstructing a number from its factorisation.
- §15 GCD and LCM (p. 71): GCD by listing, then by prime factorisation (common
  primes to the *smaller* exponent); coprime numbers (GCD 1); special cases (one
  number divides the other); LCM by factorisation (all primes to the *larger*
  exponent, topped up from the other number); for coprime numbers LCM = product;
  LCM(one divides other) = the larger; an exercise table leads pupils to discover
  GCD(a,b) · LCM(a,b) = a·b (ex. 253–255); word problems (equal teams, gift sets,
  tiling/buses type in B/C tiers).

**Chapter 3 — Common fractions and operations on them (pp. 78–141).**
- §16 The common fraction; reading and writing (p. 78): equal parts of a rectangle/
  circle; fraction m/n (m may be 0 or any natural, n natural), numerator and
  denominator and what each says; the fraction bar as division (m : n = m/n); a
  fraction as the ratio of a smaller to a larger quantity; fractions of units
  (1 cm = 1/10 dm, minutes of an hour, kg of a tonne).
- §17 The basic property of a fraction (p. 86): multiply/divide numerator and
  denominator by the same number (seen on a divided circle); the "additional
  factor" written as a superscript; **reducing** — three taught methods: (I) divide
  by common divisors one at a time, (II) factorise numerator and denominator into
  primes and cancel, (III) divide by the GCD at once; irreducible fraction; also
  cancelling inside fractional expressions with sums/products.
- §18 Proper and improper fractions; mixed numbers (p. 92): proper < 1, improper
  ≥ 1; a/a = 1; mixed number as whole + fraction (from sharing 7 strawberries
  between 2 children, 7 : 2 = 7/2 = 3½).
- §19 Converting improper ↔ mixed (p. 96): by division with remainder, and back by
  whole × denominator + numerator; linked to the division-with-remainder table.
- §20 Fractions and mixed numbers on the coordinate ray (p. 100).
- §21 Reducing fractions to a common denominator (p. 103): LCD = LCM of the
  denominators; shortcut rules (if one denominator divides the other, take the
  larger; if coprime, take the product).
- §22 Comparing fractions and mixed numbers (p. 106): same denominators; same
  numerators (the larger denominator gives the smaller fraction); via a common
  denominator; mixed numbers by whole part first, then fractional part; comparing
  with ½ as a benchmark in exercises.
- §23 Adding and subtracting fractions (p. 110): like denominators from pie
  pictures (a/c + b/c); unlike denominators via the LCD; regrouping addends;
  fractional-part equations.
- §24 Adding mixed numbers (p. 117): add wholes and fractional parts separately,
  carrying over when the fractional sum is improper, reducing the result.
- §25 Subtracting mixed numbers (p. 121): separate wholes and parts; borrowing one
  whole when the fractional part is too small; subtracting from a whole number by
  writing it as a mixed number with the needed denominator (98 = 97 21/21).
- §26 Multiplying fractions and mixed numbers (p. 128): motivated by the area of a
  rectangle with fractional sides on a grid (4/6 · 2/3); numerator × numerator,
  denominator × denominator; **cancel before multiplying**; fraction × natural;
  mixed numbers via improper fractions; powers of fractions in the exercises
  ((1/4)², (2/5)³ …) and the volume of a box with fractional edges.
- §27 Dividing fractions and mixed numbers (p. 132): division *derived* — the
  quotient is defined as the number which multiplied by the divisor gives the
  dividend; reciprocal numbers (product 1); rule: multiply by the reciprocal of
  the divisor; fraction ÷ natural (multiply the denominator); mixed via improper.
- §28 Operations with fractions, mixed numbers, naturals and zero (p. 137):
  long mixed expressions evaluated step-by-step; letter expressions evaluated for
  fractional values of a, b, c.
- Answers (pp. 141–143), colophon (p. 144).

**Honest negatives, Pt 1:** no decimal fractions, no percent, no negative numbers,
no geometry beyond perimeter/area formulas inside §7 (all deferred to Pt 2 / G6).
Percent is only named in the introduction as coming later in the course.

**Defects noticed (all language scans of this work):** the "You will…" goals boxes
and the dotted self-check boxes are printed in a decorative font that renders as
character mojibake in these PDF files (e.g. the §1 goals box is unreadable in the
scan); body text, rules and exercises are clean. In file 148 (Russian Pt 1) the
PDF carries one extra page over the stated 144 pp (the extra PDF page 145 is a blank leaf after p. 144, which carries the answers tail and the colophon together);
no content missing. No missing or duplicated pages found in Pt 1.


#### Part 2 (printed pp. 3–128; the part has its own pagination starting at the Ch. 4 divider, and in the Kazakh scan PDF p. = printed p. throughout; the colophon stands on printed p. 128 and the PDF's 129th page is a blank leaf)

**Chapter 4 — Text problems (pp. 3–10).**
- §29 Finding a fraction of a number and a number from its fraction (p. 4):
  fraction of a number = multiply the number by the fraction (worked first by
  division-then-multiplication on a segment diagram, e.g. 4/9 of a 45-minute
  lesson); number from its fraction = divide by the fraction (via x · 3/4 = 3).
- §30 Problems on joint work (p. 8): the whole job (or the whole distance, for
  meeting-motion problems) is taken as 1; each worker's rate is 1/time; rates
  add; time = 1/(combined rate). Two taps fill a pool in 1 1/5 h; two
  motorcyclists meet in 20 min. Exercises extend to three workers, one tap
  emptying, and "compose the problem yourself" tasks.

**Chapter 5 — Decimal fractions and operations on them (pp. 12–57).**
- §31 The decimal fraction; reading and writing (p. 12): denominators 10, 100,
  1000… written with a decimal comma (the book uses a comma as the decimal
  separator); whole part / fractional part; full place table from millions down
  to millionths; pad the numerator with zeros so its digit count matches the
  denominator's zeros (23 71/1000 = 23,071); reading rules in speech boxes;
  Stevin history box.
- §32 Converting a decimal to a common fraction (p. 17): write over 10/100/…,
  keep the whole part, reduce (0,038 = 38/1000 = 19/500).
- §33 Decimals on the coordinate ray; comparing decimals (p. 20): refine the unit
  segment by tenths then hundredths; 0,3 = 0,30 = 0,300 (trailing zeros rule shown
  as equal fractions); compare whole parts, then place-by-place, padding with
  zeros to equal length (3,297 < 3,2974); natural numbers lying between two
  decimals.
- §34 Adding and subtracting decimals (p. 26): column method, comma under comma,
  pad with zeros first, drop trailing zeros in the answer; decimals also written
  as sums of place values (527,0346 expanded); all laws of addition carried over.
- §35 Multiplying a decimal by a natural number (p. 30): multiply ignoring the
  comma, then separate as many places as the decimal has; padding zeros on the
  left when the product has too few digits (0,0013 · 67 = 0,0871).
- §36 Multiplying decimals (p. 33): from an area problem (9,1 dm × 1,9 dm);
  ignore both commas, separate (places of 1st + places of 2nd) digits; decimal
  powers in exercises (0,5²; 0,3³; 0,011²).
- §37 Dividing a decimal by a natural number (p. 36): long division, put the comma
  in the quotient when the whole part is exhausted, keep appending zeros;
  a common fraction is turned into a decimal by dividing numerator by denominator
  (5/16 = 0,3125); **2/3 = 0,666…** — the book names this an "infinite decimal"
  and answers with an ellipsis, without formal period notation.
- §38 Dividing a decimal by a decimal (p. 40): shift the comma in both numbers by
  the divisor's decimal places (3,792 : 7,9 = 37,92 : 79), then divide by a
  natural.
- §39 Multiplying and dividing by 10, 100, 1000… and by 0,1; 0,01; 0,001… (p. 43):
  comma-shift rules both ways, with the equivalence ×0,1 = ÷10 made explicit in
  the self-check questions.
- §40 Operations with decimal and common fractions together (p. 47): convert to
  one kind — to common fractions when the decimal is not finite, otherwise either;
  note printed in the book: the arithmetic laws for naturals and common fractions
  also hold for decimals.
- §41 Rounding numbers (p. 51): approximate value by deficiency and by excess;
  the sign ≈ and how to read it; the rounding rule (first dropped digit < 5 →
  down, ≥ 5 → up) applied to naturals (to tens…thousands) and decimals (to
  tenths, hundredths, thousandths) with the dropped-digit-underlined notation.
- §42 Solving text problems (p. 55): a pure problem § — mixed decimal/fraction
  word problems, pattern puzzles (number wheels), working-backwards problems.

**Chapter 6 — Sets (pp. 60–72).**
- §43 A set; elements of a set; representing sets (p. 60): a set as a collection
  (flock, herd, stamp album…); elements; describing a set by listing or by a
  characteristic property; finite vs infinite sets; the set of naturals **N**;
  the empty set ∅; membership ∈ / ∉ with speech forms; Euler–Venn circles;
  history: Cantor, Euler, Venn.
- §44 Relations between sets; subset (p. 64): disjoint vs intersecting sets;
  subset ⊂ (F ⊂ E read three ways); equal sets; ∅ and the set itself are subsets;
  a vocabulary box introduces **quantifier words** ("all/each/any" = generalising,
  "there is/at least one/some" = existence) as mathematical language.
- §45 Union and intersection of sets (p. 67): ∪ and ∩ defined in words and symbols,
  read off Venn diagrams (incl. three sets); pupils verify commutativity and
  associativity of ∪ and ∩ on diagrams.
- §46 Solving text problems (p. 70): counting problems by Venn diagram — the rule
  is stated in words and used numerically (camp: 15 + 9 − 2 = 22 pupils), i.e.
  inclusion–exclusion for two sets; sports-club and languages problems, incl. a
  three-language problem in the C tier.

**Chapter 7 — Percent (pp. 74–87).**
- §47 Percent (p. 74): percent = one hundredth; % sign written after the number;
  every familiar "1% of a unit" (1 mm of a dm, 1 cm of a m…); percent → number:
  drop the sign and divide by 100 (70% = 0,7; 280% = 2,8); number → percent:
  multiply by 100 and add % (0,3 = 30%); finding 1% of a quantity (÷100) and the
  quantity from its 1% (×100).
- §48 Finding the percent of a number and the number from its percent (p. 80):
  three problem types in one §, each with two methods — (i) percent of a number:
  write the percent as a common or decimal fraction and multiply, or find 1% then
  multiply by the percent count; (ii) number from its percent: divide by the
  fraction, or find 1% then ×100; (iii) what percent one number is of another:
  divide and express the quotient as a percent (shown in the closing tables of
  the §, ex. 838–841).
- §49 Solving text problems (p. 84): the three types camouflaged in one story
  (fresh vs dried mushrooms), then price rise/fall (+100% = the price doubled;
  −50% = halved), salary +30%, a simple bank deposit at 3,5% per year (ex. 859),
  percent remaining (100% − 14% = 86%), business-maths boxes (shop income split).

**Chapter 8 — Angles. Polygons (pp. 90–96).**
- §50 The angle (p. 90): angle, vertex, sides; notation ∠ABC (vertex letter in the
  middle) or one letter; the degree; protractor structure and the **measuring
  algorithm** (vertex on the centre, one side on the 0 mark, read the other);
  straight angle 180°, full angle 360°; acute < 90°, right = 90°, obtuse between;
  clock-face angles in exercises; theodolite in the report box.
- §51 The polygon (p. 94): polygon as a closed broken line; vertices, adjacent
  vertices, sides, angles; **diagonal**; perimeter as sum of sides; rectangle and
  square area recalled (S = ab, S = a²); counting diagonals of pentagon/heptagon
  in exercises.

**Chapter 9 — Diagrams (pp. 98–107).**
- §52 Circle and disc (p. 98): circle vs disc; centre, radius, diameter, d = 2r;
  the **sector** of a disc and its angle — the two sectors at a point sum to 360°,
  used to find an unknown sector angle (135° → 225°).
- §53 Diagrams; presenting statistical data (p. 101): pie diagram built
  step-by-step (total → per-unit angle, here 360 : 180 = 2° per ha → sector
  angles); bar diagram built two ways (scale in mm, or grid squares) and read;
  line diagram as joined segments of a bar diagram; conversion tasks table ↔ bar
  ↔ pie; Achenwall "statistics" history box; answers printed inside the working
  for the nitrate-data tasks.

**Chapter 10 — Nets of solids (pp. 110–116).**
- §54 The rectangular parallelepiped (cube) and its net (p. 110): faces, edges,
  vertices counted by a fill-in dialogue (cube/parallelepiped: 6 faces, 12 edges,
  8 vertices); cutting along edges to get a net; drawing and gluing nets; surface
  area computed from the net (cube face 49 cm² etc.); volume used from primary
  school (V of a cube from its edge; open-box cardboard problems).
- §55 Problems on cutting and assembling figures (p. 114): figures cut from a
  square along a diagonal are **equal-composed** (тең құралған); polygons that can
  be cut into the same pieces are equal-composed and therefore **equal in area**
  (тең шамалас); tangram silhouettes; Archimedes report box.

**Chapter 11 — Revision exercises (pp. 118–126)**, then **Answers** (pp. 127–128)
and colophon. Exercise numbering runs 556–956+ across Pt 2 continuously from Pt 1.

**Honest negatives, Pt 2 / whole G5 Mektep:** no negative numbers; no ratios or
proportions as such (percent ratio is the only ratio work); no mean/median; no
probability; no coordinates beyond the number ray; circle circumference/area NOT
taught (only sector angles); no triangle classification beyond naming in figures.
**Defects:** same mojibake goals/self-check boxes as Pt 1 (also in the chapter
title banners, e.g. Ch. 9–10 headings render garbled in the scan, while body text
stays clean). File 1445-style issues do not occur here: Pt 2 KZ scan (126) is
complete, 128 printed pp + title leaf. File 149 (RU Pt 2) matches §-for-§ (checked
at §29 and the contents of Ch. 4–5 openings).


### WORK 3 — Aldamuratova, Baysholanova, Baysholanov, *Mathematics*, Grade 6 (Atamura, 2018)

**As printed (Kazakh edition):** Т. А. Алдамуратова, Қ. С. Байшоланова, Е. С. Байшоланов, *Математика*, textbook for Grade 6 of the general-education school, in 2 parts, Almaty: Atamura, 2018. Pt 1 a stated 208 pp., Pt 2 a stated 224 pp. KZ ISBNs: set 978-601-331-146-3; Pt 1 978-601-331-145-6; Pt 2 978-601-331-147-0; print run 50,000 each part (orders № 3416 / № 3417; IB № 065 / № 066; Pt 1 sent to typesetting 11.01.2018, signed to print 15.05.2018; Pt 2 sent to typesetting 20.01.2018, signed to print 14.05.2018). Russian editions: set ISBN 978-601-331-130-2; Pt 1 978-601-331-129-6; Pt 2 978-601-331-131-9; print run 30,000 each part (orders № 3450 / № 3451; IB № 051 / № 052). Editorial board headed by Prof. S. Mukhambetzhanov, doctor of physical-mathematical sciences; the book is prepared to the standard programme of updated content for grades 5–6. The KZ imprint records agreement with experts of the A. Baitursynuly Institute of Linguistics; the RU imprint instead names two reviewers (G. N. Simakina, L. N. Onoprienko). Held files: 180, 181 (KZ); 207, 208 (RU). Page mapping: in the KZ scans PDF p. 1 is the title page, PDF p. 2 the imprint with the symbols key, and **PDF p. = printed p.** from printed p. 3 to the end (Pt 1: main text pp. 3–204, subject index p. 205, contents pp. 206–207, colophon on PDF p. 208; Pt 2: main text pp. 3–220, subject index p. 221, contents pp. 222–223, colophon on PDF p. 224). In the RU scans the symbols key has its own page: PDF p. 1 title, PDF p. 2 imprint, PDF p. 3 = printed p. 3 (symbols), and again PDF p. = printed p. throughout; RU contents at the end (Pt 1 p. 207; Pt 2 pp. 222–223). The contents of both editions are printed at the END of each part. One end-matter difference: where the KZ parts close with a subject index (Пәндік атау көрсеткіші), the RU parts close with a **Glossary** (Глоссарий, Pt 1 p. 206; Pt 2 p. 221) — an alphabetical list of terms with the page on which each is defined. There is no separate answers section in either edition: selected answers are printed in small blue boxes (marked ▲) at the ends of exercise blocks. Exercise numbering: Pt 1 opens with a Grade 5 revision block numbered 1–50, then the main numbering restarts at 1 in §1.1 and runs to 762; Pt 2 continues the same sequence, 763–1590. Every page of all four scans carries the OKULYK.KZ / «Не для печати» watermark and a footer recording that the book is provided for educational purposes under Order № 217 of the Minister of Education and Science of the RK of 17 May 2019; no outer-cover leaf is present in any file (PDF p. 1 is the title page itself).

**How the book teaches (both parts).** The unit of work is the numbered §, and almost every § is built the same way: a preparatory task (picture icon) or a boxed "problem" with a numbered instruction leads the pupil to the new fact; a worked **"Өзіңді өзің тексер" (check yourself)** block then shows the expected working, with expected pupil answers/conclusions marked by a double vertical bar; the theory follows in short stretches with definitions and rules in blue boxes and numbered worked examples ("1-mysal", "2-mysal", …); end-of-topic questions (book icon) close the theory; many §§ end with a boxed **"Тақырыптың түйіні" (topic summary)** recap (in RU: «Ключевые факты»). Exercises are tiered **A / B / C** (levels 1 / 2 / 3); the symbols key also marks revision exercises (blue type), harder problems (*), ingenuity problems (°), creative/logic exercises (blue border), tasks with several questions on one condition (bullet), ICT tasks (snowflake) and independent-study tasks (? in a box). Two recurring devices deserve note: **"Үйреніп алыңдар!" (Learn this!) inserts**, which pre-teach the core skill of the next § in worked form before the § begins (inverse division before §1.7's topic; triangle construction inside Ch. I; transposition solutions before §4.2; deriving quotients before §2.15); and **historical-information blocks** at chapter ends (ratio/proportion history; number systems and negative numbers; the history of zero; Descartes on variable quantities). Sections marked "(қосымша оқу үшін)" — for additional reading — carry the hardest or most optional matter (§§2.6, 2.16, 3.5, 10.2). The coordinate line is the book's universal instrument: addition, comparison, distance, modulus equations and modulus inequalities are all first done on the line and only then by rule. Kazakh realia are constant (tenge amounts, Astana–Almaty distances, the Ertis, Balkhash, the Aral Sea, Talgar peak, Khan Tengri). Set language is used early and matter-of-factly (N ⊂ Z ⊂ Q with Euler–Venn diagrams in §2.3; ∅ for empty solution sets; ∪ and ∩ in §5.4). Against the Mektep Grade 6 book (Work 4), which covers the same broad territory, the order differs: algebraic expressions are finished (Ch. III) before equations are opened (Ch. IV); statistics and combinatorics form their own chapter (Ch. VIII); and this book alone of the two treats periodic decimals in both directions, the compound-percent formula, space-figure depiction, and the vector concept. Two depth differences are flagged in the bullets below: §1.5 teaches direct proportionality **without** the formula y = kx and without a named coefficient (the formula and the coefficient arrive only in §9.5), and §10.2 **does** draw the graph of a two-variable linear equation, where the Mektep book draws none.

**Pagination in the Russian edition.** Section and exercise numbering are identical to the KZ edition everywhere checked (see the spot-check note at the end), but the RU type is set slightly looser, so printed start pages drift. Pt 1 RU: revision §§ pp. 4, 8, 12; §1.1 p. 16; §1.4 p. 32; §1.9 p. 63; §2.1 p. 72; §2.4 p. 89; §2.13 p. 143; §2.17 p. 164; §2.18 p. 170; §3.1 p. 176; §3.3 p. 189; §3.5 p. 202 — i.e. +1 to +4 against the KZ pages used below. Pt 2 RU is nearly coincident: §4.1 p. 4, §4.2 p. 9, §4.3 p. 20 (+1 each), then §5.2 p. 33, §5.5 p. 52, §§6.1–6.6 pp. 77–108, §§7.1–7.2 pp. 115–122, Ch. VIII pp. 127–146, Ch. IX pp. 148–176 and all of Ch. X on the same pages as KZ; the closing Grade 5 revision block starts at RU p. 210 (KZ 209). All page numbers in the filing below are the KZ printed pages.

#### Part 1 (printed pp. 3–208)

**Revision of what was covered in Grade 5 (pp. 3–13; exercises 1–50, numbered separately).**

- **1. Divisibility of natural numbers (p. 3).** Recall box ("Есіңе түсір") with the tests for 2, 5, 10, 3, 9 in task-and-self-check form; prime vs composite; divisor and multiple; GCD defined and used to reduce 48/80 (GCD 16); LCM defined and used to bring 2/15 and 7/12 to the common denominator 60. Ex. 1–17, including unicursal (one-stroke) figures (ex. 9), a gear LCM problem (ex. 14*) and a balance puzzle (ex. 17*).
- **2. Operations on common and decimal fractions (p. 7).** One working rule: in a mixed expression convert everything to one kind of fraction. The finite-decimal criterion is boxed in both directions (an irreducible fraction can be written as a decimal iff the prime factors of its denominator are only 2 and 5). Ex. 18–35, ending in fractional equations with multiple-choice answers (ex. 35).
- **3. Finding the percent of a number; finding a number from its percent (p. 11).** 1% = 1/100 = 0.01; conversions (60% = 0.6 = 3/5); the two boxed rules (percent of a number — multiply; number from its percent — divide). Ex. 36–50, with bank-deposit problems (7% a year on 7,000,000 tg, ex. 42).

**Chapter I. Ratio and proportion (pp. 14–69).**

- **§1.1 Ratio (p. 14; ex. 1–26).** Comparison by difference vs comparison by quotient; ratio defined and boxed (a : b or a/b), preceding and following terms, a "how to read it" box; inverse ratio; the basic property (both terms may be multiplied or divided by the same number); ratios of like quantities need common units first (75 cm : 1 m = 3 : 4); a ratio of unlike quantities gives a new quantity (v = s/t; 80 km in 5 h = 16 km/h). Topic summary p. 19. Ex. 26* is an ICT task (length of the Ertis from internet sources).
- **§1.2 Dividing a number in a given ratio (p. 20; ex. 27–46).** Master and apprentice share 180 rings in the ratio 18 : 12 = 3 : 2, solved two ways (by parts; by table); 350 g divided 2 : 5, also two ways, with the algebraic form x = 350/(2+5)·2; a boxed three-step algorithm. Chained ratios whose parts are equalised through the LCM appear as ex. 41* with a full printed hint; the four-term ratio 2 : 3 : 5 : 8 is ex. 38.
- **§1.3 Finding the percent ratio of two numbers (p. 24; ex. 47–70).** P% = (b/a)·100% boxed; a worked example laid out as a scheme (140 ha = 100%, 91 ha = ?%); the microcalculator key sequence for percent is taught explicitly; boxed formulas for seed-germination percent and for solution concentration. Exercises: price rises and falls, mixtures (ex. 65–67), ICT ex. 70*.
- **§1.4 Proportion. The main property of proportion (p. 30; ex. 71–99).** Discovery route: from two equal ratios to the definition; extreme and mean terms named on a diagram; the products of the terms are then computed and the main property ad = bc is boxed; finding an unknown term; a word problem set up through a scheme plus a proportion (wallpaper rolls). Ex. 71–99 include deriving seven further proportions from one given proportion (ex. on p. 33), the false coin in at most two weighings (ex. 87) and a problem from Newton's *Universal Arithmetic* (ex. 98*). The topic summary (pp. 36–37) covers the section's two halves together.
- **§1.5 Direct proportional dependence (p. 37; ex. 100–124).** Lead-ins: rectangle area against width (table) and s = vt (table plus bar chart); definition boxed; the property is stated only in the form x₁/x₂ = y₁/y₂. **There is no y = kx formula and no named proportionality coefficient in this section** (contrast Mektep §3, and this book's own §9.5, where both appear). Example pairs of quantities are listed; problems are solved with a same-direction arrows diagram.
- **§1.6 Inverse proportional dependence (p. 43; ex. 125–145).** Workers-and-time table (4 workers, 120 h → 1 worker, 480 h); the flipped proportion y₁/y₂ = x₂/x₁; the product of corresponding values is constant (y₁x₁ = y₂x₂, boxed). Lead-in also uses a constant-area rectangle table with bar chart. The book warns explicitly that not every pair of quantities is directly or inversely proportional and gives counterexamples. Ex. 125–145 include an "ancient problem" (ex. 130) and ex. 131, whose letter-coded answers spell a term. Topic summary p. 48.
- **Insert "Үйреніп алыңдар!" (p. 49).** Dividing a quantity into parts *inversely* proportional to given numbers: replace the numbers by their reciprocals and divide directly (136 m in parts inverse to 3 and 5 → 85 m and 51 m); five unnumbered problems inside the insert.
- **§1.7 Solving percent problems using proportion (pp. 50–53; ex. 146–155).** One § with three subsections (I. percent of a number, p. 50; II. a number from its percent, p. 51; III. percent ratio of two numbers, p. 52) and a single four-step method: write the condition as a — 100%, b — P%; form a/b = 100/P; mark the unknown x; find it. A practical task on p. 53 (measure your room and draw it reduced) bridges to §1.8.
- **§1.8 Scale (p. 54; ex. 156–180).** Opens by drawing the book's own page reduced seven times; scale defined and boxed; numerical scale vs named scale (1 cm — 75 km); plan scale defined and boxed (p. 55); enlargement scales 5 : 1, 10 : 1, …; worked examples: scale from 3.5 cm ↔ 175 km = 1 : 5,000,000; a room plan at 1 : 200; an insect's leg drawn at 10 : 1 measures 6 mm. Exercises include an Astana–Karaganda map measurement and an ICT practical with a school atlas (ex. 180*). Topic summary p. 59.
- **§1.9 Circumference. Area of a disc. Ball. Sphere (p. 60; ex. 181–203).** Circumference is found by a practical (thread round a glass rim; C/D computed for two glasses); π printed as 3.14159265…, π ≈ 3.14 used; C = πD and C = 2πR boxed; circumference noted as directly proportional to diameter. The disc area S = πR² is **stated, not derived** — presented as a result of mathematical research (the disc equals π times the square on the radius). Ball and sphere: centre, radius, diameter defined; hollow everyday spheres (soap bubble, volleyball, tennis ball); a plane section of a ball is a disc; the Earth as a ball. The topic summary (p. 65) restates ball and sphere in set language (sets of points of space at distance ≤ R / = R from the centre). Exercises include a cylinder-net problem (ex. 196 — the cylinder's only appearance in the chapter) and a clock-hands angle problem (ex. 202*).
- **Insert "Үйреніп алыңдар!" (p. 65).** Constructing a triangle from three given sides with compass and ruler, in steps (AB = 5 cm, AC = 4 cm, BC = 3 cm).
