- **Chapter I revision exercises (pp. 65–68; ex. 204–226).**
- **Historical information (pp. 68–69).** Ratio and proportion in Omar Khayyam and Nasir al-Din al-Tusi; Euclid's *Elements* (portrait), Theaetetus and Eudoxus; the history of proportion notation (Arabic right-to-left form, Descartes' 8/15/96/180, Leibniz's modern form of 1693); a quotation from Newton's *Universal Arithmetic*. **Contents slip:** the Pt 1 contents (p. 206) lists this block at p. 62; it actually stands at pp. 68–69, after the revision exercises.

**Chapter II. Rational numbers and operations on them (pp. 70–172).**

- **§2.1 Positive and negative numbers (p. 70; ex. 227–240).** Quantities changing in opposite directions; agreed zeros (ice melting at 0 °C, sea level); "+" above zero, "−" below; thermometer reading tasks with self-checks (+7 °C read "plus 7 degrees"); Talgar peak +4973 m, Black Sea depth −2210 m; 0 is neither positive nor negative (boxed); positive numbers together with 0 are "not negative", negative with 0 "not positive"; the + sign is omitted at the start of an expression, negatives are bracketed after operation signs. Exercises include a Kazakhstan temperature map for 15 March (ex. 228), the Karakia depression −132 m (ex. 231) and accounting tables of profit and loss (ex. 235–236).
- **§2.2 The coordinate line. Opposite numbers (p. 75; ex. 241–258).** Lead-in (p. 74): two boys run in opposite directions from one point — a guided drawing (unit 1 cm = 1 m) that motivates the line. I. The ray is extended to a line; definition boxed (origin O, positive direction, unit segment); each number corresponds to exactly one point; notation A(5), B(2.5), C(−3), D(−4.5). II. Opposite numbers differ only in sign (boxed); 0 is its own opposite (boxed); the notation −a and −(−a) = a. Exercises include equations −x = 15 4/7 (ex. 247), a return of proportion equations (ex. 252) and the 150 g sugar weighing puzzle (ex. 255). The topic summary (p. 81) already defines opposite numbers through equal moduli (|6| = |−6|) — **before modulus is taught in §2.4**.
- **§2.3 Integers. Rational numbers (p. 81; ex. 260–278).** I. Integers: the naturals, their opposites and 0; the set Z written out; N ⊂ Z in subset language with an Euler–Venn diagram; positive and negative integers. II. Rational numbers: numbers writable as m/n with m ∈ Z, n ∈ N (boxed); every integer as m/1; decimals as fractions (−8.21 = −821/100); the set Q; "rational" from Latin *ratio*; N ⊂ Z ⊂ Q. The **sign rule for dropping brackets is taught here** (p. 84) as a table: +(+) → +, +(−) → −, −(+) → −, −(−) → +. Exercises include a letter-coded computation whose answers spell a phrase about the planets (ex. 273) and a family logic problem (ex. 276).
- **§2.4 Modulus of a number (p. 87; ex. 279–303).** Lead-in: a girl and a ball both 3 m from a tree, on opposite sides — same distance, different coordinates. Modulus = the distance of the point from the origin (boxed); Latin *modulus* = measure; also called the absolute value. I. |a| = a for a > 0; II. for a < 0 the modulus is the opposite number; III. |0| = 0; the piecewise definition is boxed (p. 88); the modulus of any non-zero rational is positive (boxed); IV. opposite numbers have equal moduli, |−a| = |a|. Equations |x| = 4 are solved by cases, |5x − 8| = 0 directly, |x| = 9 also geometrically. Exercises include magic squares (ex. 295), sums by the Gauss method (ex. 298) and the nested modulus ||x| − 3| = 0 (ex. 302*). Topic summary p. 94.
- **§2.5 Comparing rational numbers (p. 95; ex. 304–330).** I. Through thermometer tasks and the line: the larger number lies to the right (boxed); any negative is less than any positive; the notation a > 0 for positive, a < 0 for negative. II. Two negatives, first through thermometers (−2 > −5), then the boxed modulus rule: of two negative numbers the one with the smaller modulus is the larger. Betweenness is written as double inequalities (ex. 312; crop germination temperature ranges, ex. 313). Exercises include reading |x| < 2, |x| > 3, … as sets of integers (ex. 320*) and the 15-rings-in-3-weighings puzzle (ex. 319).
- **§2.6 Complex problems on motion on a river (additional reading) (p. 102; ex. 331–345).** A formula box: v_down = v_own + v_current; v_up = v_own − v_current; v_own = (v_down + v_up)/2; v_current = (v_down − v_up)/2. Worked example by the unit method: distance taken as 1, boat 30 min down and 40 min up, hence the raft needs 240 min = 4 h.
- **Historical information (pp. 104–106).** Number systems and negative numbers: counting origins; digits from India through the Arabs; Polynesian word-counting in twos; the Roman quinary system; Babylonian sexagesimal reckoning (hours, degrees); positional decimal notation from the East (9th c.); negatives in China (2nd c. BC; increase/debt), the *Nine Chapters*, Diophantus, Indian mathematicians and debt (6th–7th c.), Brahmagupta (598–660) giving the first arithmetic rules, Fibonacci, Stifel, Girard (1595–1632) placing negatives to the left of the origin, Descartes 1637 (portrait); full acceptance only in the early 18th century.
- **§2.7 Adding rational numbers with the help of the coordinate line (p. 107; ex. 346–364).** Boxed algorithm: mark A(a); if b > 0 shift right by b, if b < 0 shift left by |b|. Adding b is "changing a by b units"; a + (−a) = 0 (boxed); a + 0 = a. The exercise set is dominated by shift diagrams to read and to draw; a parity puzzle about a telephone network (ex. 358) is boxed. Topic summary p. 113 (with a hexagon diagram).
- **§2.8 Adding negative numbers (p. 114; ex. 365–390).** The thermometer problem −3 + (−4) is first done on the line (task plus self-check), noting |−3| + |−4| = |−7|; rule boxed: add the moduli and prefix the minus sign; the sum of two negatives is negative (boxed); adding 0. Exercises include Oymyakon and Vostok record temperatures (ex. 379), splitting 32 kg of sugar without weights (ex. 386) and an ICT task on the depth of Baikal (ex. 390*).
- **§2.9 Adding rational numbers with different signs (p. 120; ex. 391–412).** Two thermometer/line examples (−3 + 4 = 1; 3 + (−5) = −2); rule boxed: subtract the smaller modulus from the larger and take the sign of the addend with the larger modulus; opposite addends in a long sum are crossed out. Exercises include the shrinking Aral Sea (ex. 405), a football goal-difference table (ex. 402) and modulus equations (ex. 399, 408).
- **§2.10 Properties of addition of rational numbers (p. 126; ex. 413–426).** Lead-in on p. 125: compute conveniently, in two ways. I. Commutativity a + b = b + a (boxed, checked numerically); II. associativity (a + b) + c = a + (b + c) (boxed). Working strategy: group the positives together and the negatives together, then combine. Exercises include a heavier false coin (ex. 421) and Gauss sums of negative numbers (ex. 423).
- **§2.11 Subtracting rational numbers. Length of a segment on the coordinate line (p. 129; ex. 427–454).** I. Subtraction is defined through the unknown addend (b + x = a); the sign rule rewrites −2 − (+5) as −2 + (−5); the rule a − b = a + (−b) is boxed; the cases reduce to the two addition rules; special cases a − a = 0, 0 − a = −a, a − 0 = a. II. The length of a segment on the line is the right end's coordinate minus the left end's (boxed): AB = 3 − (−4) = 7. Exercises run to an entrepreneur's bank-loan problem (ex. 454*; the answer box gives net profit 2,064,000 tg at 12%).
- **§2.12 Distance between two points on the coordinate line (p. 137; ex. 455–469).** Lead-in tasks (p. 136) compare |a − b| for A(−8), B(−2) and C(−1), D(4). Distance = |a − b| (boxed), with |a − b| = |b − a| shown; C(−9), D(−2) → 7. Exercises include the equidistant point (ex. 459: C(2) between A(−3) and B(7)).
- **§2.13 Multiplying rational numbers (p. 140; ex. 470–496).** Lead-in tasks: products as sums of equal addends; a diver descending 3 m a second. I. Unlike signs: (−4)·3 = (−4) + (−4) + (−4) = −12; rule boxed with the sign table. II. Two negatives (p. 141) are justified **through Euler**: since (−7)·5 = −35, the product (−7)·(−5) must be its opposite, 35; rule boxed, (−)(−) → (+). Working order: sign first, then modulus. The sign of a long product follows the parity of the negative factors; the zero-factor property is boxed and used at once ((−7)(x + 4) = 0 → x = −4). Powers of negative numbers appear in the exercises ((−11)², (−4)³; comparing (−10)³ with 10³; (−1)⁹⁹ vs (−1)¹⁰⁰).
- **§2.14 Commutative and associative properties of multiplication of rational numbers (p. 147; ex. 497–509).** a·b = b·a and (a·b)·c = a·(b·c), boxed, with numeric checks; convenient grouping (0.25·(−1.2)·(−8)·5 = 12). **The distributive property is not in this section** — it is held back for §3.2. Exercises include a pigeonhole proof (ex. 504: 16 pupils at 8 tables, so some table seats 2 boys) and worst-case ball draws (ex. 505: 7 green, 4 yellow, 2 red).
- **§2.15 Dividing rational numbers (p. 150; ex. 510–535).** A "Learn this!" insert (p. 149) first derives quotients from multiplication equalities (−12 : (−3) = 4, …). Division is defined as the inverse of multiplication: a : b = c iff c·b = a, b ≠ 0. I. Unlike signs, through guess-the-root tasks with a self-check; rule boxed. II. Negative divided by negative: write as a fraction and multiply numerator and denominator by −1 ((−4) : (−8) = 4/8 = 0.5); like signs give a positive quotient; 0 : a = 0; division by zero is impossible (stated). Exercises include zero-product equations such as (4.5x + 3.6)(−19.6) = 0 (ex. 523) and a ribbon-folding puzzle (ex. 524*).
- **§2.16 Complex problems on percent (additional reading) (p. 155; ex. 536–569).** A problem set (percent of a percent, successive rises and falls, reverse problems), with one piece of new theory printed in the middle of the set (p. 157): **compound percent growth** — each step's growth is taken on the preceding value — with the formula Sₙ = S(1 + P/100)ⁿ and a legend; worked deposit 3,000,000 tg at 8% compound for 3 years = 3,779,136 tg, by iteration and by the formula; ex. 565 compares simple and compound growth on the same deposit. The formula appears nowhere else in the book.
- **Historical information on the number zero (p. 160).** Babylonian empty place and wedge mark; the Greek O from *ouden* ("nothing"); Indian *sunya* → Arabic *as-sifr* → "cipher"; Latin *nullus*; zero is a number (a + 0 = a, a − 0 = a, a·0 = 0, 0 : a = 0 for a ≠ 0); 0 is the coordinate of the origin and is neither positive nor negative.
- **§2.17 Writing rational numbers as periodic decimal fractions (p. 160; ex. 570–596).** I. Finite decimals: for an irreducible m/n, if the denominator's prime factors are only 2 and 5 the decimal is finite (7/20 = 0.35, long division printed); otherwise the division never ends (2/9 = 0.222…, 5/6 = 0.833…, long divisions printed); periodic decimal and period defined (boxed); notation 0,(2) with its reading. II. Pure vs mixed periodic decimals: pure when the period starts immediately after the comma (2/3 = 0,(6); denominators containing neither 2 nor 5); every integer is pure periodic with period 0 (7 = 7,(0)); mixed when digits stand between the comma and the period (7/15 = 0.4(6); denominators containing 2 or 5 and another prime); every finite decimal can also be written as mixed periodic with period 0 (2.31 = 2.31(0)). Boxed conclusion: every rational number can be written as an infinite periodic decimal. Exercises include a research task on the periods of 1/11 … 10/11 (ex. 588) and a milk-measuring puzzle (ex. 584).
- **§2.18 Converting an infinite periodic decimal fraction to a common fraction (p. 167; ex. 597–606).** Built on 1/9 = 0,(1), 1/99 = 0,(01), 1/999 = 0,(001). Pure periodic: numerator = the period, denominator = as many 9s as the period has digits (boxed): 0,(417) = 417/999. Mixed periodic: a three-step algorithm (multiply by 10, 100, … to make it pure; convert; divide back): 0.6(54) = 36/55; 3.71(63) = 3 197/275. Exercises include the page-numbering problem (ex. 603: 904 digits spent, first page numbered 3 → 338 pages) and a multi-storey fraction (ex. 606).
- **Chapter II revision exercises (pp. 169–172; ex. 607–626)**, headed "Applying arithmetic operations to rational numbers".

**Chapter III. Algebraic expressions (pp. 173–204).**

- **§3.1 Algebraic expression. Variable. Algebraic sum (p. 173; ex. 627–657).** Lead-in (p. 172): a bus table for s = v·t — which quantity may be taken as the variable, and is 50t an expression with a variable? I. Definition boxed (letters, numbers, operation signs, brackets); the basic property: a letter may be replaced only by a value for which the expression has meaning — permissible values, with 5/(x − 3), x ≠ 3, written {x | x ≠ 3}; substituting gives a numerical expression, whose value is the value of the algebraic expression. II. Variable: the value of 2(a + b) depends on the letters; the set of permissible values is called the domain. III. Algebraic sum: expressions joined by + and −; its terms; writing a sum without brackets (14a + (−3b) + (−8c) + (+9d) = 14a − 3b − 8c + 9d). Exercises include a practical proof of the divisibility tests for 2, 5, 9 from the expansion 100a + 10b + c (ex. 647), a numerical comparison of (a + b)² with a² + 2ab + b² and of (a − b)² with a² − 2ab + b² (ex. 651 — computed at values, not named as identities) and the volume of an open box from a net (ex. 655).
- **§3.2 Expanding brackets. Coefficient (p. 180; ex. 658–684).** Lead-in (p. 179): the distributive law with negatives, 4(−2 + 3a − 5b) = −8 + 12a − 20b, with fill-in tasks. Case 1: "+" before the brackets — the terms keep their signs: a + (−b + c − d) = a − b + c − d (boxed). Case 2: "−" before the brackets — every term changes sign: a − (b − c + d) = a − b + c − d (boxed). Case 3: number and letter factors in a product are regrouped by the associative and commutative laws (2·(−4a)·3b = −24ab). II. Coefficient: the number factor in a product expression, written before the letter factors; 1a = a and −1a = −a, the 1 unwritten. Case 4 (p. 182): a(b + c) = ab + ac by the distributive law. The topic summary (p. 186) adds: to multiply a sum by a number, multiply each term. **Factoring out a common factor is not treated here** — it appears only as the lead-in tasks of §3.3 and as revision ex. 738.
- **§3.3 Like terms. Combining like terms (p. 187; ex. 685–710).** §3.3 stands at p. 187 (the summary's earlier uncertainty is resolved: the Pt 1 contents, p. 207, lists 187, and the § opens there). Lead-in: simplify sums by factoring out the common letter factor (ax + ay + az, …). I. Like terms have the same letter part and may differ only in coefficient (boxed side definition). II. Combining = factoring out: 3x − 8x + 7x = (3 − 8 + 7)x = 2x, shown in an annotated diagram (common letter factor / original expression / simplified form); rule boxed: add the coefficients and multiply by the common letter part; several groups are underlined differently; terms without a letter part are called free terms and combine too (2.8x − 7y + 5.6 + 3y − 4x − 2 → −1.2x − 4y + 3.6). Exercises include a proof that in a school of 24 classes and 764 pupils some class has fewer than 32 pupils (ex. 698, blue box), angles of a circle in letters (ex. 709) and the value of a continued fraction (ex. 710).
- **§3.4 Identities. Identity transformations (p. 193; ex. 711–734).** I. a(b + 8) and ab + 8a are checked at a = −2, b = 3 and then justified for all values by the distributive law; an identity is an equality true for **every** permissible value of the variables (boxed); identically equal expressions (boxed); the arithmetic laws and a·0 = 0, a + 0 = a, a·1 = a are identities, and a true numerical equality (2(3 + 5) = 2·3 + 2·5) also counts as one. II. An identity transformation replaces an expression by an identically equal one (2x − 7x → −5x cuts evaluation at x = 0.3 from three steps to one). Tasks are keyed to each law, with self-checks. Exercises include "prove the identity" items that are expansions in disguise (ex. 729: (a + b)a − (a − b)b = a² + b²; (a + b)a + (a + b)b = a² + 2ab + b²), the monkeys-and-bananas problem (ex. 733*) and solving equations efficiently (ex. 734).
- **Chapter III revision exercises (pp. 199–200; ex. 735–748).**
- **§3.5 Multi-storey fractions (additional reading) (p. 201; ex. 750–762).** A fraction whose numerator or denominator (or both) is itself a fraction. Two methods: (1) carry out the operations step by step; (2) multiply numerator and denominator by the LCM of the small denominators (worked with LCM(2, 3, 4) = 12). A continued fraction is built up in four steps: 1 + 1/(2 + 1/(3 − 1/4)) = 1 11/26.

Part 1 closes with the subject index (p. 205), contents (pp. 206–207) and colophon (p. 208).

#### Part 2 (printed pp. 3–224)

**Chapter IV. Linear equations in one variable (pp. 3–25).**

- **§4.1 Numerical equalities. Properties of true numerical equalities (p. 3; ex. 763–778).** The numerical equality is recalled from Grade 5, then **five** properties, each in a blue box: (1) transitivity — if a = b and b = c, then a = c; (2) the same number may be added to (subtracted from) both sides — with the corollary stated immediately: a term may be carried to the other side with its sign changed; (3) both sides may be multiplied or divided by the same non-zero number; (4) true equalities may be added termwise (a + c = b + d); (5) true equalities may be multiplied termwise (ac = bd). Exercises include placing brackets to make an equality true (ex. 769), building three equalities from the a = b, b = c, a = c property (ex. 773) and measuring out water with 9 l and 5 l vessels (ex. 775). The topic summary (p. 7) tabulates only properties 1–3.
- **Insert "Үйреніп алыңдар!" (p. 8).** Two transposition solutions worked in full (9x = x + 12 → x = 1,5; 8x − 7 = 2x + 5 → x = 2) plus two practice equations — the §4.2 skill pre-taught before §4.2 opens.
- **§4.2 Linear equations in one variable. Equivalent equations (p. 8; ex. 779–820).** The § opens with a biographical box and portrait: **Orynbay A. Zhautikov (1911–1989)**, Kazakh academician whose main works concern equations and theoretical and applied mechanics. I. A linear equation has the form ax + b = 0, where a is the coefficient of the variable and b the free term (boxed); the root is defined; to solve an equation is to find all its roots or prove there are none. II. Equivalent equations have the same root, or both have none (boxed); equivalence rule 1 (add/subtract the same number or expression on both sides) with the transposition corollary boxed; rule 2 (multiply/divide both sides by the same non-zero number); an explicit warning that multiplying both sides by 0 destroys equivalence (x = 6 vs x·0 = 6·0). III. Solving ax + b = 0 in three cases: I. a ≠ 0 — one root x = −b/a, with a three-step instruction and a worked self-check (8x − 9 = 3x + 8 → 3,4); II. a = 0, b ≠ 0 — no root, the solution set is the empty set ∅; III. a = 0, b = 0 — every number is a root, infinitely many roots. A word problem (two rectangles) is solved **with a check** (288 = 288). The topic summary (p. 17) carries the roots table (5x + 3 = 0 → −0.6; 0x + 4 = 0 → none; 0x + 0 = 0 → any number). Exercises include sorting equations into "no root" and "every number is a root" (ex. 795), finding roots by trial — the "selection method" — for the non-linear x(x + 5) = 104 (ex. 805, blue box), the largest-area rectangle from 18 sticks of 4 cm (ex. 806), parameter tasks (ex. 807–808: choose n so that the root is −2; find a so that 2ax = 5 has no root), recognising equivalent pairs among modulus and quadratic equations (ex. 809: |y + 2| = 7 against (y − 5)(y + 9) = 0), an ancient walnut-division problem (ex. 818) and an ICT barometric-altitude task (ex. 820*: Talgar peak pressure 262.7 mm Hg).
- **Independent work (p. 18, unnumbered).** Four balance-picture tasks: compose a linear equation from the scales and solve it (flour packets against 4 kg; five cabbage heads and 500 g against two heads and 2 kg; tea packets; a jar of honey and 500 g against the empty jar and 3 kg of weights).
- **§4.3 Linear equations with the variable inside the modulus sign (p. 19; ex. 821–837).** Lead-in (p. 19): |x| = 2; |x| = 3,2; |x − 1,5| = 3; |x + 6| = 3, with |x| = 3,5 solved geometrically as a sample. The modulus properties used are listed: |a| ≥ 0; |−a| = |a|; |ab| = |a|·|b|; |a/b| = |a|/|b| (b ≠ 0); |a|² = a² — a fuller list than the properties named in §2.4. **Two methods are taught side by side and both are used in every worked example**: (1) distance on the coordinate line through |a − b|; (2) the piecewise definition of modulus, by cases. |x| = 5; |x − 3| = 4 → −1, 7; |x + 1| = 6 rewritten as |x − (−1)| = 6 → −7, 5; |5x + 1| = −6 has no solution because the left side's value is a non-negative number. Exercises include the nested modulus ||5x − 4| + 2| = 7 with a full printed solution (ex. 836*), a logic puzzle solved by a graph/table (ex. 833) and dice events classified as certain, random or impossible (ex. 837 — the book's only probability-flavoured item, purely qualitative).
- **Chapter IV revision exercises (pp. 24–25; ex. 838–848).**

**Chapter V. Linear inequalities in one variable and their systems (pp. 26–75).**

- **§5.1 Numerical inequalities (p. 26; ex. 849–872).** Lead-in (p. 26): compare two numbers by the sign of their difference, in a table. Definition boxed (two numerical expressions joined by > or <); left and right sides named; strict vs non-strict inequalities; the readings of ≥ ("greater than or equal", "not less") and ≤ ("not greater"); a double inequality 3 ≤ x ≤ 7 read two ways; inequalities of the same sense and of opposite sense. The comparison rule is **derived** in a task with a self-check table: if a − b > 0 then a > b; if a − b < 0 then a < b (boxed). Exercises include proving an inequality from a condition (ex. 867) and a pencil problem asking how many solutions it has (ex. 869).
- **§5.2 Properties of numerical inequalities (p. 33; ex. 873–904).** **Six** properties, each boxed with numeric examples, several applied to double inequalities as wholes: (1) transitivity, argued on the coordinate line; (2) the same number may be added to or subtracted from both sides — transposing a term with its sign changed is boxed separately; (3) multiplying or dividing by a positive number keeps the sign, by a negative number **reverses** it (two boxes); (4) same-sense inequalities may be added termwise; (5) same-sense inequalities with positive sides may be multiplied termwise (ac > bd); (6) reciprocals: if 0 < a < b then 1/a > 1/b. **There is no termwise-division property** (the Mektep book's sixth property is a different one). The dominant exercise theme is *estimating*: from 4 < a < 5 estimate a + 3, a − 0,6, 2a, a/2, 1/a; from two double inequalities estimate x + y, x − y, xy, x/y (ex. 884–903). The topic summary (p. 40) restates all six properties in a table with a worked column.
- **§5.3 Number intervals (p. 41; ex. 905–924).** Lead-in: the values satisfying 1 < x ≤ 5, with the endpoint conventions (a filled dot belongs, a small ring does not). The interval between the points a and b is defined, then five forms, each worked with picture, notation and spoken reading: the interval (2; 7); the segment [−4; 3]; the half-interval [−2; 4); the ray [8; +∞); the open ray (−∞; 5); the whole line (−∞; +∞) is called the number line. Summary table p. 46. Exercises include drawing the solution sets of |x| conditions as intervals (ex. 923).
- **§5.4 Union and intersection of number intervals (p. 46; ex. 925–937).** Both operations are **discovered** through guided tasks with self-checks. Union defined (boxed): A ∪ B consists of the numbers belonging to at least one of them; [−2; 7] ∪ [4; 11] = [−2; 11]; a disjoint pair's union is **not** one interval ([−6; 1] ∪ [4; 10] stays a union). Intersection defined (boxed): (−∞; 3] ∩ [−4; +∞) = [−4; 3]; [−2; 9] ∩ [5; 12] = [5; 9]; disjoint intervals give the empty set; intervals touching at a point give {7}. A three-step rule closes the § (draw both; find the common / at-least-one part; write the notation). Exercises include constructing intervals whose intersection is (3; 7) and whose union is [−2; 5] (ex. 936).
- **§5.5 The linear inequality in one variable. Equivalent inequalities. Solving linear inequalities (p. 52; ex. 938–966).** Lead-ins (pp. 51–52): 4x − 5 < 3x + 1 → x < 6, and a transposition diagram with arrows (4x + 3 > x + 18 → x > 5). The solution of an inequality is the **set** of values making it a true numerical inequality (boxed — see the editions note below); equivalent inequalities have the same solution set (boxed), and inequalities without solutions are also mutually equivalent. **Three equivalence rules**, boxed: (1) carry a term to the other side with the opposite sign; (2) multiply or divide both sides by the same positive number; (3) multiply or divide by the same negative number **and reverse the sign**. The solving instruction is given in task form (collect the x-terms on the left, the numbers on the right; combine; divide by the coefficient; write the answer as an inequality or an interval). All cases are worked: a > 0 gives [b/a; +∞); a < 0 reverses (fractions cleared by the LCM: x/2 − 5x/3 > 7, ×6, gives x < −6); a = 0 with b > 0 gives 0x > b with no solutions (7(x + 1) − 4x > 3x + 16 → 0x > 9, answer ∅); a = 0 with b < 0 gives every number (6x + 17 > 2(3x + 4) → 0x > −9, answer (−∞; +∞)). Exercises include the 9-coins weighing problem (ex. 948) and letter-coded equations whose answers spell the name of the French mathematician who introduced conditional signs and letter notation (ex. 949 — Viète).
- **§5.6 Solving systems of linear inequalities in one variable (p. 60; ex. 967–986).** Lead-in (pp. 59–60): a whole-number problem (add a quarter of it — more than 10; subtract a half — less than 5; 8 < x < 10) motivates a system. The system is written with the brace; its solution is the set of values making **every** inequality true (boxed); solving means finding the **intersection** of the solution sets (boxed), in three steps. Worked: a word problem (four notebooks cost more than 200 tg but, after a 20 tg rise, less than 360 tg → (50; 70)); a system with no common solution (x ≤ 4 and x > 6 → ∅); and the double inequality 17 < 4x − 3 < 33 solved **both** as a system and directly as a double inequality → (5; 9). Systems of three inequalities occur in the exercises (ex. 975, 977). The crows-on-trees problem is ex. 985*.
- **§5.7 Solving linear inequalities with the variable inside the modulus sign (p. 67; ex. 987–1003).** Every worked example is solved **two ways** — (1) distance on the coordinate line; (2) the definition of modulus by cases. |x| ≤ 6 → [−6; 6]; |x − 2| < 4 → (−2; 6), also rewritten as the double inequality −4 < x − 2 < 4 and as a system, side by side; |x + 2| ≥ 3 → (−∞; −5] ∪ [1; +∞). Conclusions boxed: for a > 0, |x| ≤ a is equivalent to −a ≤ x ≤ a, i.e. to the system {x ≤ a; x ≥ −a}; |x| ≥ a and |x| > a give the **union** x ≥ a or x ≤ −a; for a < 0, |x| ≤ a and |x| < a have no solutions while |x| > a holds for every number; |x| ≤ 0 has the single solution x = 0 (summary examples, pp. 72–73). A summary table (p. 72) tabulates form / picture / notation for a > 0 and a < 0. Exercises include converting between double inequalities and modulus form (ex. 988) and distances between A(x) and B(3) stated as inequalities (ex. 1000).
- **Chapter V revision exercises (pp. 73–75; ex. 1004–1020).** These include fractional sign inequalities such as 4/(5x + 8) < 0 (ex. 1013) — **no general method for them is stated anywhere in the book**; they can only be attacked with the sign-of-a-quotient reasoning of §5.2.

**Chapter VI. The coordinate plane (pp. 76–114).**

- **§6.1 The plane. Intersecting lines (p. 76; ex. 1021–1035).** I. The plane through images (a lake's surface, a tabletop, window glass); the plane has no edge and is unbounded in all directions (boxed); only a part is drawn; a plane is denoted by a Greek letter or by three capital letters (ABC). II. A line has no endpoints; exactly one line passes through two points; intersecting lines have exactly **one** common point (boxed); four angles arise. Vertical angles are defined (the sides of one are extensions of the sides of the other, boxed) and their equality is established by computing all four angles from one given angle through straight angles (50° → 130°, 50°, 130°), then boxed: vertical angles are equal. Exercises run to three lines through one point and a clock problem (ex. 1033*).
- **§6.2 Perpendicular lines. Perpendicular segments (p. 80; ex. 1036–1056).** (The full § title, confirmed at the § opening on p. 80, is "Perpendicular lines. Perpendicular segments".) Definition boxed (lines meeting at a right angle); notation AB ⊥ CD; Latin *perpendicularis*; construction with set square and with protractor, both pictured; segments lying on perpendicular lines are perpendicular segments; through a given point, on or off the line, exactly **one** perpendicular can be drawn (boxed); the foot of the perpendicular; the distance from a point to a line is the length of the perpendicular segment (boxed). Exercises include true/false statements about perpendiculars (ex. 1045) and a pigeonhole proof (ex. 1048: 17 contestants, 135 points — two scored the same; blue box).
- **§6.3 Parallel lines. Parallel segments (p. 86; ex. 1057–1076).** Examples from life and technology (rails, wheel tracks, opposite edges of a notebook or table); definition boxed (two lines in one plane with no common point); Greek *parallelos*, "walking beside"; notation a ∥ b; segments on parallel lines are parallel segments; drawing parallels by sliding a set square along a ruler (steps pictured); through a point not on the line exactly **one** parallel can be drawn (boxed); two lines perpendicular to a third line are parallel (boxed); the distance between parallel lines is the length of their common perpendicular segment (worked: LN = 12 mm). Exercises include counting the parallel edges of a drawn parallelepiped and of a triangular prism (ex. 1072).
- **§6.4 The rectangular coordinate system. The coordinate plane (p. 92; ex. 1077–1102).** Motives: a classroom desk fixed by row and number; Astana at 51° N, 71° E; a body treated as a point. The system is defined (boxed: two mutually perpendicular coordinate lines with a common origin and equal unit segments) and named for Descartes (1596–1650); "coordinates" from Latin *coordinatus* (ordered); the axes Ox (axis of abscissas) and Oy (axis of ordinates); the origin from Latin *origo*; a point's coordinates are written x first, then y. I. Reading coordinates by dropping perpendiculars (task plus self-check, A(4; 3)); each point corresponds to exactly one ordered pair (boxed); points on Ox have the form (x; 0), on Oy the form (0; y). II. Plotting D(−3; 4) through the perpendiculars at (−3; 0) and (0; 4); each ordered pair corresponds to one point (boxed); the quadrants I–IV counted counter-clockwise, with the sign patterns diagrammed. Exercises include shading the sets of points defined by double inequalities (ex. 1084, 1099) and a grasshopper's jumping path (ex. 1095).
- **§6.5 Axial symmetry (p. 100; ex. 1103–1126).** A butterfly and a Kazakh ornament open the §; "symmetry" from Greek, "proportionality"; the line itself is the axis of symmetry. The symmetric point is constructed as a task with self-check (perpendicular to the axis, equal distance on the other side); the axis is the perpendicular bisector of AA₁ (boxed); a point **on** the axis is symmetric to itself (boxed); figures that coincide when the plane is folded along the line are symmetric with respect to it (boxed) and are equal to each other; a figure with an axis: an angle (its bisector's line), a rectangle (2 axes), a square (4 axes), a circle (infinitely many — any line through the centre). Symmetry with respect to Ox and Oy is practised in coordinates (ex. 1107, 1109, 1115, 1116), but the rules (x; y) → (−x; y) and (x; y) → (x; −y) are **never stated as formulas**. Two proof-type problems are boxed (ex. 1114: of 9 points symmetric with respect to each of two intersecting lines, one lies at their intersection; ex. 1121: of 5 points symmetric with respect to a line, one lies on the line).
- **§6.6 Central symmetry (p. 107; ex. 1127–1142).** Lead-in: the midpoint of AA₁ for A(−3; 0), A₁(3; 0). Definition boxed; the centre of symmetry; constructions: I. a point (the ray through O, with OA₁ = OA beyond O); II. a segment; III. a triangle. Central symmetry is identified as a rotation through 180°, so centrally symmetric figures are equal; a figure that contains the centre and the symmetric of each of its points is centrally symmetric — a segment (centre = its midpoint), a circle (centre = its centre) and a rectangle (centre = the intersection of the diagonals), each boxed separately. About the origin: A(3; 5) ↔ A₁(−3; −5), B(−5; 7) ↔ B₁(5; −7) — the coordinates of centrally symmetric points are opposite numbers (stated in the text). Exercises include an Euler–Venn two-clubs problem (ex. 1135).
- **Chapter VI revision exercises (pp. 113–114; ex. 1143–1151).**

**Chapter VII. Figures in space (pp. 115–126).**

- **§7.1 Position of figures in space. Depicting space figures. Invisible lines (p. 115; ex. 1152–1170).** Plane vs space figures; the solids named and pictured: rectangular parallelepiped (6 rectangular faces, 12 edges, 8 vertices), cube, pyramid, cylinder, ball, cone. **Five depiction rules** are listed: parallelism of corresponding segments is preserved; ratios of lengths on parallel segments are preserved; angle measures are **not** preserved; visible edges are drawn solid; invisible edges dashed. The solids are also generated by rotation: cylinder = a rectangle about a side; cone = a right triangle about a leg; ball = a semicircle about its diameter. The pyramid is built from a base polygon and an apex off its plane; the triangular pyramid is constructed by joining S to A, B, C, with faces, lateral edges and base edges named; pyramids are named by their base (triangular, quadrilateral, pentagonal, …). Exercises include two great circles on a sphere (ex. 1156), balls fitting inside a cylinder (ex. 1155), a cone made from a sector of a disc (ex. 1166), a ball inscribed in a cube, with its volume (ex. 1167), and a plane section of a cone (ex. 1161). **No new surface-area or volume formulas are given** — Grade 5 volumes are only recalled.
- **§7.2 The vector concept (p. 121; ex. 1171–1181).** Scalar quantities (mass, time, temperature, volume — given by number alone) against vector quantities (number and direction; a plane flying north-east at 700 km/h). A vector is a directed segment AB, with start A and end B (boxed); notation AB with an arrow, or one lowercase letter with an arrow; the vector's direction is the direction of the ray AB. **Nothing further**: no equal vectors, no vector coordinates, no operations on vectors. Exercises include reading a wind rose (ex. 1177), approaching and separating speeds (ex. 1178) and drawing the vector symmetric to AB with respect to Ox (ex. 1179).
- **Chapter VII revision exercises (pp. 125–126; ex. 1182–1189).** Including a snail's displacement drawn as a vector (ex. 1187) and the area of a cylinder's net (ex. 1189*).

**Chapter VIII. Statistics. Combinatorics (pp. 127–147).** The chapter introduction (p. 127) defines statistics (Latin *status*, state) as the science of numerical and qualitative change in frequently occurring situations, names the four characteristics in advance (arithmetic mean, mode, median, range), and defines combinatorics (Latin *combinare*; the term is credited to Leibniz).

- **§8.1 Arithmetic mean of several numbers (p. 127; ex. 1190–1218).** Definition boxed (the sum divided by the number of addends, written as a fraction formula); chess scores give mean 12. **Average speed is treated separately**, as a task with self-check: total distance over total time (61.3 km/h for 2 h, 73.4 for 3 h, 65.8 for 1 h → 408.6 km in 6 h → 68.1 km/h), boxed; crop yield is handled the same way (total harvest over total area, ex. 1200). Exercises include the midpoint of a segment as the mean of its ends' coordinates (ex. 1204, 1208), the price of a mixture (ex. 1212*) and an ICT task on the average speed of a flight to the Moon (ex. 1218*).
- **§8.2 Range, median and mode of a given number series (p. 133; ex. 1219–1239).** I. Range = greatest value minus least value (boxed; a March week of daily mean temperatures: 8 °C − 3 °C = 5 °C). II. Median: order the series; with an odd count the median is the middle number; with an even count, the arithmetic mean of the two middle numbers (both boxed); reading speeds worked in a table (median 80 words a minute); figure-skating scores give the even case (5.4 and 5.5 → 5.45). III. Mode = the most frequent value (boxed); a series may have **no** mode (worked example: fish masses all occurring once) and the text notes that more than one mode is possible; a gloss ties mode to fashion and to demand. Exercises include completing a worker's output table and then finding its median (ex. 1237).
- **§8.3 Solving combinatorics problems by the enumeration method (p. 141; ex. 1240–1266).** Lead-in (p. 140): choosing a toy bear and a ball from shelf colours, with a tree. The method: list **all** variants systematically (a variant must satisfy the condition). Example 1: the duty order of three pupils (Birzhan, Samal, Miras) — 6 variants, found by a "tree of possible variants" whose root is marked *; objects are coded by first letters, and a table is offered as an alternative record. Example 2: the segments joining 5 points on a line, listed alphabetically — AB, AC, AD, AE, BC, BD, BE, CD, CE, DE = 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 10 — with its tree. **No multiplication or counting rule is stated anywhere in the chapter** — even the routes problem with 3 roads followed by 4 roads (ex. 1262) is left to enumeration. Exercises include handshake counts, the worst case for trying keys in 4 rooms (ex. 1261: 6 tries) and pairwise fish weighings (ex. 1265*).
- **Chapter VIII revision exercises (pp. 146–147; ex. 1267–1280).**

**Chapter IX. Dependencies between quantities (pp. 148–177).**

- **§9.1 Dependencies between quantities. Giving a dependency by formula (p. 148; ex. 1281–1295).** I. s = vt with v = 14 fixed and t = 2, 3, 4. Quantities whose values change are **variable quantities** (boxed); t is the independent variable, s the dependent one. The correspondence is stated in set language (boxed): to **each** permissible value of the independent variable (an element of set A) there corresponds **exactly one** value of the dependent variable (from set B) — the function idea, but **the word "function" is never defined or used in the theory of the book**; it surfaces once, undefined, in ex. 1348 ("from the functions given by formulas, pick out the direct proportionalities"). A dependency may be given by a formula, by a table or by a graph. II. Giving by formula: P = 4a worked. Exercises include a simple-interest formula in n months (ex. 1292: 7,000,000 tg at 3% a month, simple growth) and an ICT task on the Moon's circumference (ex. 1295*).
- **§9.2 Giving dependencies by table (p. 152; ex. 1296–1309).** The independent variable goes in the first row, the dependent in the second; the **step** of a table is defined (the difference of neighbouring independent values); only tabulated values may be taken from a table. Worked: water flow V = 1.5t. Exercises recover formulas from tables (wood: m = 0.7V), complete tables (a runner's remaining distance; two cars' closing distance), and read a boat's upriver table with missing entries.
- **Historical note: "On Descartes' variable quantities" (p. 156).** Seventeenth–eighteenth-century science needed variables; Descartes (1596–1650) and Fermat (1601–1665) first brought the variable quantity into mathematics (Descartes' *Geometry*, 1637); Newton (1643–1727) and Leibniz (1646–1716) continued.
- **§9.3 Representing a dependency by graph (p. 157; ex. 1310–1341).** Lead-in: a loom's output graph (4 m an hour, the line AB). The graph is defined (boxed) as the set of points whose abscissas are the values of the independent quantity and whose ordinates are the corresponding values of the dependent one. Example 1: pine-wood mass against volume — table, then the points A(1; 0.5), …, E(5; 2.5), joined by segments into a straight graph, read in both directions. Example 2: a sunflower's growth **curve** over six weeks — the height at 4 weeks (70 cm) is found by the perpendicular construction. Thermograph, seismograph and cardiogram are named as graph-recording instruments. Exercises include the fixed-distance hyperbola t = 240/v (ex. 1314), S = a² by table and graph (ex. 1315), and two-graph motion problems.
- **§9.4 Studying dependencies using graphs of real processes (p. 164; ex. 1328–1341, shared with §9.3's tail).** Example 1: wind speed through a day, read off (greatest 5 m/s; constant 5 m/s from 14 to 20 h; calm from 6 to 8 h and from 22 to 24 h). Example 2: a daily temperature curve (0 °C at 8 h and at 20 h; highest 3 °C, lowest −2.5 °C; rising from 2 to 14 h; the negative stretches). Exercises: a child's height against age; a **discrete** point graph for fabric used against shirts sewn (fig. 9.19); heating water from a table to a graph; air pressure against altitude (Khan Tengri, Talgar); a traveller with a rest; a boat's round trip.
- **§9.5 Direct proportionality and its graph (p. 170; ex. 1342–1358).** I. Here, at last, direct proportionality is **defined by the formula**: y = kx with k ≠ 0 is a direct proportionality and k is the **coefficient of proportionality** (boxed) — the named coefficient that Ch. I did without. Domains are stated per example (s = 70t, t > 0; C = 35n, n ∈ N; P = 4a, a > 0); y₁/x₁ = y₂/x₂ = k, so the pairs form a proportion; the domain of y = kx itself is all rational numbers. II. The graph of y = 2x is built from a step-1 table (x = −4…4): the points lie on a line through the origin (boxed conclusion); two points suffice — the origin and one more (y = −3x/4 is drawn through (8; −6)); for k > 0 the line lies in quadrants I and III and makes an acute angle with the positive direction of Ox, for k < 0 in quadrants II and IV with an obtuse angle; k is called the line's **angular coefficient**. Exercises include reading k off graphs and deciding which formulas are direct proportionalities (ex. 1348).
- **Chapter IX revision exercises (pp. 176–177; ex. 1359–1364)** — reading the S = a² curve; a day's temperatures from a table; finding k from a point on the graph of y = kx; a taps-and-tank proportionality problem. **Contents slip:** the Pt 2 contents (p. 223) lists these exercises at p. 184; p. 184 is §10.2.

**Chapter X. Linear equations in two variables and their systems (pp. 178–208).**

- **§10.1 A linear equation in two variables (p. 178; ex. 1365–1389).** From a verbal condition (the sum of two numbers is three times their difference) the equation x + y = 3(x − y) is built and simplified to −2x + 4y = 0. The general form ax + by + c = 0 is boxed; a and b are the coefficients, c the free term; equivalent equations (the same solution pairs); **two** properties (carrying a term across changes its sign; both sides may be multiplied or divided by a non-zero number). Solving means expressing one variable through the other: 6x + 5y − 10 = 0 → y = −1.2x + 2 → the pairs (1; 0.8), (2; −0.4), (3; −1.6). A solution is a pair (x; y) making a true numerical equality (boxed), x written first; in general there are infinitely many. Applied example: 30 matchsticks are made into triangles (y) and squares (x): 3y + 4x = 30 → y = 10 − 4x/3, whose whole-number solutions are only (3; 6) and (6; 2). A lead-in on p. 177 works 3x + 2y − 8 = 0 the same way. Exercises include finding an unknown coefficient from a known solution pair (ex. 1384–1385) and the rabbits-and-chickens count, which has 4 solutions (ex. 1381).
- **§10.2 The graph of a linear equation in two variables (additional reading) (p. 184; ex. 1390–1406).** The graph is defined (boxed) as the set of points whose coordinates are solutions. I. a ≠ 0, b ≠ 0: for x − 2y − 4 = 0 four solutions are plotted and lie on one line; conclusion: the graph of ax + by + c = 0 is a straight line. Two constructions: (1) by the two axis intercepts (5x − 4y + 20 = 0 → (0; 5), (−4; 0)); (2) express y through x and take any two points. II. b = 0: 4x − 8 = 0 → x = 2, a vertical line through (2; 0). III. a = 0: 3y − 9 = 0 → y = 3, a horizontal line. Boxed: if at least one of a, b is non-zero, the graph is a straight line. IV. a = b = 0, c ≠ 0: no solutions. V. a = b = c = 0: every point of the plane is a solution. Exercises include writing the equations of pictured lines (ex. 1399, 1405) and ex. 1404 — draw both graphs and read off the intersection point; the graphical idea is thus exercised, but it is **never named as a method for solving systems**. (Contrast the Mektep book, whose corresponding chapter draws no graph of a two-variable equation at all.)
- **§10.3 Linear equations in two variables and their systems (p. 189; ex. 1407–1423).** From the rectangle problem (x − y = 4; 2(x + y) = 36) the two equations are united by the brace, because the x of one is the x of the other; the pair (11; 7) is verified in both. The solution of a system is a pair making **every** equation true (boxed); equivalent systems are named; the general form is written with subscripts: a₁x + b₁y + c₁ = 0, a₂x + b₂y + c₂ = 0. **There is no graphical method here, and the theory says nothing about systems with no solution or with infinitely many.** Exercises are mostly "is this pair a solution?", plus finding c₁, c₂ (ex. 1417) and a, b (ex. 1422) from a given solution pair.
- **§10.4 Solving systems of linear equations in two variables by substitution (p. 193; ex. 1424–1459).** A lead-in on p. 193 solves x − y = 29, 2x + 7y = 112 by substitution in three steps (→ (35; 6)) before the § title. Worked: a two-number word problem (x − y = 7, 3x − 2y = 27 → (13; 6)); a fractional system first cleared by the LCMs 15 and 21, then solved by substitution (→ (6; 4)); a four-step algorithm (pp. 194–195); the method is noted as convenient when some variable has coefficient 1 (or −1) in one equation. Exercises include systems in 1/x and 1/y (ex. 1451), left to substitution.
- **§10.5 Solving systems of linear equations in two variables by addition (p. 201; ex. 1460–1499).** Lead-in (p. 201): x + y = 21, x − y = 9 added termwise (→ (15; 6), with a check). **Case I** — the coefficients of one variable are opposite numbers: add at once (3x − 2y = 6, 6x + 2y = 30 → 9x = 36 → (4; 3)); the replacement of the system by an equivalent one is shown explicitly. **Case II** — the coefficients are equal: multiply one equation by −1 and add, or subtract termwise (→ (3; 2)). **Case III** — neither: multiply the equations by chosen factors to create opposite coefficients (×7 and ×(−2) → (5; 2), with a check). A four-step algorithm follows. Degenerate systems appear **only** as ex. 1464 ("does the system have a solution, and if so, how many?": 2x − 5y = 1 with 6x − 15y = 3 — dependent; 5x − 3y = −3 with −5x + 3y = 8 — inconsistent) and are never theorised.

**Closing revision blocks.**

- **I. Exercises revising the Grade 5 topics (pp. 209–211; ex. 1500–1519).** Divisibility, GCD and LCM (including a parallelepiped's volume and dimensions, and numbers built from the digits 2, 3, 5, 7 divisible by 2, 3 or 5); common and decimal fractions and operations on them; percent (a bus speeding up by 20%, the zinc in an alloy, a rectangle's area change, a worker at 150% of the norm, diluting a salt solution, ex. 1517*; iron ore, ex. 1518*; dried apples).
- **II. Yearly revision exercises for the Grade 6 topics (pp. 212–220; ex. 1520–1590)**, arranged by topic: ratio and proportion (ex. 1520–1531 — brass in the ratio 3 : 2, seed germination, fraction ratios rewritten as ratios of integers, proportions, inverse workers-and-days, sound and lightning, wallpaper, map scale — ex. 1531's answer gives the scale 1 : 8,000,000); rational numbers and operations on them (ex. 1532–1548); algebraic expressions (ex. 1549–1558); linear equations in one variable (ex. 1559–1567, including equations in x|x|: 2x|x| = 6x; 3x|x| = −15x; |x| = −3|x|, ex. 1567); linear inequalities and their systems (ex. 1568–1574); the coordinate plane (ex. 1575–1578); statistics and combinatorics (ex. 1579–1587); linear equations in two variables and their systems (ex. 1588–1590 — drawing the graphs of y = −3x + 2, y = 3x/4, y = 1.5x + 4, y = −x − 5, y = 2x, y = 2x + 3 as graphs of equations). Ex. 1590 is the last numbered exercise of the book.

Part 2 closes with the subject index (p. 221), contents (pp. 222–223) and colophon (p. 224).

**Defects, slips and honest negatives.**

- *Contents errors (KZ edition, verified against the interior).* Pt 1 contents (p. 206) places the Chapter I historical information at p. 62; it stands at pp. 68–69. Pt 2 contents (p. 223) places the Chapter IX revision exercises at p. 184; they stand at pp. 176–177. The same Pt 2 contents page heads the last yearly-revision block "Екі айнымалысы бар сызықтық **теңсіздіктер** және олардың жүйелері" (inequalities) where the block itself (p. 220) is headed "…**теңдеулер**…" (equations).
- *A forward reference.* The §2.2 topic summary (Pt 1, p. 81) defines opposite numbers by equal moduli and writes |6| = |−6| two sections before modulus is introduced in §2.4.
- *Taught by statement, not by derivation.* The disc area S = πR² (§1.9) is asserted as a result of mathematical research. Vertical angles (§6.1) are "proved" only by computing one numeric case. The compound-percent formula Sₙ = S(1 + P/100)ⁿ occurs once, inside an additional-reading problem set (§2.16), and is not taken up again.
- *Set but not taught.* Fractional sign inequalities (Pt 2, ex. 1013) are set in the Chapter V revision with no method stated anywhere; the degenerate cases of two-variable systems (none / infinitely many solutions) are set in ex. 1464 but never theorised; factoring out a common factor is used from §3.3's lead-in onwards but is never given a rule of its own.
- *Named gaps.* No y = kx and no proportionality coefficient in Chapter I (both arrive in §9.5); no distributive property among the multiplication properties of §§2.13–2.14 (it enters through §3.2); the word "function" is never defined and f(x) notation never appears — dependencies are carried by "independent/dependent variable" language throughout Ch. IX, and lines y = kx + b appear only as graphs of two-variable equations (§10.2 and the revision), never as functions; no graphical method for systems; no counting rules in combinatorics (enumeration and trees only) and no probability beyond naming certain/random/impossible dice events (ex. 837); vectors (§7.2) are a concept only — no equality, coordinates or operations; symmetry about the axes is practised in coordinates but the sign rules are never stated.
- *Glyphs in the held scans.* The book's compact ≥ / ≤ sort prints in these scans in a form easily misread (it appears variously like a turned sign, "J" or "I" — e.g. Pt 2 ex. 887, 914, 952, 960), and the ∞ sign in the answer boxes prints like "+u" (e.g. Pt 2, the answers to ex. 951, 995). These are rendering features of the held copies, noted so that citations are not misread from them.
- *Editions divergence (wording).* In §5.5 the KZ text defines the solution of an inequality as the **set** of values making it true; the RU text defines it as **a value** of the variable that makes it true. The mathematics is used identically afterwards.

**Russian-edition spot-check (both parts).** The RU title pages, imprints and full contents of both parts were read, and interiors were spot-checked at Pt 1 §§1.3–1.4 (percent ratio; the proportion task with 24 : 6 = 60 : 15), §2.3 (the sign-rules table; ex. 262–278 including the letter-coded ex. 273 and the family problem ex. 276) and §3.5 (ex. 757–762 and their answer box), and at Pt 2 §4.1 (the five properties and the transposition corollary), §5.5 (the three equivalence rules and the same worked examples: 5x − 1 ≥ 3x + 7 → [4; +∞); x/2 − 5x/3 > 7 → x < −6; 0x > 9 → ∅; 0x > −9 → all numbers; ex. 938–949) and §10.5 (the same three cases and systems; ex. 1460–1464). At every check the section numbers, worked examples, exercise numbers and printed answers are **identical** to the Kazakh edition; the differences found are only those recorded above — pagination drift (+1…+4 in Pt 1, ±1 in Pt 2), the Glossary in place of the subject index, the summary heading «Ключевые факты» for "Тақырыптың түйіні", the print runs (30,000 against 50,000), the named RU reviewers, and the §5.5 wording difference. On that basis the content is filed once, from the Kazakh edition, for both.

### WORK 4 — Abylkasymova, Kucher, Zhumagulova, *Mathematics*, Grade 6 (Mektep, 2018)

**As printed (Kazakh edition):** А. Е. Әбілқасымова, Т. П. Кучер, З. Ә. Жұмағұлова,
*Математика*, textbook for Grade 6, in 2 parts, Almaty: Mektep, 2018. Each part a
stated 184 pp. Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-07-0978-2, Pt 2 ISBN 978-601-07-0980-5, set
978-601-07-0979-9; print run 75,000. Russian editions: Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-07-0983-6,
Pt 2 978-601-07-0985-0, set 978-601-07-0984-3, run 50,000. Uyghur (translator
K. Mansurov): Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-07-1044-3, set 978-601-07-1045-0, run 2,500.
Uzbek (translator V. Musaeva): Pt 1 ISBN 978-601-07-1049-8, Pt 2 978-601-07-1051-1,
set 978-601-07-1050-4, runs 2,500/7,000. Held files: 182, 183 (KZ); 209, 210 (RU);
1375, 1445 (Uyghur); 1450, 1380 (Uzbek). The contents pages are printed at the END
of each part (Pt 1: p. 183). In the Kazakh scans PDF p. = printed p. throughout
(verified in file 182: cover = printed p. 1, §1 at PDF 18 = printed 18, contents
at PDF 183 = printed 183, QR/colophon leaf at PDF 184 = printed 184; the 185th
PDF page of files 182 and 183 is a blank leaf). Design differs from the same authors'
Grade 5 book: each § opens with a "Key words" strip and is developed through
guiding questions; exercise tiers A/B/C plus interspersed **Қ (revision) blocks**
that recycle earlier chapters; each part ends with a Kazakh glossary and answers.
Exercise numbering is continuous across both parts (Pt 1: 1–797; Pt 2 continues).

#### Part 1

**Revision of the Grade 5 course (pp. 4–17, ex. 1–89):** operations with common
and decimal fractions, percent, rounding, equations, coordinates on a ray, areas
and volumes — set as exercises only, many encoding Kazakh facts (independence
dates, Baikonur launches) as answers.

**Chapter 1 — Ratio and proportion (pp. 18–58).**
- §1 Ratio of two numbers; percent ratio (p. 18): ratio = quotient; "how many
  times larger/smaller" and "what part of"; the inverse ratio; multiplying/
  dividing both terms by the same number leaves the ratio unchanged; percent
  ratio: divide, multiply by 100, attach % (150 : 200 = 75%); percent change
  problems (yield rose 80 → 150 c/ha = by 87,5%).
- §2 Proportion; the main property (p. 24): extremes and means named on the
  diagram; the property ad = bc **derived** by bringing a/b and c/d to the common
  denominator bd; testing whether an equality is a proportion; finding an unknown
  term; producing three new proportions from one by swapping means/extremes;
  proportion equations with the unknown inside brackets.
- §3 Directly and inversely proportional quantities (p. 32): y = kx with the
  (direct) proportionality coefficient k; y = k/x for inverse; deciding from
  tables and from situations which kind (and which neither); solving by
  proportion — direct: equal ratios; inverse: flipped ratio (4 bulldozers clear a
  site in 9 h → 6 bulldozers in 6 h, worked both by the "how many times" method
  and by 9 : x = 9/6 equality of products).
- §4 Solving text problems with proportion (p. 39): percent problems three ways
  (×0,04; via 1%; via the proportion x/700 = 4/100); **dividing a quantity in a
  given ratio** with a printed algorithm (parts total a + b; each part is
  x : (a+b) · a etc.); dividing into parts *inversely* proportional to given
  numbers (replace by their reciprocals, then divide directly); pie-chart sector
  angles computed as a ratio-division problem (40 : 8 : 32 = 5 : 1 : 4 →
  180°/36°/144°).
- §5 Scale (p. 49): scale as a ratio; notation M 1 : 100 000 = M 1/100000;
  enlargement scales (10 : 1 for watch parts); the three standard problem types
  (map distance ↔ real distance ↔ scale) with unit conversion cm ↔ km.
- §6 Circumference; area of a disc; ball and sphere (p. 54): circumference found
  by a string-measuring practical — C/d is the same (≈ 3) for every circle; π
  named as an infinite decimal, π ≈ 3,14 used; C = 2πR obtained from C/(2R) = π;
  disc area S = πR² stated and used (not derived); a ball's surface is a sphere,
  radius of a sphere; C-tier: Eratosthenes' Earth-radius problem, annulus and
  composite-shape areas.

**Chapter 2 — Rational numbers and operations on them (pp. 60–148).**
- §7 Positive and negative numbers; the coordinate line (p. 60): subtraction
  0 − 1 motivates extending the ray to a line; origin, unit segment, direction;
  positive = right of 0 (the + sign usually omitted), negative = left; 0 is
  neither; A(x) notation; negatives in measurement (thermometer, altitude/depth);
  Descartes report box.
- §8 Opposite numbers; integers; rational numbers (p. 67): opposites as points
  symmetric about 0; −a notation and −(−a) = a; integers = naturals + opposites
  + 0 (set Z), even/odd integers; rationals (set Q) = integers + positive and
  negative fractions; Venn nesting N ⊂ Z ⊂ Q; solving −x = 432 type equations.
- §9 Modulus of a number (p. 74): |a| as the distance from 0; |positive| = itself,
  |negative| = its opposite, |0| = 0; modulus is never negative; |x| = a equations
  (two roots, one, none discussed through tasks); nested-modulus identities in
  the C tier.
- §10 Comparing rational numbers (p. 79): by position on the line; any positive >
  any negative; of two negatives the one with the **larger modulus is smaller**;
  a > 0, a < 0 notation; "not positive" (a ≤ 0) and "not negative" (a ≥ 0) with
  their readings; values of a variable satisfying an inequality; integer
  solutions of |x| < n type tasks in the C tier.
- §11 Adding rationals on the coordinate line (p. 85): adding = shifting right
  (positive) or left (negative); temperature-change model; a + (−a) = 0;
  a + 0 = a.
- §12 Adding negative rationals (p. 91): from the line, (−2) + (−4) = −6; rule:
  add the moduli, put "−" in front.
- §13 Adding rationals with different signs (p. 96): from the line examples;
  rule: subtract the smaller modulus from the larger, take the sign of the
  larger-modulus addend.
- §14 Properties of addition of rationals (p. 102): commutativity and
  associativity stated for all rationals and used to group conveniently
  (positives together, negatives together, opposites cancelling to 0).
- §15 Subtracting rationals (p. 106): a − b = a + (−b) from the line (3 − 8 =
  3 + (−8) = −5); the sign of a difference (minuend > subtrahend → positive…).
- §16 Adding and subtracting rationals (algebraic sums) (p. 113): rewrite the
  whole expression as a sum, group positive and negative terms separately — the
  book's standard technique for long expressions.
- §17 Distance between points on the coordinate line (p. 120): distance =
  |a − b| (M(7), K(3) → 4 units; |−8 − (−5)| = |−3| = 3).
- §18 Multiplying rationals (p. 123): negative × positive as repeated addition
  ((−4)·3 = −12), commutativity then gives positive × negative; rule for unlike
  signs (multiply moduli, minus in front); a · (−1) = −a; **two negatives derived
  formally**: −3 · (−4) = −1 · (3 · (−4)) = −1 · (−12) = 12 — i.e. multiply the
  moduli and the product is positive; zero-factor property, used at once to solve
  equations like (−5)(x + 42) = 0 and (−21 + 7x)(18 − 9x) = 0 (each factor = 0).
- §19 Commutative and associative properties of multiplication (p. 131): stated
  for rationals; the sign of a long product: an odd number of negative factors →
  negative product, even → positive.
- §20 Dividing rationals (p. 135): quotient defined through multiplication
  (a : b = c ⇔ a = b·c); sign rules mirror multiplication; |quotient| = quotient
  of moduli; −a/b = a/(−b) = −(a/b); 0 : a = 0 (a ≠ 0). **Periodic decimals are
  fully developed inside this §:** dividing 28 by 9 gives 3,111… = 3,(1); the
  period, pure vs mixed periodic decimals, reading them aloud; a finite decimal
  is a periodic decimal with period 0 (5,27 = 5,27(0)); **converting a periodic
  decimal to a common fraction** — pure: numerator = the period, denominator =
  as many 9s as the period has digits (0,(35) = 35/99; 4,(378) = 4 378/999);
  mixed: the (second-period-minus-first) rule (0,15(132) = (15132 − 15)/99900 =
  5039/33300).
- §21 Applying arithmetic operations to rationals (p. 144): long mixed
  expressions — first simplify (rewrite as a sum / use the laws), then follow the
  order of operations; evaluating letter expressions at negative values.

**Chapter 3 — Algebraic expressions (pp. 150–176).**
- §22 The variable (p. 150): variable vs its value; an expression with variables;
  in an equation/inequality the letter is also a variable; **permissible and
  impermissible values** (a value that makes a denominator 0 is impermissible;
  (−3x + 14) : (10y − 6) requires y ≠ 0,6); values of a variable that make an
  equation true / an inequality true; combined double inequalities in exercises.
- §23 The distributive property; expanding brackets (p. 156): c(a + b) = ca + cb
  verified numerically with negative rationals; expanding with a "−" before the
  bracket (all signs flip) and a "+" (signs kept); −1 · (bracket) written as just
  "−(bracket)"; **factoring out a common factor** as the reverse move, including
  fractional common factors (−3/7).
- §24 Coefficient; like terms; combining like terms (p. 162): coefficient = the
  numerical factor (1 written implicitly; −1 shown as "−"); like terms = same
  letter part; combining = add the coefficients and keep the letter part
  (worked by factoring out, e.g. 4,6m − 18,3m + 13,3m − 7,6m = −8m).
- §25 Identity; identical transformations (p. 168): identically equal expressions
  (equal at every permissible value, checked first on a value table); an identity
  as an equality true for all values; the arithmetic laws restated as identities;
  proving an identity = transforming one side (or both) into the other; the trap
  −(a + b) vs −a + b is NOT an identity; "the value does not depend on the
  variable" proof tasks in the C tier.
- §26 Solving text problems (p. 175): compose an algebraic expression from a
  story (a + b, a − b, a·b, a : b…) and a story from an expression; V = 5ac for a
  box with edges 5, a, c; perimeter answers left in letter form ((10 + 6b) cm).

Glossary (pp. 177–178), Answers (pp. 179–183), Contents (p. 183), QR page (p. 184).

**Honest negatives, Pt 1:** functions/graphs not yet (y = kx appears only as a
formula in §3, no graphs drawn); no statistics; geometry only §6. Division by zero
is handled only through permissible values in §22, not as a separate discussion in
§20. **Defects:** goals-box mojibake of the Grade 5 scans does NOT occur here
(the G6 design uses plain key-word strips, which render cleanly); the ≤/≥ signs
print in a compact glyph that is legible but unusual. Pt 1 KZ scan (182) complete.
Uzbek Pt 1 (1450) has 182 PDF pages against the stated 184 pp: its last pages run
answers pp. 181–182 and the contents/colophon leaves are absent from the scan —
the textbook body is complete through the answers; front matter present.
Uyghur Pt 1 (1375): an extra answers leaf is bound after the colophon page in the
scan (page order anomaly at the end); content complete.


#### Part 2

**Chapter 4 — The linear equation in one variable (pp. 4–33).**
- §27 Numerical equalities and their properties (p. 4): a true numerical equality
  stays true if the same number is added to / subtracted from both sides, or both
  sides are multiplied / divided by the same non-zero number; termwise addition
  and multiplication of true equalities (the five properties, including the two
  "termwise" ones, numbered 4 and 5 in the exercises); "put brackets so the
  equality is true" and missing-number tasks.
- §28 Solving equations (p. 9): solving by **transposing terms with the opposite
  sign** — first shown to be exactly the unknown-component rules in disguise
  (x + a = b; m + x = n; x − c = d; k − x = a) — then the boxed algorithm:
  simplify both sides (expand brackets, combine like terms) → move variable terms
  to one side and number terms to the other → combine → find the root → **check
  by substitution** (the worked example ends 3,6 = 3,6). Equations in proportion
  form included.
- §29 The linear equation in one variable (p. 15): standard form ax = b;
  a ≠ 0 → one root x = b/a; a = 0, b ≠ 0 → no roots; a = 0, b = 0 → every number
  is a root; **equivalent equations** (same roots, or both rootless); the three
  equivalence rules (transpose with sign change; add the same number; multiply/
  divide by the same non-zero number); solution sets written in braces, {7; −7},
  and ∅ for the empty set.
- §30 Linear equations with the variable inside a modulus (p. 23): |x| = 7 solved
  by the two cases x ≥ 0 / x < 0 using |x| = x or −x; composite equations reduced
  to |x| = k (e.g. −|x| + 12|x| − 24,3 = 5,7 − 4|x| → |x| = 2 → {−2; 2}).
- §31 Solving text problems by equations (p. 27): name the unknown x, express the
  other quantity, equate (two lightning flashes: x + (x + 140) = 158; river
  current: (16 + x) − (16 − x) = 6); standard families in the exercises — ages,
  shelves/boxes equalising after transfer, motion, consecutive integers (in C).

**Chapter 5 — Linear inequalities in one variable and their systems (pp. 34–83).**
- §32 Numerical inequalities and their properties (p. 34): true/false numerical
  inequalities; strict vs non-strict; double inequalities; opposite signs
  < ↔ >, ≤ ↔ ≥; **six properties**, each discovered on numerical examples and
  several left to "prove it yourself": (1) add/subtract the same number —
  including transposing a term with the opposite sign; (2) multiply/divide by a
  positive number (sign kept) or by a negative number (**sign reverses**) — the
  division rule derived by replacing ÷m with ×(1/m); (3) transitivity;
  (4) termwise addition of inequalities of the same sense; (5) termwise
  multiplication (positive sides); (6) termwise division (a > b > 0, c > d > 0 →
  a/d > b/c).
- §33 Number intervals (p. 42): the solution set of x > −2 as the interval
  (−∞; +∞)… precisely: whole line (−∞; +∞); closed ray [1; +∞); open ray
  (1; +∞) and (−∞; 1); segment [−3; 1]; open interval (−3; 1); half-intervals
  [−3; 1) and (−3; 1]; shading on the line, filled dot = endpoint included, small
  empty circle = excluded; every notation is given a spoken reading.
- §34 Union and intersection of number intervals (p. 50): draw both on one line;
  [−4; 1] ∪ [1; 2] = [−4; 2]; a one-point intersection written {1} using ∩;
  disjoint intervals → ∩ = ∅.
- §35 The linear inequality in one variable (p. 55): solution of an inequality;
  equivalent inequalities and the four equivalence rules (parallel to equations,
  with the sign-reversal rule as the fourth).
- §36 Solving linear inequalities (p. 59): reduce to kx > b / kx ≥ b / kx < b /
  kx ≤ b by equivalence transformations; answers as intervals, drawn on the line;
  the degenerate cases 0·x < −8 (no solutions, ∅) and 0·x < 8 (all numbers,
  (−∞; +∞)) are worked explicitly; "smallest natural / largest integer solution"
  tasks.
- §37 Solving systems of linear inequalities (p. 65): the brace notation; a
  solution must satisfy every inequality at once; solve each separately and take
  the **intersection** on one diagram (worked example ends in the half-interval
  [3 5/9; 4,25]); systems of three inequalities appear in the C tier (ex. 1039,
  whose answers encode the life dates of al-Farabi, Dulati, Qurmangazy,
  Altynsarin, Brusilovsky).
- §38 Linear inequalities with the variable inside a modulus (p. 72): |x| as
  distance from 0; |x| ≤ b (b > 0) ⇔ −b ≤ x ≤ b ⇔ the system {x ≤ b; x ≥ −b};
  |x| ≥ a ⇔ the **union** (−∞; −a] ∪ [a; +∞); the case analysis
  |x| ≤ 0 → x = 0; |x| < negative → no solutions; converting freely between
  modulus form, double inequality, system and interval.
- §39 Solving linear inequalities with modulus (p. 78): |7 − x| ≤ 8 solved two
  ways (as a system; as the double inequality −8 ≤ 7 − x ≤ 8, subtracting 7 then
  dividing by −1 with sign reversal) → [−1; 15]; |3x − 4| > 9 as a union;
  mixed systems containing modulus inequalities in B/C tiers.

**Chapter 6 — The coordinate plane (pp. 84–105).**
- §40 The plane; perpendicular lines and segments (p. 84): a plane is unbounded;
  lines in a plane either meet or not; intersecting lines; perpendicular lines
  forming right angles, notation AB ⊥ CD; constructing perpendiculars with the
  set square and with the protractor; perpendicular segments (segments lying on
  perpendicular lines); distance from a point to a line in the exercises.
- §41 Parallel lines and segments (p. 89): lines in one plane that never meet;
  notation a ∥ b; parallel segments; drawing parallels with ruler and set square
  in the exercises.
- §42 The coordinate plane; rectangular coordinate system (p. 92): abscissa axis
  (Ox) and ordinate axis (Oy), origin, the ordered pair M(−2; 3) read by dropping
  perpendiculars; the four quadrants with their sign patterns; a point on an axis
  has the other coordinate 0; plotting/connecting points into polygons, finding
  unknown vertices, intersection points of segments with axes.
- §43 Central and axial symmetry (p. 99): midpoint of a segment; points A and B
  centrally symmetric about O when O is the midpoint of AB; constructing the
  symmetric point/segment/triangle step-by-step on grid paper; centrally symmetric
  figures (segment, square, circle); axial symmetry: AB ⊥ l and AO = OB; the axis
  of symmetry; figures with axes (isosceles triangle, rectangle, square); Kazakh
  ornament as a symmetry example.

**Chapter 7 — Figures in space (pp. 106–111).**
- §44 Position of figures in space; depicting solids (p. 106): visible edges
  solid, hidden edges dashed; cube and cuboid; pyramid; cylinder (top view an
  ellipse-like oval in the book's drawings); a ball drawn as a circle with its
  centre; counting visible/invisible faces and edges from drawings; judging
  correct/incorrect cuboid drawings.
- §45 The vector concept (p. 109): quantities with direction (speed) vs scalars;
  a vector as a directed segment with start and end; notation a⃗, AB⃗; the zero
  vector 0⃗ (start = end, e.g. PP⃗); plotting vectors between coordinate points.
  **No vector arithmetic** — concept and notation only. Hamilton report box.

**Chapter 8 — Statistics. Combinatorics (pp. 112–123).**
- §46 Statistical data and their characteristics (p. 112): a data series;
  **arithmetic mean** (sum ÷ count, with the two-step rule boxed); largest and
  smallest values of a series; **range** (largest − smallest); **mode** — the most
  frequent value, which may not exist or may be several; **median** — order the
  series; odd count → the middle value; even count → the mean of the two middle
  values (worked: camel counts 2013–2017 → median 165,9; grain areas → 2988,35).
- §47 Average-speed problems; combinatorics problems (p. 118): average speed =
  total distance ÷ total time, v = (s₁ + s₂)/(t₁ + t₂) and for three legs —
  explicitly NOT the mean of the speeds (round trip 90 km/h out, 60 back → 72);
  **combinatorial problems solved by the enumeration (option-tree) method** —
  a branching diagram ("tree of possible variants") built digit by digit (numbers
  from digits 3, 5, 7, 9 ending in 5 → six numbers listed); route counting
  (3 roads A→B and 3 roads B→C) set as a follow-up question. No multiplication
  rule is stated as a formula; counting is by drawing out the tree.

**Chapter 9 — Dependencies between quantities (pp. 124–143).**
- §48 Ways of giving a dependency (p. 124): dependent variable (usually y) vs
  independent variable (x); the **analytic** way (a formula, e.g. y = 3x from
  s = vt at v = 3), the **table** way, and the **graph** way — a dependency's
  graph = the set of plane points whose abscissas are x-values and ordinates the
  matching y-values; reading a value from a graph by perpendiculars; a graph may
  be finitely many points, several points, or a curve; graph → table conversion.
- §49 Studying dependencies from graphs of real processes (p. 131): reading
  temperature-over-two-days curves (max/min, ranges of increase/decrease, mean
  over an interval), a heating/melting graph of a substance (ice–water plateaus),
  motion graphs with stops and returns; pupils compose their own questions from
  a graph.
- §50 Direct proportionality and its graph (p. 136): y = kx (k ≠ 0) recalled;
  the table of y = 3x plotted — all points lie on one line through the origin,
  and since x may be any number the graph is the whole line; **to draw it, the
  origin plus one point suffice** (y = 0,5x through A(2; 1)); graphs for several k
  shown fanning through O; reading the s = 50x motion graph in both directions.

**Chapter 10 — Linear equations in two variables and their systems (pp. 144–167).**
- §51 The linear equation in two variables (p. 144): ax + by + c = 0; its solution
  is an **ordered pair** (x; y) making a true numerical equality; with b ≠ 0 there
  are infinitely many solutions (choose x freely, compute y); equivalent
  two-variable equations (transpose terms; multiply/divide both sides by a
  non-zero number); expressing one variable through the other.
  **Notable: this book does NOT draw the graph of a two-variable equation** —
  no graphical method for systems anywhere in Ch. 10 (contrast Work 3).
- §52 Systems of linear equations in two variables (p. 149): the brace notation;
  a solution of a system is a pair satisfying both equations simultaneously;
  equivalent systems (same solution set, or both unsolvable).
- §53 Solving systems by the addition (elimination) method (p. 152): motivated by
  true-equality properties — add the equations memberwise when one variable
  cancels; if equal terms (8x) appear, subtract (addition of the opposite);
  if neither cancels, multiply one or both equations first (×3, ×(−4)); the
  five-step algorithm is boxed; back-substitution for the second variable.
  Exercises include systems with fraction/power coefficients and "prove the
  system has no solution / has infinitely many solutions" tasks (ex. 1250–1260).
- §54 Solving systems by the substitution method (p. 158): express one variable
  from one equation (x = 7 − 5y), substitute into the other, solve the
  one-variable equation (−7y + 42 = 0 → y = 6, x = −23); systems already given in
  "y = …" form first.
- §55 Solving text problems by systems of equations (p. 163): sum/difference of
  two numbers (x + y = 95, x − y = 19); two purchases (3 pencils + 5 notebooks =
  375 tg; 7 pencils + 4 notebooks = 530 tg → 50 tg and 45 tg); motion meeting
  problems; animal/area data problems from Kazakh statistics.

**Grade 6 revision exercises (pp. 168–176)**, Glossary (pp. 177–178), Answers
(pp. 179–182), Contents (p. 183), QR/colophon leaf (p. 184).

**Honest negatives, Pt 2 / whole G6 Mektep:** no probability; combinatorics stops
at drawing option trees (no multiplication rule, no permutations formula); no
graph of y = k/x; no graphical solution of systems; vectors have no operations;
no similar triangles / no Pythagoras; statistics has no frequency tables or
weighted mean. **Defects:** the KZ Pt 2 scan (183) is complete and clean except
one report box (ex. 1242, p. 151) whose text renders as mojibake, as in the G5
books. Uyghur Pt 2 (1445) is **missing its front matter** — the scan opens on the
Ch. 4 divider; cover, title, imprint and the part's first contents leaf are
absent, so its part-ISBN cannot be verified from the holding (the Uzbek and
Russian Pt 2 imprints confirm the edition); its body is complete from printed
p. 3 to the end-of-book contents (pp. 182–183 of the printed book), and its
contents agree §-for-§ with the Kazakh book. File 210 (RU Pt 2) ends with a
QR-only final page after the colophon — normal Mektep layout, nothing missing.
