### Work 4 — Shynybeкov, Shynybeкov & Zhumabaev, *Algebra*, Grade 8 (Atamura, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** Kazakh ed. (file 300): Алматы: Атамұра, 2018, 192 pp,
ISBN 978-601-331-162-3. Russian ed. (file 335): 208 pp, ISBN 978-601-331-160-9,
print run 20,000; the held PDF is the publisher's proof file, every page
overprinted «Не для печати». Authors: Әбдухали Насырұлы Шыныбеков, Данияр
Әбдухалиұлы Шыныбеков, Ринат Нурланұлы Жұмабаев; general editor Acad.
M. Ötelbaev. Language of filing: Kazakh edition (PDF page = printed page —
the title page is PDF p.1, the Grade-7 review on PDF p.4 is printed p.4 and
the contents on PDF p.190 is printed p.190);
Russian edition has identical section sequence, longer typesetting.
**Structure.** Grade-7 review (printed pp.4–15), then six chapters; exercises
numbered continuously inside each chapter (e.g. 1.1–1.16x), graded A (basic),
B (medium), C (advanced), *-marked creative/harder; each topic opens with
theory questions and practical/creative tasks; historical notes (Т) throughout.

- **Ch. 1 Square root and irrational expressions** (§1.1 p.16; §1.2 p.23; §1.3
  p.32; §1.4 p.38; §1.5 p.48; extra problems p.53). Square root introduced as
  the inverse operation of squaring (table: number / its square root / why);
  arithmetic square root defined for a ≥ 0. Irrational numbers: proof that √2
  is irrational by contradiction (even/odd parity argument); every rational
  number is a finite or periodic decimal, irrationals are non-periodic.
  Real numbers ↔ points of the number line, locating an irrational (π) by
  nested decimal approximations. Properties of roots (product, quotient,
  powers, √(a²)=|a| type identities) proved, incl. separate even/odd n cases.
  Function y = √x built as the reflection of y = x² (x ≥ 0) in the line y = x.
  Worked examples: multi-step radical simplifications; problems include
  rationalising, comparing surds, identities to prove, expressions with
  sign conditions on variables (a<0, b<0).
- **Ch. 2 Quadratic equations** (§2.1 p.56; §2.2 p.63; §2.3 p.70; §2.4 p.77;
  §2.5 p.82; §2.6 p.87; §2.7 p.95; extra problems p.99). Definition
  ax²+bx+c=0, coefficients named; the trinomial named. Root formula derived by
  completing the square, with D = b²−4ac and the two/one/no-root cases read off
  D. Vieta's theorem **and its converse**, both proved. §2.4: reading the signs
  of the roots from p and q of the reduced equation x²+px+q=0 before solving.
  §2.5 "Solving equations": factorising, equations with the modulus sign
  (worked: 3x²−5|x|+2=0 split by sign of x), biquadratic-type and fractional
  equations reduced to quadratics. §2.6: rational equations and word problems
  set up as quadratics (number/digit problems, motion, work). §2.7: systems of
  two second-degree equations, incl. symmetric systems solved by Vieta
  (worked: x+y=5, xy=6 → roots of z²−5z+6=0).
- **Ch. 3 Quadratic function** (§3.1 p.102; §3.2 p.111). y = a(x−m)² and
  y = ax²+n built from the known graph of y = ax² (comparative graph plates
  for a = 2, 1/2, −1/2 …); vertex, axis, monotonicity read from the graph.
  §3.2 general graph transformations y = f(x)+n, y = f(x−m), y = f(x−m)+n
  for arbitrary f — the general function-transformation idea, not only
  parabolas.
- **Ch. 4 Elements of statistics** (§4.1 p.118; §4.2 p.128). Sample, variant,
  frequency table built with tally marks; relative frequency Wᵢ = nᵢ/n;
  graphical representation (bar/polyline; worked material includes a
  population-of-Kazakhstan table in the Russian edition). Mean defined from
  the frequency table; sample variance D and standard deviation as measures
  of spread, with the deviation-from-mean motivation.
- **Ch. 5 Inequalities** (§5.1 p.136; §5.2 p.144; §5.3 p.153). Properties of
  numerical inequalities stated and **proved**, then used to prove
  inequalities. Quadratic inequalities solved by **two methods in parallel**:
  (1) sign of the parabola on intervals from its sketch; (2) the interval
  method after factorising. Rational inequalities by the interval method.
  Chapter review includes systems of (linear) inequalities.
- **Ch. 6\* Additional materials. Real numbers** (optional, *-marked;
  §6.1\* p.160; §6.2\* p.165; §6.3\* p.168; §6.4\* p.173; §6.5\* p.177).
  Positional number systems (Babylonian sexagesimal note), divisibility
  tests; primes and composites; the fundamental theorem of arithmetic —
  explicitly *accepted without proof*; GCD/LCM from prime factorisations
  (worked: GCD(680, 612) = 68); integers and division with remainder,
  Euclid's algorithm; the Dirichlet (pigeonhole) principle with worked
  "rabbits into hutches" examples. Answers p.180, index p.189.
**Honest negatives.** No probability at all; no mode/median in statistics
(mean, variance, standard deviation only); no quadratic-function chapter
beyond graph transformations (no vertex-formula drill chapter, no
quadratic modelling chapter); no systems of quadratic *inequalities*; no
sequences/progressions (Grade 9 material).
**Defects/notes.** Russian copy is a proof PDF («Не для печати» on every
page) — content complete (208 pp incl. answers/index). Kazakh copy is a
clean print PDF with a full text layer.

### Work 8 — Shynybeкov, Shynybeкov & Zhumabaev, *Geometry*, Grade 8 (Atamura, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** Kazakh ed. (file 303): Алматы: Атамұра, 2018, 152 printed
pp, ISBN 978-601-331-014-5; the held PDF (156 pp) is a pre-print proof —
its colophon still shows placeholder data (set in type "00.00.0000",
print run "000 copies"). Russian ed. (file 338): 112 printed pp,
ISBN 978-601-331-161-6, proof PDF watermarked «Не для печати», print run
20,000. Same work; the Russian typesetting is much more compact.
**Structure.** Grade-7 review (p.4), five chapters; theorems numbered within
each section, proofs closed with ▲/■ markers; A/B/C/* problem grading;
practical tasks (ПТ) and historical notes (Т) in the margins. Chapter 2 is
prefaced by an explicit list of learning outcomes.

- **Ch. 1 Quadrilaterals** (§1.1 p.13; §1.2 p.20; §1.3 p.26; §1.4 p.34; §1.5
  p.38; §1.6 p.43; §1.7 p.49; §1.8 p.57). Polygons via broken lines: convex
  polygon, interior-angle sum. Parallelogram: properties proved (opposite
  sides/angles, diagonals bisecting) and recognition criteria. Rectangle,
  rhombus, square as special parallelograms, each property proved from the
  parallelogram theorems (e.g. rectangle diagonals equal by congruent
  triangles). §1.4 **Constructions**: quadrilateral constructions taught with
  the classical four-stage scheme — analysis, construction, proof,
  investigation. Thales' theorem and the triangle midline theorem;
  trapezoid (definition by exactly-one-pair-parallel here: "the other two
  sides are NOT parallel"), its midline. §1.7 Remarkable points: medians
  concurrent 2:1 (centroid), circumcentre, incentre, orthocentre, and
  excircles (\*1.7.5). §1.8 inscribed angles and inscribed/circumscribed
  quadrilaterals.
- **Ch. 2 Relations between sides and angles of a right triangle** (§2.1
  p.65; §2.2 p.74; §2.3 p.82). Opens with proportional segments; notably
  the **cosine of an acute angle is defined before** the Pythagorean
  theorem (2.1.2), and Pythagoras is then proved (2.1.3). Sine, tangent,
  cotangent defined; values for standard angles; deriving the basic
  trigonometric identities and complementary-angle relations; constructing
  an angle from a given trig value; solving right triangles (2.3.1) and
  constructing them from data (2.3.2). Problems mix computation, proof and
  construction (e.g. legs summing to 17 with hypotenuse 13).
- **Ch. 3 Area** (§3.1 p.90; §3.2 p.94). Concept of area and its properties;
  rectangle area; parallelogram, triangle, trapezoid areas derived;
  equidissection ("equal-composed figures") construction problems.
- **Ch. 4 Rectangular coordinate system in the plane** (§4.1 p.103; §4.2
  p.111). Coordinates, distance between two points, dividing a segment in a
  given ratio and midpoint coordinates; equation of a figure, equation of a
  line (incl. through two points), equation of a circle. Practical tasks
  use Kazakh geography (e.g. write the equation of the line joining
  Zhezkazgan and Balkhash using class-estimated coordinates); a local-studies
  spread on the Karaganda region sits inside this chapter.
- **Ch. 5\* Supplementary materials for in-depth study** (p.118 ff.).
  Proof of the square-area formula, projections of points/segments,
  harder problems (numbered 5.x, e.g. 5.70–5.72 proof problems), and a
  historical note on conic sections (Apollonius' "Conics": ellipse,
  hyperbola, parabola as cone sections).
**Honest negatives.** No vectors; no circle theorems beyond the inscribed-
angle property in §1.8; no similarity chapter (proportional
segments only, as a tool inside Ch. 2); no area of a circle; Heron's formula
is **not** in this book (contrast Work 10).
**Defects/notes.** Both held copies are publisher proofs, not final print
scans: KZ colophon placeholders; RU «Не для печати» watermark. Content in
both is complete (TOC + answers present).

### Work 3 — Abylkasymova & Kucher, *Algebra 8: Electronic Supplement* (teacher's aid, Mektep, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** Russian ed. (file 1555): «Алгебра: Электронное приложение.
Пособие для учителей 8 класса общеобразовательных школ», Алматы: Мектеп,
2018, 43 PDF pp. Kazakh ed. (file 1556): «Электронды қосымша», 42 PDF pp.
Authors А.Е. Әбілқасымова (Абылкасымова) and Т.П. Кучер. Filed in the record
as student textbooks — they are **teacher aids**, the electronic companion to
Work 2, and contain no new student theory.
**Content (both languages, mirroring Work 2's §1–§20).** Two parts.
(1) *Lesson tasks for developing critical thinking and organising individual
and group work*, one block per textbook paragraph: each block runs a named
critical-thinking routine (e.g. «Верю — не верю» / «Сенемін — сенбеймін»,
«До — После», «Зигзаг», «Вращающаяся станция», «Снежный ком», «Пазл») over statements
about that paragraph's material — e.g. for §1 Real numbers, agree/disagree
statements such as "a rational number whose square is 2 does not exist".
(2) *Formative-assessment tests*, again per paragraph (§1–§20): short test
items on each topic (real numbers, root properties, quadratic equations,
Vieta, rational equations, quadratic function, statistics, inequalities,
systems of non-linear inequalities). Useful as a map of what the Mektep
course expects per paragraph, and as ready formative items; it teaches no
mathematics itself.

### Work 1 — Apeeva, Zhampeisova, Kozhakhmetova, Ryskeldieva (+ Egorkina in Pt 2), *Mathematics*, Grade 8, NIS-Programme (Nur-Sultan, 2019)
**Imprint facts.** A combined Mathematics course for Nazarbayev Intellectual
Schools (not an "Algebra" book, and not Russian-first as recorded). Part 1:
192 pp, ISBN 978-601-328-329-6 (set ISBN 978-601-328-330-2), held in Kazakh
only, in two identical copies (files 1077, 1078). Part 2: 256 pp; Kazakh
(file 1079) ISBN 978-601-328-540-5; Russian (file 1080) ISBN
978-601-328-542-9 (set ISBN 978-601-328-334-0). Printed page = PDF page
in both parts (Part 1: the contents on PDF p.3 is printed p.3 and §1.1 on
PDF p.8 is printed p.8; Part 2: §5.1 on PDF p.6 is printed p.6); Part 2
pagination restarts at 1.
**Pedagogy (both parts, uniform).** Every chapter opens with a full-page
photo spread listing the learning outcomes ("In this chapter you will…").
Sections carry colour-coded boxes: reading text, *Solution*, *Proof*,
"Preparing for olympiads" (harder problems with full solutions), *Remember!*,
"Do you know?" (history/extra facts, e.g. Cantor, Viète, Pólya, Descartes,
Lobachevsky portraits), "We speak and read correctly" / "We write correctly"
— language-of-mathematics boxes that teach how to pronounce and write the
new terms (a signature NIS feature for a trilingual school system).
GeoGebra screenshots and calculator tasks recur; some theory is presented as
scripted Teacher–Student dialogues. Exercises are numbered continuously per
chapter (1.1–1.187 etc.), NOT graded A/B/C. Each chapter ends with a review
("…тарауына шолу") and a self-assessment page whose answers are printed
upside down; full answers to all exercises close each part.

#### Part 1 (files 1077/1078, Kazakh)
- **Ch. 1 Square roots and irrational expressions** (§§1.1–1.18, printed
  pp.8–54). Square root via the graph of y = x² and the area problem;
  arithmetic root. Real numbers in two sections: sets N ⊂ Z ⊂ Q with Venn
  diagrams, periodic decimals, irrational = non-periodic decimal, locating
  π on the line; a Teacher–Student dialogue section works through "is √5 = 0
  possible?" type questions. Estimating root values by bracketing (with the
  pendulum formula T = 2π√(l/g) as a physics application). y = √x in two
  sections: graph by table and GeoGebra, domain/range in D(f), E(f)
  notation, symmetry in the line y = x. Root properties spread over three
  sections (§§1.7–1.9) with card-matching tasks; monotonicity theorem
  (a < b ⇒ √a < √b) stated and proved; domain of radical expressions;
  taking a factor out of / into the root; rationalising denominators incl.
  conjugate pairs; transforming nested radicals (olympiad box works
  √(a ± √b) denesting); comparing real numbers; operations on rational vs
  irrational numbers (closure questions: is the sum of two irrationals
  irrational?). Applications: Saturn gravity, free-fall and energy formulas.
- **Ch. 2 Polygons. Studying quadrilaterals** (§§2.1–2.12, pp.58–88).
  Polygons and their elements from photographs (Yasawi mausoleum, Islamic
  ornament); angle-sum of a convex polygon by triangulation (fill-in
  table), exterior sum 360°. Parallelogram properties, then criteria —
  presented with a theorem/converse flowchart; rectangle–rhombus–square
  properties with a quadrilateral-family Venn diagram and a big
  property-comparison table to complete. Trapezoid defined exclusively
  (one pair of sides parallel, the other pair NOT), its midline proved by
  extending a leg. Thales' theorem (two proof routes tabulated) and the
  proportional-segments theorem (§§2.11–2.12), with segment-division
  constructions.
- **Ch. 3 Quadratic equations** (§§3.1–3.16, pp.90–136). Equation motivated
  by an area problem; incomplete equations classified by a flowchart
  (b = 0 / c = 0 cases); a "find the mistake" dialogue (two pupils solve
  2x² − 6x = 0, one loses a root). Completing the square with geometric
  area diagrams in the al-Khwarizmi style; the root formula is then taught
  **twice** (§3.4 general derivation, §3.5 again with the discriminant and,
  for even b, the half-discriminant D₁ = (b/2)² − ac). Vieta's theorem
  (§3.6) and its converse (§3.7) with biography boxes. Factorising the
  quadratic trinomial a(x−x₁)(x−x₂) with a D-sign flowchart (§3.8).
  Equations reducible to quadratics: biquadratic and (x²−2x)²-type by
  substitution (§3.9). Word problems by modelling cycle — problem →
  mathematical model → solve → interpret (§§3.10–3.11). Fractional-rational
  equations in two sections (§§3.12–3.13) with a printed 5-step algorithm
  and domain checks; word problems via them (§§3.14–3.15: work, motion,
  with rate tables). §3.16: quadratic equations containing a modulus sign,
  |f(x)| defined piecewise, case flowchart.
- **Ch. 4 Relations between sides and angles of a right triangle**
  (§§4.1–4.15, pp.138–178). Pythagoras proved by area dissection, plus its
  converse. Sine, cosine, tangent, cotangent defined from the triangle;
  road-gradient (%) application; constructing an angle from a given trig
  value; complementary-angle relations sin α = cos(90°−α) etc.; the basic
  identity sin²α + cos²α = 1 derived from Pythagoras, with 1 + tg²α =
  1/cos²α; interconversion of one angle's four functions; values at
  30°/45°/60° derived geometrically and tabulated; solving right triangles
  **with a calculator**, including inverse-trig keys (a COS⁻¹ keypad
  screenshot) and rounding discipline. §§4.11–4.12: proportional segments
  in a right triangle — altitude-to-hypotenuse (geometric mean) relations
  derived. §§4.13–4.15: mixed problem solving, incl. practical measurement
  and construction tasks.
- **Honest negatives (Pt 1).** No quadratic *function* (deferred to Pt 2),
  no statistics, no inequalities, no coordinate geometry (Pt 2), no area
  chapter (Pt 2); no proof of Thales' converse beyond the tabulated cases;
  trapezoid is the exclusive definition.

#### Part 2 (files 1079 Kazakh / 1080 Russian — same content, spot-checked)
- **Ch. 5 Quadratic function and its graph** (§§5.1–5.18, pp.6–50). The
  vertex forms are spiralled three times (§§5.1–5.3 treat y = a(x−m)²,
  y = ax²+n, y = a(x−m)²+n in turn — graphing, then properties, then
  problems — with GeoGebra plates), then y = ax²+bx+c over §§5.4–5.7:
  completing the square, vertex by formula x₀ = −b/2a, intercepts, a printed
  sketching algorithm. §§5.8–5.9 applied problems: parabolic bridges of
  Astana (photographs), projectile/football trajectories, maximal-area
  fencing. §§5.10–5.11 read the signs/positions of a, b, c and D off a
  sketch and vice versa. §§5.12–5.14 general graph transformations
  (shifts, stretches y = kf(x), y = f(kx)) with tables of anchor points.
  §§5.15–5.16 the fractional-linear function y = (ax+b)/(cx+d): graph is a
  hyperbola, asymptotes, domain. §§5.17–5.18 graphs of y = |f(x)| and
  y = f(|x|).
- **Ch. 6 Area** (§§6.1–6.17, pp.52–102). Area as counting unit squares
  (grid work); equal-area and equi-decomposable figures (§§6.2–6.3,
  tangram-style dissection, incl. nets/surface area of boxes); §6.4 the
  sine of an obtuse angle (0°–180° table, unit semicircle); parallelogram
  area; rhombus area both as a·h and as ½d₁d₂ (§6.6); triangle area
  ½ah and ½ab·sin C (§6.8); area ratios for equal-height triangles (§6.9);
  Heron's formula with a Heron of Alexandria box (§6.10); areas of
  arbitrary triangles/quadrilaterals incl. lattice (Pick-type) counting
  (§§6.11, 6.14–6.15); trapezoid area proved by three different dissections
  (§§6.12–6.13); §6.16 "the area method" as a proof technique; §6.17
  problem set. Applied contexts: roof tiles, land plots, house plans.
- **Ch. 7 Elements of statistics** (§§7.1–7.8, pp.104–132). Richer than any
  other book in the band: population vs sample; a data taxonomy chart
  (discrete / continuous / ordinal / nominal); frequency and interval
  tables with relative frequencies; §7.3 adds the **box-and-whisker plot**
  ("мұртты жәшік") with quartiles Q₁, Q₃ computed from a table; frequency
  polygons and histograms (§§7.4–7.5), including cumulative-frequency
  curves (ogives); mean and dispersion (§7.6, Gauss portrait box);
  §7.7 properties of the mean and variance under shifting/scaling data;
  §7.8 the standard deviation of a series computed by the Σ(xᵢ−x̄)² table
  method. Still no probability.
- **Ch. 8 Quadratic inequalities** (§§8.1–8.22, pp.134–184). The longest
  chapter in either part. Quadratic inequalities over four sections:
  solving from the parabola's sign, with match-the-graph-to-the-answer
  tables. Rational inequalities (§§8.5–8.8): whole vs fractional
  distinguished, the interval method with "snake" sign diagrams,
  fractional-rational inequalities with domain holes marked. Systems vs
  collections of inequalities (§§8.9–8.14) — the book explicitly contrasts
  жүйе (system, intersection) with жиынтық (collection, union) in a set-
  language table with Venn diagrams, and runs both for fractional-rational
  members. §§8.15–8.16: domain ("possible values") of expressions
  containing square roots, solved by interval method. §§8.17–8.19:
  inequalities containing a modulus sign, solved graphically from
  y = |f(x)| sketches and by cases. §§8.20–8.22: problem solving
  (geometric constraints, a Malthus/population box, fencing optimisation).
- **Ch. 9 Rectangular coordinate system in the plane** (§§9.1–9.17,
  pp.186–228). Distance between two points derived from Pythagoras
  (§§9.1–9.2, Descartes box); midpoint coordinates (§§9.3–9.4); circle
  equation (§§9.5–9.6), incl. completing the square to recover centre and
  radius; §9.7 plotting circles from equations **in GeoGebra and in a
  spreadsheet** (Excel screenshots); the line equation over four sections
  (§§9.8–9.11): two-point form, general form ax+by+c = 0, slope as tg α,
  parallel/perpendicular conditions (Fermat portrait box); §§9.12–9.17
  problem solving and constructions (GeoGebra tasks, a city-map route
  problem, circle through given points).
- **Honest negatives (Pt 2, and the work as a whole).** No probability
  anywhere; no vectors; no similarity chapter (proportional segments are
  used as a tool only); no trigonometric functions beyond 180°; no
  sequences; statistics stops at descriptive statistics (but is the
  deepest in the band — box plots and ogives appear in no other G8 book
  here). Part 1 in Russian is **not held** (owed).

### Work 2 — Abylkasymova, Kucher, Zhumagulova & Korchevsky, *Algebra*, Grade 8 (Mektep, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** One work in four language editions, 200 printed pp:
Kazakh (file 301) ISBN 978-601-07-0967-6; Russian (file 336) ISBN
978-601-07-0975-1; Uyghur (file 1383, trans. Kurbanov & Mizhitova) ISBN
978-601-07-1062-7; Uzbek (file 1397, trans. Zainiddinova) ISBN
978-601-07-1068-9. Filed here from the complete Russian edition (printed
page = PDF page − 1); exercise sets verified identical (same numbers, same
values) in the Kazakh, Uyghur and Uzbek editions. **Defect:** the Kazakh
copy ends at printed p.196 — its contents and colophon (pp.197–200) are
missing; text and answers are complete.
**Structure.** A Grade-7 review block (pp.5–15, 67 items) includes a set of
"practice-oriented tasks for functional mathematical literacy" (PISA-style:
read an alloy-composition graph, pie charts of an orchard, a motorist's
distance–time graph). Every § then follows one pattern: theory with
"Объясните!" (Explain!) worked examples, consolidation questions, exercises
in levels **A** (compulsory) / **B** / **C**, and closing strips "Повторите"
(Repeat) and "Подготовьтесь к овладению новыми знаниями" (Prepare for the
new material — preview exercises).

- **Ch. 1 Square roots and irrational expressions** (§§1–5, pp.16–51).
  §1 proves √2 irrational by contradiction in full, builds R with an
  inclusion scheme, treats periodic decimals. §2 introduces the square root
  through a table of inverse operations; arithmetic root; √(a²) = |a| by
  cases; approximate roots by calculator (worked: √57.1 ≈ 7.56) and by the
  book's table of squares; a history box on Omar Khayyam. §3 states and
  **proves** the three root theorems (product, quotient, √(a²ⁿ) cases).
  §4 transformations: factor out/in, comparing surds, rationalising
  denominators with named conjugate pairs, and a nested-radical worked
  example (√(13+4√3) + √(13−4√3)). §5 the function y = √x with D(y),
  E(y) notation, graphs of y = √(x±1), y = √x ± 1 on one axes, graphical
  solution of equations (worked: 0.5x = √x).
- **Ch. 2 Quadratic equations** (§§6–12, pp.52–107). §6 classifies
  equations (complete/incomplete, reduced/unreduced — a printed scheme)
  and solves incomplete ones and full ones by completing the square.
  §7 states the root formula as **Theorem 1 with a complete derivation**;
  the discriminant is named and glossed from Latin; the three D cases;
  **Theorem 2** gives the even-b formula. Parameter problems appear from
  §7 on (C level). §8 derives Vieta's theorem from the root formulas,
  proves the converse, and runs oral (устно) drills; history box: Viète
  and al-Khwarizmi. §9 factorises the quadratic trinomial (theorem proved
  via Vieta) and applies it to cancelling fractions; a modelling item uses
  a piecewise braking-distance function of a tourist's speed. §10
  fractional-rational equations with a printed algorithm (domain → common
  denominator → solve → discard extraneous roots). §11 equations reducible
  to quadratics: biquadratics, substitution of a repeated expression, and
  equations with the modulus sign |ax²+bx| + c = 0 by sign cases; also
  f(x) = f(g(x))-type items. §12 word problems with a printed 5-step
  scheme; motion (rivers, trains), joint work, areas — one of the largest
  word-problem banks in the band (33 items).
- **Ch. 3 Quadratic function** (§§13–14, pp.108–125). §13 builds
  y = a(x−m)², y = ax²+n, y = a(x−m)²+n from y = ax² by shifts, motivated
  by physics formulas (free fall s = gt²/2, Joule heat Q = Rt²). §14
  converts y = ax²+bx+c to vertex form by completing the square, gives the
  vertex formulas, and prints a boxed **algorithm for sketching the
  parabola** (vertex → axis → zeros → y-intercept → extra points); a
  comparison table of two parabolas' properties; applied maximum problems
  (greatest depth of a river from a current-speed model).
- **Ch. 4 Elements of statistics** (§§15–17, pp.126–142). §15 interval
  frequency tables (worked on 25 children's body masses) and the histogram,
  with a printed construction algorithm; official-data items (population
  of Kazakhstan by year, from the national statistics committee). §16
  cumulative frequency, with cumulative polygons read and built. §17 works
  in genuine random-variable language: a random variable's values and
  frequencies; **general population vs sample**; mean x̄; **dispersion
  D(X) = mean of squares − square of mean** (the E(X²) − (E X)² identity,
  worked numerically); standard deviation σ(X) = √D(X). No probability,
  no median/mode, no box plots (contrast Work 1 Pt 2).
- **Ch. 5 Inequalities** (§§18–20, pp.143–166). §18 solves quadratic
  inequalities **only** from the parabola sketch, with all D-sign cases
  tabulated as figures (incl. D < 0: no-solution and all-reals cases).
  §19 the interval method, developed carefully: sign constancy on
  intervals proved from factor signs, the "wavy curve" diagrams, repeated
  (even-multiplicity) roots handled; §19 also reaches fractional
  inequalities and, at C level, max/min-of-two-functions notation
  (ex. 19.24). §20 systems of non-linear inequalities with one variable:
  solve each, intersect on the number line; function domains as systems;
  systems containing modulus (20.19).
- **Back matter.** Grade-8 review exercises (pp.167–175, 87 items incl.
  graphical equations and modulus); practice-oriented tasks on official
  2017–2018 Kazakhstan agricultural statistics with spreadsheet (Excel)
  instructions; a two-page **glossary** of all new terms; subject index;
  table of squares 10–99; answers.
**Honest negatives.** No geometry (separate book); no quadratic-function
*applications* chapter beyond single max/min items; no systems of
inequalities with two variables; no probability; statistics has no
median/mode/quartiles.

### Work 5 — Soltan, Soltan & Zhumadilova, *Algebra*, Grade 8 (Keleshek-2030, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** Kazakh ed. (file 302): ISBN 978-601-317-342-9. Russian
ed. (file 337): ISBN 978-601-317-332-0, print run 20,000. 216 printed pp,
with CD. Filed from the Russian edition; the Kazakh edition's contents
match section-for-section (~1 p. offset). **Design:** exercises are numbered
**continuously through the whole book** (1–~700), each section carrying
"Вопросы" (oral questions) and exercises in levels A/B/C; each chapter
closes with "Проверь себя!" self-tests and an "Это интересно" history page;
chapter openers state what the pupil will know/be able to do. Kazakh realia
throughout (a coloured map and a 2016 population-density table by region in
the review chapter; Charyn canyon and Burabay photographs).
**Structure.** The Grade-7 review (pp.7–15) is a *theory* recap with formula
tables — powers, short-multiplication formulas, graphs of y = kx+b,
y = k/x, y = ax², y = ax³, and Grade-7 statistics **including median, mode
and range**, all defined — before the A/B/C exercises.

- **Ch. I Square roots and irrational expressions** (§§1–7, pp.16–60).
  §1 proves that a fraction whose denominator has prime factors other than
  2 and 5 gives an infinite periodic decimal, and converts periodic
  decimals to fractions by the 1000x − 100x method (worked: 0.42(7)).
  §2 proves the diagonal and side of a square incommensurable (parity
  argument on the measured lengths) — irrationality shown geometrically;
  reals as infinite decimals; √2 trapped by successive approximation
  (1.4 < d < 1.5 < … < 1.41). §3: arithmetic square root; solving x² = 2
  graphically; a worked digit-by-digit bracketing of √59; a compound-growth
  (bank deposit) worked problem. §4 states **four theorems, all proved**:
  √(a²) = |a|; root of a product; root of a quotient; monotonicity
  (a > b ≥ 0 ⇒ √a > √b) — applied to comparing two rectangles' areas.
  §5 transformations (factor in/out with sign warnings, rationalising,
  heavy worked fractions, an embedded pay word problem, a least-perimeter
  problem). §6 y = √x: *increasing/decreasing function defined formally*,
  D(y) = [0; +∞), and the symmetry of y = √x and y = x² in the line y = x
  **proved** by congruent triangles. §7 chapter review; the history page
  covers Heron of Alexandria's root-extraction formula √m ≈ a + b/2a,
  Bhaskara, and Rudolff's radical sign (1525).
- **Ch. II Quadratic equations** (§§8–16, pp.61–104). §8 meets the
  equation through completing the square first (3x² − 12x − 3 = 0 worked
  that way); incomplete equations; an error-hunt item (two pupils' wrong
  solution of nx² + n = 0). §9 root formulas with derivation; **D** named;
  even-b and reduced-equation formulas; Mendeleev-flavoured problem (№233,
  element masses); Bhaskara's own derivation set as an exercise (№235).
  §10 Vieta **and converse, both proved**; symmetric expressions in the
  roots (m³ + n³) at C level. §11 factorising the trinomial (theorem
  proved). §12 word problems (polygon diagonals n(n−3)/2 = 77 worked;
  carpet width to 0.01 m). §13 equations reducible to quadratics in the
  general form ax²ⁿ + bxⁿ + c = 0 (worked examples: biquadratic,
  x⁶-type, modulus and a product form); a "coded" problem (№311) built
  into the equation 36x² − 1996|x| + 178 = 0, whose constants are facts
  about the Ile-Alatau national park — 1996 the park's opening year, 36
  its Red-Book plant species, 178 its bird species (ibisbill photograph). §14 whole and
  fractional-rational equations with a printed 5-step algorithm and an
  Omar Khayyam problem (№330). §15 word problems on fractional-rational
  equations (motion; a market-price problem solved in general form).
  §16 chapter review; history page: al-Khwarizmi's geometric solution of
  x² + 10x = 39 with the figure.
- **Ch. III Quadratic function** (§§17–22, pp.105–140). §§17–19 take the
  three vertex forms one per section, each with a full properties table
  (D(y), E(y), monotonicity intervals, zeros); §19 *proves* the graph of
  y = a(x−m)²+n is the shifted parabola (congruence argument). §20
  y = ax²+bx+c: vertex formulas, properties table, **monotonicity proved
  algebraically** (sign of y₂ − y₁), a 5-step sketching algorithm. §21
  applied problems: maximal rectangle area S(t), a projectile
  h(t) = 40t − 4.9t² (max height ≈ 82 m), the diagonals-of-a-polygon
  count as a quadratic function. §22 review; Dirichlet and Lobachevsky
  boxes on the function concept.
- **Ch. IV Elements of statistics** (§§23–25, pp.141–158). §23 discrete
  vs continuous data; frequency tables, polygons, histograms; interval
  tables; a cumulative-frequency curve (рис. 39); problems use election
  results and city populations. §24 variance as the mean squared deviation
  σ² = Σ(xᵢ−x̄)²/n, standard deviation as its root; **the shortcut
  σ² = (Σxᵢ²)/n − x̄² is proved**; a Tien Shan peaks data problem. §25
  review works mode/median/mean of one sample in full. No probability.
- **Ch. V Inequalities** (§§26–31, pp.159–196). §26 quadratic inequalities
  graphically, with **equivalent inequalities defined** and a complete
  printed case table (sign of a × sign of D → solution set). §27 the
  interval method, incl. fractional-rational inequalities and a parameter
  problem (x² + 2x + a > 0 for all x). §28 word problems as inequalities
  (admissible boat speeds). §29 systems of non-linear inequalities
  (definitions, schematic pictures of solution sets, function domains).
  §30 word problems as systems (uniformly accelerated motion bound).
  §31 review (a Burabay national-park visitor problem). The year review
  (pp.192–195) and a "Это интересно" page on **Bolzano's intermediate
  value theorem** close the book; answers and hints (many are worked
  hints), subject index, table of squares 10–99, extra-literature list.
**Honest negatives.** No geometry; no probability; statistics has no
cumulative-frequency *table* chapter separate from §23's curve, and no box
plots; no functions beyond quadratic/√x except the review's y = ax³,
y = k/x reminders.

### Work 7 — Smirnov & Tuyakov, *Geometry*, Grade 8 (Mektep, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** One work in four language editions: Kazakh (file 304)
ISBN 978-601-07-0960-7, 160 printed pp; Russian (file 339) ISBN
978-601-07-0959-1, 152 printed pp; Uzbek (file 1357, trans. Zainiddinova)
ISBN 978-601-07-1069-6; Uyghur (file 1421, trans. Taiirova) ISBN
978-601-07-1063-4. Filed from the complete Russian edition (printed page =
PDF page − 1); problem sets verified identical in all four editions.
**Defect:** the Kazakh copy is **truncated** — it stops at printed p.143
(subject index); its answers and contents (pp.144–160) are missing, though
all theory chapters are present.
**Design.** The most proof-driven book in the band: every property is a
numbered theorem/property with a printed proof; recurring prompts
"Попробуй обосновать" (try to justify) hand steps to the pupil; exercises
in levels A/B/C per §; a multiple-choice "Проверь себя" test closes each
chapter; history boxes (Thales, Euclid-era material, Pythagoras, Euler,
Descartes). Grade-7 review (pp.5–9) is a pure problem bank organised by
the four Grade-7 chapters.

- **Ch. 1 Polygons. Study of quadrilaterals** (§§1–12, pp.10–55). §1
  broken lines — length, closed/open, self-intersecting; digits drawn as
  broken lines and grid path-counting problems. §2 polygons: convex vs
  non-convex, regular polygons, star polygons. §3 **both** angle-sum
  theorems proved (interior 180°(n−2) by triangulation; exterior 360°).
  §4 parallelogram: three properties, each proved (opposite sides/angles;
  diagonals bisect). §5 two recognition criteria, proved. §6 rectangle:
  the *distance between parallel lines* is defined here; equal diagonals
  proved. §7 rhombus and square: diagonal theorems and recognition
  criteria, all proved (rhombus from perpendicular diagonals, etc.).
  §8 triangle midline theorem, proved by doubling to a parallelogram.
  §9 trapezoid — defined **exclusively** (the other pair of sides is NOT
  parallel); isosceles-trapezoid properties proved. §10 trapezoid midline
  via the triangle midline. §11 Thales' theorem and proportional segments
  with the full classical proof (commensurable case by equal parts,
  incommensurable case sketched with the OA/OB = k argument), plus
  segment-division constructions and a C-level problem on Astana's
  Pyramid of Peace and the Louvre. §12 the four remarkable points are
  listed together (incentre, circumcentre, centroid, orthocentre);
  the median 2:1 theorem is proved; altitude concurrency is shown on a
  figure.
- **Ch. 2 Relations between sides and angles of a right triangle**
  (§§13–18, pp.56–85). §13 defines sin/cos/tg/ctg and **proves they depend
  only on the angle** (via Thales/proportional segments); tg = sin/cos;
  values at 30°/45°/60° derived from triangles; table of approximate
  values taught (Bradis-style); tangent read off grid paper. §14
  **Pythagoras is proved from proportional segments** (altitude to the
  hypotenuse: BD² = AD·DC, hence the theorem), followed by the converse,
  the circumradius of a right triangle, Pythagorean triples, and applied
  problems (two ships leaving a port, the classical reed-in-the-pond
  problem). §15 the basic identity sin²A + cos²A = 1 proved, with
  1 + tg²A = 1/cos²A and tgA·ctgA = 1. §16 solving right triangles: the
  four working relations are tabulated (leg = hypotenuse × sin/cos of an
  angle, etc.) and four worked examples cover the standard cases; the
  drill bank is the band's largest (~40 near-identical items at B/C).
  §17 extends sin/cos/tg/ctg to right and obtuse angles by the unit
  semicircle (sin(180°−A) = sin A, cos(180°−A) = −cos A) and keeps the
  main identity valid for all angles. §18 practical measurement problems
  (bearings walks, pendulums, an aircraft's glide path) + chapter test.
- **Ch. 3 Areas** (§§19–24, pp.86–115). §19 area as measurement: unit-
  square tiling with a remainder term (the Φ/Φ* discussion is unusually
  rigorous for Grade 8), three properties of area stated, S = ab for the
  rectangle, S = a² for the square; many grid-counting tasks. §20
  parallelogram area proved by cutting off a triangle to form a
  trapezoid/rectangle; corollary S = ab·sin α. §21 triangle area as half
  a parallelogram; **S = ½ab·sin C**; and — remarkably — **a second,
  dissection proof of Pythagoras** ((a+b)² = 2ab + c²) plus the inradius
  formula S = p·r derived from triangle areas. §22 trapezoid area by the
  diagonal split. §23 area of a polygon by triangulation, with the theorem
  **S = ½·d₁·d₂·sin φ for a convex quadrilateral** proved, and corollaries
  (rhombus S = ½d₁d₂). §24 equal-area and equi-decomposable figures,
  containing **a third proof of Pythagoras** (the square on the hypotenuse
  is dissected into the squares on the legs) and a rich cutting-puzzle
  bank (Greek cross into a square, etc.). Chapter test.
- **Ch. 4 Rectangular coordinate system on the plane** (§§25–28*,
  pp.116–131). §25 builds coordinates **on a line first** (distance
  |x₂ − x₁| and midpoint theorems proved), then the plane; Descartes box.
  §26 distance between two points via Pythagoras; the circle's equation
  obtained as a locus (x−a)² + (y−b)² = R². §27 the line's equation: the
  two-point form derived, slope k = tg α named "угловой коэффициент",
  parallel (k₁ = k₂) and perpendicular (k₁k₂ = −1) conditions derived,
  general form ax + by + c = 0. §28* (extra-curricular) *analytic
  specification of figures*: half-planes by linear inequalities, a convex
  polygon as a **system of inequalities** (unit square worked), with a
  note on mathematical modelling. Review (114 problems), a full table of
  approximate trig values, index, answers — including drawn cutting
  diagrams for §24.
**Honest negatives.** No Heron's formula (contrast Works 8–10); no circle
theorems (inscribed angles are Grade 9 here); no vectors; no similarity
beyond proportional segments; Heron-free area work relies on ½ab·sin C;
no coordinate *proofs* of quadrilateral properties beyond §28* sketches.

### Work 9 — Soltan, Soltan & Zhumadilova, *Geometry*, Grade 8 (Keleshek-2030, 2018)
**Imprint facts.** Kazakh ed. (file 305): ISBN 978-601-317-343-6, 216
printed pp. Russian ed. (file 340): ISBN 978-601-317-335-1, 208 printed pp.
Filed from the Russian edition; Kazakh contents match section-for-section.
Same design as Work 5: continuous exercise numbering (1–~375 plus a
separate picture-drill appendix), "Вопросы" per section, A/B/C levels,
"Проверь себя!" chapter tests, "Это интересно" history pages, practical
tasks (Практическое задание) — e.g. model your land plot as a pentagon
and measure its angles with a protractor. The Grade-7 review (pp.7–18) is
an illustrated theory recap (adjacent/vertical angles, parallel lines,
triangle facts, circle facts incl. central and inscribed angles and
tangent properties) followed by questions and A/B/C problems.

- **Ch. I Polygons. Study of quadrilaterals** (§§1–13, pp.20–73).
  §1 polygons and the angle-sum theorem (proved by triangulation);
  exterior angles; research-flavoured problems (largest possible number
  of acute interior angles; a quadrilateral's perimeter exceeds that of
  any inscribed triangle — by the broken-line inequality). §2 the
  quadrilateral family: parallelogram defined by both pairs of opposite
  sides parallel; trapezoid defined **exclusively** (one pair parallel,
  the other pair NOT); parallelogram properties proved (opposite
  sides/angles; diagonals bisect) and **central symmetry is introduced**
  — the parallelogram as a centrally symmetric figure, a Soltan signature.
  §3 three parallelogram criteria, each proved; a coordinate-geometry
  problem hidden here (prove A(−3;−3), B(−3;1), C(3;1), D(3;−3) form a
  rectangle). §§4–6 rectangle, rhombus, square — properties AND criteria
  for each, all proved; axial symmetry is defined in the rhombus section
  (its diagonals as axes of symmetry; leaf/butterfly photographs); the
  square is credited with **four** axes of symmetry. §7 trapezoid:
  leg-angle sums (180°) and the isosceles criteria/properties proved.
  §8 compass-and-ruler constructions of quadrilaterals in the
  Given/Analysis/Construction format (rhombus from its two diagonals
  worked). §9 Thales' theorem with the full two-stage proof
  (equal segments via parallelograms; proportional segments by a
  contradiction/squeeze argument), the angle-bisector division theorem,
  and constructing a fourth proportional segment. §10 the triangle
  midline — proved via Thales, with Varignon's theorem worked as a
  problem (midpoints of a quadrilateral form a parallelogram whose
  perimeter is the sum of the diagonals). §11 trapezoid midline.
  §12 remarkable points: altitude concurrency **proved** (the altitudes
  of ABC are the perpendicular bisectors of the medial-complement
  triangle), median concurrency with the 2:1 ratio **proved** via
  midlines; incentre/circumcentre recapped. §13 review; history page on
  Descartes and Lobachevsky.
- **Ch. II Relations between sides and angles of a right triangle**
  (§§14–20, pp.75–104). Unusual order: **cosine comes first** (§14),
  defined as adjacent/hypotenuse, with cos 60° = 1/2 obtained from the
  30-60-90 triangle. §15 **Pythagoras is proved from the cosine
  definition** (leg = projection relation AC² = AB·AH, added for both
  legs), then the converse by constructing an equal right triangle.
  §16 sine, tangent, cotangent derived from cosine via Pythagoras
  (ratios MH/AM etc.), independence of the chosen point proved; Greek
  etymology of the terms. §17 properties: a monotonicity theorem (larger
  acute angle ⇒ larger sine and tangent, smaller cosine and cotangent)
  left to the pupil to prove; complementary-angle relations set as a
  guided problem; exact values at 30°/45°/60° computed and tabulated
  both exactly and decimally. §18 identities: tg α = sin α/cos α named,
  the main identity sin²α + cos²α = 1 called "the basic trigonometric
  identity", 1 + tg²α = 1/cos²α and tg α · ctg α = 1. §19 solving right
  triangles by table lookup with stated rounding (0.1 cm, 1°); an applied
  problem measures the height of a rock in the Burabay park from two
  angles of elevation (answer 275 m). §20 chapter problems: distance
  across a lake by trigonometry; an aircraft's glide angle (answer 9°);
  constructions of an angle with given sine/cosine. History page: Nasir
  al-Din al-Tusi and the birth of trigonometry.
- **Ch. III Areas of figures** (§§21–25, pp.106–127). §21 area concept
  with four listed properties; **equal-area vs equi-decomposable figures
  distinguished**; rectangle area proved by the (a+b)² dissection; a
  worked fencing problem shows the square beats the rectangle of equal
  perimeter; data problems use Kazakhstan's total area and Lake
  Balkhash. §22 parallelogram area (S = a·h) and triangle area proved;
  **S = ½ab·sin A derived**, with the obtuse-angle case sin(180°−A).
  §23 the convex-quadrilateral theorem **S = ½·d₁·d₂·sin φ** proved by
  summing four triangle areas; corollaries for perpendicular diagonals
  and the rhombus. §24 trapezoid area proved by the diagonal split.
  §25 chapter problems, incl. areas of pyramid faces and national-park
  areas (Katon-Karagai). History page: F. Vieta (Vieta) on area theory.
- **Ch. IV Rectangular coordinate system on the plane** (§§26–30,
  pp.130–151). §26 coordinates recapped from the line; **midpoint and
  distance theorems both proved**; a Khan Tengri relief problem. §27
  equations of lines: the general equation ax + by + c = 0 is *derived*
  from two given points; special cases; y = kx + p with **k = tg α**;
  a worked proof that perpendicular lines have k₁·k₂ = −1; a Burabay
  flora counting problem in coordinates. §28 circle equation from the
  distance definition; recovering centre/radius by completing the
  square; two tangent circles. §29 **trigonometric functions of angles
  0°–180° defined by coordinates** on the unit semicircle (sin α = y,
  cos α = x), with the 0°/90°/180° table (tg 90° "does not exist") and
  the reduction formulas sin(180°−α) = sin α, cos(180°−α) = −cos α read
  off the figure. §30 chapter problems include a **full coordinate proof**
  that a trapezoid's diagonal-squares sum equals the leg-squares sum
  plus twice the product of the bases. History page: Leibniz and Fermat
  on coordinates.
- **Back matter.** Grade-8 review (pp.152–156, "formulate the definition/
  property/criterion" items dominate); appendices: tangent table 0°–89°,
  sine/cosine table 0°–90°, and ~40 pages of **picture-based training
  exercises** ("find the unknown elements" from diagrams, incl. multiple
  choice) for every topic; answers and hints; subject index; extra
  literature.
**Honest negatives.** No Heron's formula; no circle theorems beyond the
Grade-7 review recap; no vectors; no similarity theory (proportional
segments only); area of a circle absent; no Pick-type lattice methods.

### Work 6 — Yelemessov, Umbetov, Auezov, Amanzhol, Malbasarova & Turapbekov, *Algebra. Grade 8. Textbook* (Astana-kitap, 2023), in English
**Imprint facts.** File 1583: Алматы: Астана-кітап, 2023, 188 pp, ISBN
978-601-80993-4-2; recommended by the Ministry of Education of the RK.
Lead author **Akzhol Yelemessov** (title page and bibliographic entry;
the back cover misprints his first name as "Akhol" — the book's own slip).
Editor Yernar Bazarov. **Design (shared with Work 10):** an international-
style textbook adapted for Kazakhstan — narrative explanations, fully
worked Examples with every step annotated, short PRACTICE sets after each
example, end-of-section PROBLEMS graded A/B/C, "You will:" outcome boxes,
Discussion / Activity / Research / Fact / Real-life side boxes, Kazakh and
Russian glosses for every key term, chapter SUMMARY spreads, and an Answer
key (answers only). Kazakh names and settings (Nurdaulet, Aibek, Esirkep,
Aigerim; tenge amounts) are woven into an otherwise imported template —
units occasionally slip (a bridge problem in feet; a worker paid in
dollars on a "Work&Travel" stay in the USA; Delhi rainfall data).

- **Ch. 1 Square roots and irrational expressions** (§§1.1–1.3, pp.14–46).
  §1.1 builds the number system narratively — counting sheep motivates
  natural numbers, then whole numbers W (with 0), integers, rationals,
  irrationals (Greek discovery story; π box), reals — closed by a nested-
  sets diagram with trilingual labels. Square root via areas of squares
  (1²–10² grid pictures); the rule √(x²) = |x| boxed; an error-hunt
  activity (a pupil's √1.96 = −196 slip). Properties 1–3 (product,
  quotient, (√a)²ⁿ = aⁿ — with a short proof) are *stated*, not proved,
  except Property 3. Estimating roots two ways: nested bracketing to 3
  decimal places, and the formula √c = √(a²+b) ≈ a + b/(2a) (worked:
  √28 ≈ 5.3). §1.2 works in US vocabulary — **pure and mixed radicals**,
  index and radicand named; converting between them; like terms; adding/
  subtracting; multiplying with the **FOIL method** box; *Property 6*:
  denesting √(a ± 2√b) = √m ± √n, **with a printed proof**; *Property 7*:
  monotonicity, used to compare surds by squaring; rationalising
  denominators in printed step lists, first one-term, then two-term
  (conjugate) denominators. §1.3 y = √x as the inverse of y = x²
  (informally stated), domain/range found by guided investigation;
  "Fact: programmers write sqrt(x)"; applied items: tsunami speed
  v(x) = √(9.8x), a car's braking distance from a photograph.
- **Ch. 2 Quadratic equations** (§§2.1–2.3, pp.48–96). Unusual order:
  **trinomials before equations** (§2.1): factoring x²+bx+c by inspection
  (factor-pair diagrams), ax²+bx+c by grouping (the ac-method), an
  algebra-tile activity, completing the square as a factoring device.
  §2.2 solving: the forms ax² = 0, ax²+bx = 0, ax²+c = 0 separately, with
  the **Zero Product Property** named and the rule x² = a ⇒ x = ±√a
  boxed; then three methods for the general case — factoring, completing
  the square (6-step procedure), and the **quadratic formula derived in
  full** by completing the square on ax²+bx+c = 0. The discriminant Δ is
  defined with its three cases matched to graphs; parameter problems
  (find k/m/n for no/double/two roots) are worked. **Vieta's theorem is
  derived from the quadratic formula** (the sum and product computations
  are printed line by line); a Viète history box; a pair-work activity
  extends the pattern to a cubic. §2.3: equations with a modulus sign —
  ax²+b|x|+c = 0 and |ax²+bx|+c = 0 by sign cases with condition checks;
  rational equations P(x)/Q(x) = 0 (solve P = 0, exclude roots of Q; a
  note insists on checking numerator roots against the denominator);
  equations reducible to quadratic form by substitution (biquadratic,
  (3x+1)²-type, (x/(x+2))²-type); word problems built on "mathematical
  model" tables (real case vs changed case): two-pipe pool filling
  (1/x + 1/(x−5) = 1/6), a job-pay problem, motion problems, number/
  fraction problems (with a "black sheep in Kazakhstan" logic-joke box).
  Note: Problems 2.3 also set **irrational equations** (√(x−5) = 1,
  √x = x, …) although no section teaches them.
- **Ch. 3 Quadratic function** (§3.1, pp.98–124). Shifts y = ax²+k and
  y = a(x−h)² with boxed Properties; a QR code links to an Excel graphing
  video; vertex form y = a(x−h)²+k; the **vertex formula derived** by
  completing the square (h = −b/2a, k = (4ac−b²)/4a); x- and y-intercepts
  defined; the Δ-sign ↔ x-intercepts correspondence pictured; a 4-step
  "Sketching the Graph" routine; **the equation of a parabola** from
  vertex form, intercept form y = a(x−x₁)(x−x₂), or three points (worked
  with a simultaneous-equation solve; an Astana bridge photograph
  activity). Word problems: projectile motion h(t) = h₀ + v₀t − ½gt²
  (with the simplification g = 10 stated outright), room-temperature
  modelling, a **business application** (car-dealer earnings
  E(x) = −x² + 250x − 10000 million tenge: break-even at 50 and 200 cars,
  maximum at 125), an **architecture application** (arch height and
  whether an 18 m sailboat passes under it).
- **Ch. 4 Elements of statistics** (§§4.1–4.2, pp.126–140). §4.1 frequency
  polygons and histograms for discrete and interval data, **including
  frequency density nᵢ/dᵢ for unequal intervals** — the only book in the
  band to treat it; cumulative frequency and the cumulative (ogive)
  graph; a ceiling-function note (⌈x⌉) inside a grouping example;
  practice on UNT mock-exam scores and basketball heights. §4.2 mean;
  variance by a printed 5-step algorithm; standard deviation σ; comparing
  the spread of two data sets. No median/mode/quartiles (contrast
  Work 5's review chapter, Work 1's box plots).
- **Ch. 5 Inequalities** (§§5.1–5.3, pp.142–166). Interval notation is
  used from the first page. §5.1 solves quadratic inequalities by **sign
  charts with test values** (not by parabola sketches), the discriminant
  supplying the shortcut cases (Δ = 0, Δ < 0 sign tables); a driver
  reaction-time model V(x) = 0.005x² − 0.23x + 22 is solved graphically.
  §5.2 rational inequalities by a printed 5-step algorithm (one fraction,
  zeros of numerator and denominator, sign chart; an explicit warning
  never to multiply through by the denominator; even powers in a factor
  are "counted as 2", odd powers ignored). §5.3 systems — one linear +
  one quadratic, then two quadratics — solved on a **combined sign-chart
  with a "system" row** rather than paired number lines; a pond-plant
  growth activity. The chapter summary prints a 10-step sign-chart
  algorithm for all three topics.
- **Slips spotted in the book itself.** (1) §5.1's concept note prints
  the solution of x > a as x ∈ (−∞, a) and of x ≥ a as x ∈ (−∞, a] —
  the intervals run the wrong way (should be (a, +∞) / [a, +∞)). (2) The back cover's "Akhol Yelemessov" for
  Akzhol. (3) §1.3 calls y = √x "its inverse i.e. y = x or y = √x" —
  loose wording around inverse functions. None of these blocks use; all
  worked examples I checked (denesting, formula derivation, Vieta,
  business problem) are correct.
**Honest negatives.** No geometry; no probability; no systems of
quadratic *equations*; statistics lacks median/mode/box plots; the
irrational equations in Problems 2.3 have no supporting theory section.

### Work 10 — Turapbekov, Yelemessov, Kabylkayev, Shermagambet, Orynbassarova & Maksutkhanov, *Geometry. Grade 8. Textbook* (Astana-kitap, 2023), in English
**Imprint facts.** File 1584: Алматы: Астана-кітап, 2023, 198 pp, ISBN
978-601-80993-3-5. Bibliographic entry and back cover both put **Azat
Turapbekov first** (the record credited Yelemessov alone). Same design
family as Work 6 (worked Examples, PRACTICE, PROBLEMS A/B/C, trilingual
terminology, SUMMARY spreads, Answer key), but in US school-geometry
idiom: angle measures written m(∠A), segments "congruent", postulates
named (ASA, SAS, Angle Addition, Segment Addition, Cross Product
Property), and several theorems proved in **two-column
Statements/Reasons form**. The Grade-7 review (pp.8–13) mixes multiple
choice, fill-in definitions, true/false judgements ("a midline of a
triangle that joins midpoints of any pair of sides is parallel to the
third side — T or F?"), grid constructions, and circle problems
(tangents from an external point, an inscribed circle).

- **Ch. 1 Polygons. Study of quadrilaterals** (§§1.1–1.5, pp.14–74).
  §1.1 defines a polygon as a closed plane shape whose sides meet only
  at vertices; parts (side, vertex, diagonal), naming "in three
  different ways", the names table up to hendecagon/dodecagon/n-gon;
  equilateral/equiangular/regular; concavity tested by a diagonal lying
  outside. **Theorem 1** (interior sum (n−2)·180°) is built from a
  triangulation table; **Theorem 2** (exterior sum 360°) is **proved**
  (n·180° − (n−2)·180°). A Research item stages a two-pupil dispute over
  counting a decagon's diagonals. §1.2 the quadrilateral family chart
  (two pairs / one pair of parallel sides); Theorems 3–6 — parallelogram
  opposite sides, opposite angles, supplementary adjacent angles,
  bisecting diagonals — **each with a two-column proof**; examples fuse
  geometry with algebra (solve for x from side expressions; a 2×2 linear
  system for the halves of the diagonals). Rectangle (Theorem 7),
  rhombus (Property 1: diagonals bisect the angles; Theorem 8: diagonals
  perpendicular), square (properties listed; diagonal = a√2 derived).
  §1.3 **Theorem 9, the Triangle Proportionality Theorem, proved via AA
  similarity** (two-column), with its converse stated; **Theorem 10,
  Thales' theorem of parallel lines**, proved with parallelograms; then
  a fully illustrated 10-step construction dividing a segment into n
  equal parts; applications include a non-parallel shoe rack (converse
  in action) and shadow measurement. §1.4 the trapezoid is defined with
  "**exactly one pair** of parallel sides"; types right / isosceles /
  scalene; Theorem 11 (leg angles supplementary, paragraph proof),
  Theorem 12 (base angles of an isosceles trapezoid, numbered-step
  proof), Theorem 13 (its diagonals congruent, two-column); a Fact box
  explains the British/USA trapezium–trapezoid confusion with pictures.
  Midsegment of a triangle (Theorem 14, proved by SAS congruence plus a
  parallelogram) with a paper-folding activity and a Sierpinski-fractal
  research box; the trapezoid median (Theorem 15, two-column proof via
  ASA). §1.5 remarkable points: medians and the centroid (Property 3:
  the 2:1 ratio, with a proof sketch — reflect the triangle to a
  parallelogram and apply Thales); a physical activity finds a paper
  triangle's centre of gravity by balancing; angle bisectors and the
  incentre (Property 4); **Theorem 16, the Angle Bisector Theorem**
  (a bisector divides the opposite side proportionally to the adjacent
  sides), proved via Thales with an auxiliary parallel; altitudes and
  the orthocentre, with Property 5 (AO·OD = BO·OE = CO·OF) stated;
  perpendicular bisectors and the circumcentre.
- **Ch. 2 Metric relations in right triangles** (§§2.1–2.3, pp.76–112).
  §2.1 trigonometric ratios with the **SOH-CAH-TOA** mnemonic table;
  a measuring activity builds a class table of ratios at 30° and 55° to
  see they depend only on the angle. **Theorem 1 (Pythagoras) is proved
  by the altitude-to-hypotenuse projection method**; Theorem 2 (the
  converse) by constructing an equal right triangle; then the **Euclidean
  theorems**: Theorem 3 (altitude rule h² = p·q) and Theorem 4 (leg rule
  b² = p·c, a² = q·c), both proved from the ratios in the two
  sub-triangles; applications (ladder against a wall; the horizontal
  width of an 80 cm TV from its diagonal and height). §2.2 identities:
  Properties 1–4 (tan = sin/cos; cot = cos/sin; tan·cot = 1;
  sin²α + cos²α = 1 derived from Pythagoras), the **trig hexagon**
  mnemonic diagram, a 4-step guide to *verifying* identities,
  complementary-angle ratios derived and used to evaluate expressions
  without tables, "find the other ratios from one given ratio" by a
  printed 4-step draw-a-triangle method, and constructing an angle with
  a given sine (compass steps, sin θ = 3/5). §2.3 the special triangles
  (30°-60°-90° and 45°-45°-90° ratios derived from Pythagoras), then
  solving right triangles **with the printed trigonometric table**
  (p.109: sin/cos/tan for every degree 0–90°, 4 decimal places) — finding
  sides from an angle and inverse-looking-up angles from a ratio;
  applications: an airliner's glide path (tan 10° = 30000/x), river
  width by a 79° sighting, a grilled-cheese sandwich cut diagonally.
- **Ch. 3 Areas** (§§3.1–3.2, pp.114–150). §3.1 "polygonal region"
  defined (polygon ∪ interior); area as the count of unit squares;
  Properties 1–2 (congruent figures have equal areas; area adds over a
  split); grid estimation with half-squares. §3.2 is the band's richest
  area chapter: Theorem 1 (right triangle = ab/2, from the rectangle),
  Theorem 2 (any triangle = ½·base·height, by splitting on an altitude),
  Theorem 3 (equilateral triangle = a²√3/4), **Heron's formula as
  Theorem 4 — stated, not proved** (history box pairs Heron with the
  Chinese *Nine Chapters* tradition), Theorem 5 (A = ½ab·sin C, via
  h = b·sin C), **Theorem 6 (A = p·r, inradius — proved** by splitting
  into three triangles), **Theorem 7 (A = abc/4R, circumradius)** —
  derived by quoting "the law of sines tells us a/sin A = 2R", a result
  **nowhere taught in this book** (a real gap to note for lesson
  building); Theorem 8 (parallelogram = base × height, cut-and-paste
  proof), Theorem 9 (A = ab·sin A), Theorem 10 (for an interior point P:
  A(PAB) + A(PCD) = ½A(ABCD), proved), rhombus area three ways
  (a·h; a²·sin A; ½d₁d₂), Theorem 11 (trapezoid = median × height).
  Problems 3.2 runs to ~99 items, many tagged **(UNT)** — genuine
  Unified National Test questions — and one tagged (SAT).
- **Ch. 4 Rectangular coordinate system on a plane** (§4.1, pp.152–180).
  The deepest coordinate treatment in the band, all in one long section:
  Theorem 1 distance between two points (proved); Theorem 2 midpoint
  (proved with congruent triangles on the grid); **Theorem 3, the
  section formula** for a point dividing a segment in ratio k (proved
  via similar triangles), with external division noted (k < 0) and a
  Kazakhstan-geography activity (Kyzylorda–Almaty–Karaganda–Semey route
  choice by distances); slope m = (y₂−y₁)/(x₂−x₁) with a four-picture
  slope-types summary and the note m = tan α (obtuse case
  m = −tan(180°−α)); **Theorem 4, point-slope form** (proved);
  **Theorem 5, two-point form** (proved by equal slopes); **Theorem 6,
  the circle's standard equation** (x−a)² + (y−b)² = r² (from the
  distance definition), then circles tangent to the x-axis (r = |b|),
  to the y-axis (r = |a|), and to both axes with the equation written
  per quadrant; a circle through two points with its centre on a given
  line is solved in full. Problems 4.1 adds mixed items in function
  notation (lines f(x), g(x)) and transformation items (a circle
  translated, reflected, enlarged).
- **Slips/gaps spotted.** (1) **Theorem 7 (rectangle diagonals, p.31):
  the printed two-column "proof" actually proves Theorem 4** (opposite
  angles of a parallelogram) — its goal line reads "we need to show
  ∠A ≅ ∠C" and it never touches the diagonals; the congruent-diagonals
  fact itself is true and used correctly later, but the proof printed
  for it is the wrong proof, copied from §1.2. (2) Theorem 7 of Ch. 3
  (A = abc/4R) rests on the law of sines, which the book never states
  or proves. (3) The contents page misprints §3.1's page numbers
  (concept of area "114" vs properties "112"). Everything else I checked
  (proofs of Theorems 2, 9, 10, 14, 15, 16; Heron and inradius examples;
  the tangent-circle examples) computes correctly.
**Honest negatives.** No vectors; no circle theorems (inscribed angles
appear only inside Grade-7 review problems); no similarity chapter —
similarity is *used* (AA postulate, §1.3 and §4.1 proofs) but never
developed; no area of a circle or sector; no Pick-type lattice counting;
trigonometry stops at 90° (no 0°–180° extension, contrast Works 7/9);
the law of sines/cosines are absent (yet silently used once, above).

---

### Band synthesis (all 10 works)
Grade 8 in Kazakhstan runs **four parallel textbook lines**, all covering
the same state curriculum skeleton, plus the NIS line and the new English
line:
- **Algebra** everywhere = square roots/irrationals → quadratic equations
  → quadratic function → statistics (descriptive only) → inequalities.
  Differences are of depth: NIS adds systems-vs-collections of
  inequalities, box plots and ogives; Mektep (Work 2) is the leanest;
  Shynybeкov adds an optional number-theory chapter; Soltan adds periodic-
  decimal theory and Bolzano's theorem;
  the English book (Work 6) is the only one to teach denesting with a
  proof, FOIL, frequency density, and sign-chart (test-value) methods.
- **Geometry** everywhere = quadrilaterals → right-triangle relations →
  areas → coordinates. Smirnov (Work 7) proves Pythagoras three times by
  three methods; Soltan (Work 9) derives it from the cosine definition;
  Shynybeкov (Work 8) is the most compact; the English book (Work 10) is
  the only one with two-column proofs, the angle-bisector theorem,
  Euclidean (projection) theorems, Heron, A = p·r and A = abc/4R.
- **Trapezoid is defined exclusively** ("exactly one pair of parallel
  sides") in all four geometry lines — a uniform national convention
  that differs from the inclusive definition used in some other
  countries' books.
- **No probability anywhere in Grade 8**; statistics is purely
  descriptive in every line. No vectors, no circle theorems, no
  similarity theory (only proportional segments as a tool) in any line.

---

### Citation check — Band E (2026-11-04)

Checker ≠ reader: every anchor below was verified by rendering the exact
PDF page (pdftoppm -jpeg -r 100) and viewing it, using the PDF↔printed
mapping stated in the draft for that work (or derived from the
cover/imprint where the draft states none).

**Totals: 199 anchors checked — 193 PASSED, 4 CORRECTED, 2 NOT FOUND.**
All 10 works were covered; no work remains unchecked.

Per-work counts (checked / passed / corrected / not found):
- Work 1 — NIS *Mathematics* Pt 1+2: 22 / 21 / 1 / 0
- Work 2 — Abylkasymova et al., *Algebra* (Mektep): 21 / 21 / 0 / 0
- Work 3 — *Algebra: Electronic Supplement* (teacher aid): 15 / 14 / 0 / 1
- Work 4 — Shynybekov et al., *Algebra* (Atamura): 20 / 19 / 1 / 0
- Work 5 — Soltan et al., *Algebra* (Keleshek-2030): 21 / 20 / 0 / 1
- Work 6 — Yelemessov et al., *Algebra* (English, Astana-kitap): 20 / 19 / 1 / 0
- Work 7 — Smirnov & Tuyakov, *Geometry* (Mektep): 22 / 22 / 0 / 0
- Work 8 — Shynybekov et al., *Geometry* (Atamura): 19 / 18 / 1 / 0
- Work 9 — Soltan et al., *Geometry* (Keleshek-2030): 19 / 19 / 0 / 0
- Work 10 — Turapbekov et al., *Geometry* (English, Astana-kitap): 20 / 20 / 0 / 0

Corrections made (draft fixed in place):

1. **Work 1 — page mapping wrong (CORRECTED).** Draft said "Printed page =
   PDF page − 2 in Part 1". Evidence: in file 1077 the contents page is
   PDF p.3 and is printed "3"; §1.1 begins on PDF p.8, printed "8". In
   Part 2 (file 1080) §5.1 is on PDF p.6, printed "6". Mapping in both
   parts is printed page = PDF page; the sentence was rewritten.
2. **Work 3 — «Карусель» routine NOT FOUND.** The supplement's own list of
   critical-thinking techniques (Russian ed., printed p.3; Kazakh ed.
   likewise) names «Верю — не верю», «Согласен — Не согласен»,
   «До — После», «Зигзаг», «Вращающаяся станция», «Снежный ком», «Пазл»,
   «Ромашка Блума», «Инсерт» — no «Карусель» anywhere in the list or on
   the rendered card pages (pp.4–8, 20–21). Draft now reads
   «Вращающаяся станция».
3. **Work 4 — page mapping wrong (CORRECTED).** Draft said the Kazakh
   edition has "PDF page = printed page + 1". Evidence: in file 300 the
   title page is PDF p.1, the Grade-7 review on PDF p.4 is printed "4",
   the contents on PDF p.190 is printed "190", and §6.3 (GCD/LCM) on
   PDF p.168 is printed "168". Mapping is PDF page = printed page; the
   sentence was rewritten.
4. **Work 5 — §13 "exponential equation" NOT FOUND; coded-problem details
   wrong (CORRECTED).** No worked exponential equation (36ˣ − 7·6ˣ + 2 = 0,
   t = 6ˣ) exists in §13: the worked examples on printed pp.84–85 are
   biquadratic, x⁶-type, modulus and a product form, and the exercises on
   pp.86–89 contain no exponential equation either. The "coded" problem
   is №311 (p.87): the equation is 36x² − 1996|x| + 178 = 0, and its
   constants are the Ile-Alatau park's opening year (1996), its Red-Book
   plant species (36) and its bird species (178) — 178 is not a year.
   The §13 passage, and the matching "exponential-equation cameo" phrase
   in the Band synthesis, were rewritten/removed.
5. **Work 6 — §5.1 slip misquoted (CORRECTED).** Printed p.142 reads:
   x > a "shortly written as x ∈ (−∞, a)" and for x ≥ a "that is
   x ∈ (−∞, a]". The draft quoted the second interval as [−∞, a] and
   claimed −∞ is given a square bracket; the book prints a round bracket
   at −∞. The slip entry was corrected to the book's exact text (the
   book's error is the reversed direction only).
6. **Work 8 — §1.8 wrongly starred (CORRECTED).** The Kazakh contents
   (printed pp.154–155) lists 1.8 (inscribed/circumscribed quadrilaterals)
   with no asterisk; in Ch. 1 the asterisk sits on 1.7.5* (excircles).
   The draft's "§1.8\*" was changed to "§1.8" in the chapter list, the
   section text and the honest-negatives line.

Spot-verified highlights that PASSED verbatim: Work 1 Pt 2 §7.1 data-taxonomy
chart (printed p.104) and §9.1 distance formula (p.186); Work 2 §1's
full √2-irrationality proof (p.16) and §17's D(X) = mean of squares −
square of mean with the worked D(X) = 0.61 (p.138), print run 65,000
(colophon); Work 4 §6.3's worked GCD(680, 612) = 68 (p.169) and the

copy ending at the subject index, printed p.143; Work 8's placeholder

## Kazakhstan MATHS — Band F: Grades 9–10 (first read)

Band list: 17 files, 3,446 PDF pages. All PDFs are image-only scans (the only embedded text is the repeated OKULYK.KZ watermark banner on every page), so every file was verified and read from rendered pages.

### STEP 1 — Cover/imprint verification and clustering

**Rule applied:** (a) same edition + language + part in several print-files = one work; (b) Kazakh vs Russian editions of the same series + part = the same work in two language editions — maths content filed once per part, both editions listed, units spot-checked in both; (c) different years/editions = distinct works; (d) kind judged from the book's own pages.

**Headline finding of this band: there are NO true re-scan pairs in it.** Every pair the record holds twice turned out, from the imprints, to be the **Kazakh and Russian language editions of the same 2019 edition** — different ISBNs, slightly different pagination. The pairs' tables of contents align section-for-section, and spot-checked interior units (Abylkasymova §19 in KZ Pt 2 vs RU Pt 2 — identical text and problems, translated; Smirnov Geometry 9 grade-8 review exercises — item-for-item identical; Soltan Algebra 9 trig-table appendices — identical) confirm the content is the same work in two languages. Content is therefore filed once per work, with both editions cited.

**GAP STATEMENT (top of the in-hand set):** The in-hand Kazakhstan set contains **no Grade 10 Algebra at all, and no Grade 11 or Grade 12 mathematics of any kind**. Grade 10 is represented only by two geometry-side books: a humanities-track Grade 10 Geometry textbook (W8) and a stereometry problem-solving manual keyed to a Grade 10 Geometry textbook that is itself not in hand (W9). This is a library-side gap, documented here, not something this read can fix.
