### W1 — Yelemessov et al., *Algebra. Grade 9. Textbook* (ENGLISH)

**As printed:** Akzhol Yelemessov, Nurbol Amanov, Saule Kattabekova, Smail Abibulla, Kuanysh Nurgazin, Zhumadulla Abdulkhakov, Anuar Nasyr, Akhmet Orazalin, Azat Turapbekov. Almaty: Astana-kitap (Астана-кітап), 2023. 234 pp. ISBN 978-601-80993-5-9. UDC 373.167.1; LBK 22.14 я72. "Recommended by the Ministry of Education of the Republic of Kazakhstan." Language: English throughout, with a trilingual terminology box (English/Kazakh/Russian) on most teaching pages. File 1586; printed page numbers = PDF page numbers.

**Shape of the book:** Five chapters, each split into numbered sections; every section teaches through fully worked "Example" items each followed by a matching "Practice" item, plus Fact/History/Research/Activity side boxes and QR codes to GeoGebra tasks. At the end of each section a "PROBLEMS" set graded A (basic) / B (medium) / C (hard); at the end of each chapter a one-spread SUMMARY restating every definition and formula. It opens with Review Exercises for 5–8th grades (pp. 8–12, graded A/B/C: powers and standard form, simplifying, linear equations and simultaneous equations, graphs and gradient/intercept, area word problems, a population-pyramid chart question) and closes with Review Exercises for 9th Grade (pp. 221–225) and a full Answer Key (pp. 226–232; some items marked "left for students"). References (p. 233) cite the authors' own Pre-Algebra/Algebra books and Singapore's *New Syllabus Mathematics* — the book's style follows that Singapore model.

**Chapter 1 — Equations, inequalities in two variables and their systems (pp. 14–42).**
- §1.1 (p. 14): linear equation in two variables defined; a solution is an ordered pair, checked by substitution (Examples 1–5). Non-linear equations in two variables (p. 17): degree of an equation = highest degree of its terms; the circle equation (x−a)² + (y−b)² = R² is given in a Note and used in examples. Systems of non-linear equations (p. 19): solution set = intersection points of the graphs. Two named methods, each as a numbered 4-step algorithm: **Substitution Method** (p. 20) and **Elimination Method** (p. 23), each with worked examples including a 4-solution system (line × circle type outcomes) and checking of candidate pairs. Word problems using systems (p. 25): two-step method — "make mathematical model", then "work with mathematical model and answer" — worked on a two-digit-number problem, a right-triangle legs/hypotenuse (Pythagoras) problem, and a fraction problem in which an extra given condition is used to reject one of the two algebraic solutions. Problems 1.1 (p. 30, A/B/C): degrees, circle equations, solve systems by a named method, rectangle/TV-screen dimensions, true/false statements about numbers of intersection points.
- §1.2 (p. 32): inequalities in two variables. Linear inequality: graph = a half-plane; 3-step sketching method (boundary line, dashed if < or >, solid if ≤ or ≥; test point, usually (0,0); shade the side that works). Quadratic inequality in two variables (p. 34): parabola as boundary, vertex found from h = −b/2a, intercepts tabulated, interior/exterior regions tested. Systems of non-linear inequalities (p. 37): solve each, take the overlap; examples include xy < 0 handled by quadrants. Problems 1.2 (p. 40): "is the pair a solution", write an inequality describing a half-plane/quadrant, sketch systems; Summary p. 42.

**Chapter 2 — Elements of combinatorics (pp. 44–72).**
- §2.1 (p. 44): counting by the **Sum Rule** (m + n for mutually exclusive actions) and **Product Rule** (m·n), each introduced by a story example (cinema halls; a Kazakh/Uzbek restaurant menu). Factorial (p. 46): n!, 0! = 1, simplifying factorial fractions. Permutation (p. 47): P(n,n) = n!, then P(n,r) = n!/(n−r)!. Combination (p. 50): C(n,r) = n!/((n−r)!r!), built by comparing 2-element permutations vs combinations of a 3-set; a Note warns that a "combination lock" is not a combination. Problems 2.1 (p. 52): fill-in definitions, evaluate P/C expressions, route/meal/clothing counting, "LOGARITHMS" letter arrangements.
- §2.2 (p. 54): solving problems with the formulas by an explicit 3-step routine — decide permutation vs combination, fix n and r, apply the formula. Worked: password counting P(26,10), bookshelf arrangements split by subject, team selection C(4,3)·C(6,4), "at least 3 letters" case-splitting, defective-bulb selections with "at least half defective" case sums. Problems 2.2 (p. 60, A/B/C) include the "Satti Zhuldyz" lottery and a 52-card deck set with many part-conditions.
- §2.3 (p. 64): Pascal's triangle built from (a±b)⁰…³ expansions, with history (Omar Khayyam, Chu Shih Chieh); seven listed properties of a binomial expansion; Newton's binomial via combination coefficients (p. 67); the r-th term rule; finding a specified term, a coefficient, the constant term, sum of coefficients (substitute 1). Problems 2.3 (p. 70, A/B/C) up to "rational terms" in (√x + 2∛x)⁷-type expansions. Summary p. 72.

**Chapter 3 — Number sequences (pp. 74–130).**
- §3.1 (p. 74): sequence, term, general term aₙ; finite vs infinite; finding terms from a formula and guessing a formula from first terms (incl. Fibonacci). Criteria for existence of a sequence (p. 75): a formula undefined at some natural n is not a general term. Monotone and piecewise sequences (p. 77): increasing/decreasing tested by the sign of aₙ₊₁ − aₙ. Recursively defined sequences (p. 78): unwinding a recurrence to a general term and back. Methods of mathematical induction (p. 80): dome/domino motivation, two-step principle (check n = 1; assume n = k, prove n = k+1), one full worked proof of 1+2+…+n = n(n+1)/2; practice proofs (sum of odd numbers = n²; divisibility). Problems 3.1 (p. 82).
- §3.2 Arithmetic sequence (p. 84): common difference d; general term aₙ = a₁ + (n−1)d; "advanced" general term aₙ = aₚ + (n−p)d; middle-term (arithmetic mean) formula aₚ = (aₚ₋ₖ + aₚ₊ₖ)/2; sum of the first n terms Sₙ = (a₁+aₙ)/2 · n with a pairing proof, and the 2a₁+(n−1)d form. Applied problems (p. 94): concert-hall seating, population growth modelled as an arithmetic sequence, tomato harvest, well-drilling cost. Problems 3.2 (p. 96, A/B/C).
- §3.3 Geometric sequence (p. 100): common ratio q (motivation: knockout tournament halving); general term bₙ = b₁qⁿ⁻¹; advanced form bₙ = bₖqⁿ⁻ᵏ; middle-term (geometric mean) rule bₚ₋ₖ·bₚ₊ₖ = bₚ²; sum Sₙ = b₁(1−qⁿ)/(1−q) with the shift-and-subtract proof; mixed arithmetic+geometric problems (p. 115); applied problems (p. 116): salary with 10% yearly rise (compound growth as a GP), bouncing ball. Problems 3.3 (p. 118).
- §3.4 Infinite geometric sequence; solving word problems (p. 122): partial sums of 1/2+1/4+… computed and graphed to motivate the limit informally; **infinite sum formula** S = b₁/(1−q) for −1 < q < 1 (stated as "Theorem 2"); repeating decimals converted to fractions via the formula (0.18̄ = 2/11 worked; 1.24̄ split into non-repeating + repeating parts). Problems 3.4 (p. 128): infinite sums, nested-squares/semicircles totals, pendulum distance. Summary p. 130.

**Chapter 4 — Trigonometry (pp. 132–204).**
- §4.1 (p. 132): radian defined from arc length (α = m/r; arc = rα); conversion D/180° = R/π with a table of standard angles; marking numbers on the unit circle; quadrant location of angles incl. angles > 360° and radian measures like 9π/4. Worked applications: bicycle chainring/cog rotation. Problems 4.1 (p. 140).
- §4.2 (p. 142): sin, cos, tan, cot of an angle in standard position from a point (x, y) with d = √(x²+y²): sin α = y/d etc. ("Soh Cah Toa" note); sign tables by quadrant; the relation between coordinates of a point on the unit circle and trig functions (x = cos α, y = sin α), with the full special-angle unit-circle diagram (Fig. 4.2.9). Problems 4.2 (p. 148).
- §4.3 (p. 150): the four trig *functions* one by one — sine, cosine, tangent, cotangent — each defined from the unit circle, with domain, range, behaviour in each quadrant, and period (2π for sin/cos; π for tan/cot, shown geometrically); then periods of function in general; even and odd trig functions (p. 161): cos even, sin/tan/cot odd, from the unit-circle figure. Worked examples are dominated by evaluating expressions at multiples of π and finding ranges of a·sin x + b type functions, and domains of composite trig functions. Problems 4.3 (p. 162). NOTE: the functions' *graphs* (sine curve etc.) are never drawn — all properties are read off the unit circle.
- §4.4 Trigonometric formulas (p. 164): sum and difference formulas for sin and cos derived (sin via a triangle-area argument; cos from sin via the co-function relation), then tan and cot; simple trig equations appear only inside examples (solve on 0 ≤ x ≤ 2π). Double-angle formulas (p. 174) for sin/cos (three forms)/tan/cot; half-angle formulas (p. 180) with sign choice by quadrant; reduction formulas (p. 183) derived from sum/difference formulas, tabulated by quadrant, with a 5-step reduction algorithm and ASTC-type memory note; sum-to-product (p. 188) and product-to-sum (p. 194) formulas for sin/cos/tan/cot pairs; simplifying trig expressions (p. 196). Problems 4.4 (p. 200, A/B/C) — very long simplification/evaluation sets. Summary p. 204.

**Chapter 5 — Elements of probability theory (p. 206).** One section only, §5.1 (p. 206): experiment, outcome, sample space, event; sure (certain) and impossible events; mutually exclusive events; **classical definition** P(E) = n(E)/n(S) for equally likely outcomes; simple vs compound events (examples only — dice sums via a 6×6 table, coins, cards, salary/subject frequency tables worked as relative counts). "Solving written problems" (p. 212) is **geometric probability**: probability = desired area / total area (dartboard worked), also length ratio on a segment; a Battleship activity closes the section. Problems 5.1 (p. 216, A/B/C): dice/coin/card classical problems, two hiring-probability items, combinatorics-counting items (Alim's clothes) re-used as counting practice. Summary p. 220.

**Honest negatives (W1):** no statistical (frequency-limit) definition of probability — the "statistical definition" exists in the other G9 books but not here; no addition/multiplication rules for probabilities of events, no independent events, no conditional probability, no expected value. No trigonometric equations as a taught topic (a handful of solve-on-an-interval examples sit inside §4.4); no inverse trig functions; no graphs of y = sin x etc.; secant/cosecant are never defined in the taught text yet appear in a few end-of-book review items (Review for 9th Grade Q12 uses sec 300°, csc 1230°). No logarithms, no exponential functions, no vectors, no coordinate geometry beyond the circle equation note in §1.1.

**Slips spotted in the book itself:** (1) The Chapter 4 opener spread (p. 131) lists the chapter's sections as "4.2 ARITHMETIC SEQUENCE · 4.3 GEOMETRIC SEQUENCE · 4.4 INFINITE GEOMETRIC SEQUENCE" — a copy-over from the Chapter 3 opener; the real §4.2–4.4 are the trigonometry sections of the TOC. (2) In §4.3, the cosine-function spread (p. 153) says "Since the cosine value of α is the abscissa of the point C, the x-axis can also be called the **sine axis**" — should read *cosine axis* (the sine spread correctly calls the y-axis the sine axis). (3) Small wording slip in §5.1: single-outcome events {e₁}, {e₂}, … are called "sure event[s]" in the definition paragraph (p. 206) where "simple/elementary event" is meant; the Chapter 5 Summary (p. 220) repeats the same "sure event" wording under its SURE heading, while also defining "simple event" correctly in a separate box on the same page.

**Copy state:** complete — all 234 printed pages present in order, answers and references included; scan clean and fully legible. This is the only English-language book in the band.

### W2 — Shynybekov et al., *Algebra 9* (Atamura, 2019) — Kazakh + Russian editions

**As printed:** Ә. Н. Шыныбеков, Д. Ә. Шыныбеков, Р. Н. Жұмабаев. General editor M. Otelbaev.
- Kazakh edition (file 385): Алматы: Атамұра, 2019, 240 pp., ISBN 978-601-331-571-3.
- Russian edition (file 436): Алматы: Атамұра, 2019, 224 pp., ISBN 978-601-331-600-0.
Read whole in the Kazakh edition; the Russian edition was verified as the same work (TOCs align section-for-section; spot-check of printed pp. 23–24 of the Russian file shows the same worked Examples 5–7 on symmetric systems as Kazakh pp. 26–27, translated, with pagination shifted ≈3 pages — the Russian typesetting is slightly denser, hence 224 vs 240 pp.). Content below is filed once; page numbers are the Kazakh edition's.

**Shape:** the most proof-heavy and exercise-dense of the three G9 algebra textbooks. Theory is stated compactly, formulas are *proved* (often by induction), and each section carries long numbered exercise runs graded A / B / C, with C reaching olympiad-flavoured problems; starred (⁎) items are harder still. Every chapter ends with a trilingual (Kazakh/Russian/English) terminology glossary. Answers pp. 221–237 (some C answers are full worked solutions). The book explicitly says (preface) it also covers the advanced-track programme, with C-group tasks aimed at maths-stream classes.

**Opening review — Grade 8 material (pp. 4–15).** Question list (18 recall questions on roots, quadratics, Vieta, inequalities) then drill sets 0.17–0.59: factorising, clearing irrationality in denominators, quadratic equations incl. parameter problems and problems *proving* root formulas, biquadratic and fractional equations, irrational equations, modulus equations |x−3|+2|x+1| = 4 type, graphing functions (incl. y = 0.5x² shifts), sign-constancy intervals, domains, extrema — a much harder review than W1's.

**Chapter 1 — Equations, inequalities in two variables and their systems (pp. 16–45).**
- §1.1 (p. 16): equations in two variables introduced through a table pairing known functions (linear, quadratic, inverse proportionality, circle) with their equations; degree of an equation; the general second-degree form ax² + bxy + cy² + dx + ey + k = 0; geometric meaning = the graph; recognising a circle by completing the square. Exercises 1.1–1.22 (A/B/C) include graphing equations with moduli (|y| = x, y = |x| − 5) and a parameter problem (vertex of y = x² + mx on a given point).
- §1.2 (p. 23): solving systems. Methods, each with numbered worked examples: substitution; algebraic addition (elimination); for **symmetric systems**, Vieta's theorem (x, y as roots of z² − pz + q = 0 — Examples 4–5); new-variable substitution u = x², v = y² (Example 6); the ratio substitution x/y = z for near-homogeneous systems (Example 7, giving four solution pairs). Exercises 1.23–1.40: systems with radicals and fractions, and parameter problems 1.39*–1.40* ("for which values of a does the system have exactly one solution?").
- §1.3 (p. 32): word problems modelled by systems — two-digit numbers, fencing a 600 m² plot (perimeter 420... solved by Vieta), two tractors ploughing (joint-work systems with 1/t₁ + 1/t₂ = 1/t), boat-and-current motion, trains, alloys (aluminium–magnesium). Exercises 1.43–1.66.
- §1.4 (p. 39): inequalities in two variables — solution = a region of the plane (half-plane, disc interior/exterior); systems of inequalities, incl. a worked example cutting out a triangle from three lines and a C-set (1.67–1.84) sketching regions bounded by parabolas, hyperbolas xy ≤ 1, and modulus inequalities. Review exercises and glossary close the chapter.

**Chapter 2 — Elements of combinatorics (pp. 46–63).**
- §2.1 Sum rule (p. 46) — taught *through set counting*: Theorem 1 proves the inclusion–exclusion count n(A∪B) = n(A) + n(B) − n(A∩B) with Euler–Venn diagrams, extended to three sets; worked on overlapping sports-participation counts.
- §2.2 Product rule (p. 48): Theorem 2, n(A×B) = n(A)·n(B), with table and tree-diagram solutions.
- §2.3 Arrangements **with repetition** (p. 50): Āₙᵏ = nᵏ, proved; §2.4 arrangements without repetition and permutations (p. 51): factorial (0! = 1 noted), Aₙᵏ = n!/(n−k)!, Pₙ = n!, both proved by the product rule.
- §2.5 Combinations without repetition (p. 53): Theorem 3, Cₙᵏ = n!/(k!(n−k)!), derived from Aₙᵏ = Pₖ·Cₙᵏ.
- §2.6 Newton's binomial and its properties (p. 54): expansion built from combination coefficients; Pascal's triangle; properties proved: Cₙᵏ = Cₙⁿ⁻ᵏ, the Pascal rule Cₙ₊₁ᵏ⁺¹ = Cₙᵏ + Cₙᵏ⁺¹ (proved by counting lattice paths on a grid), ΣCₙᵏ = 2ⁿ. Exercises 2.1–2.50 (A/B/C): counting in context (chess tournaments, logarifm letter words, book swaps with dependence), identities to prove (2.43–2.46: hockey-stick-type sums, Σ(Cₙᵏ)² = C₂ₙⁿ), equations in Cₙᵏ (2.45).

**Chapter 3 — Sequences (pp. 64–111).**
- §3.1 (p. 64): sequence and general term; ways of defining (formula, recursion — Fibonacci); §3.1.3 monotone sequences **and bounded sequences** (bounded above/below, with proofs that specific sequences are increasing/decreasing/bounded via aₙ₊₁ − aₙ). Exercises 3.1–3.20.
- §3.2* Mathematical induction principle (p. 72, starred/optional in the TOC): complete vs incomplete induction, with a deliberately false "proof" dissected (the claim that n² + n + 41 is always prime fails at n = 41); the principle stated formally with A(1), A(k) ⇒ A(k+1); worked proofs: sum of odd numbers, a telescoping sum = n/(n+1), Bernoulli's inequality 2ⁿ > n² for n > 5. Exercises 3.21–3.31 include proving Σk² and Σk³ formulas and (1+h)ⁿ ≥ 1 + nh.
- §3.3 Arithmetic progression (p. 80): nth-term formula *proved by induction*; characteristic property aₙ = (aₙ₋₁ + aₙ₊₁)/2; exercises 3.32–3.55 (inserting arithmetic means, systems in a₁ and d, the [xₙ] > 0 inequality problem).
- §3.4 Geometric progression (p. 86): nth-term formula proved by induction; characteristic property aₙ² = aₙ₋₁·aₙ₊₁ (signed form discussed); exercises 3.56–3.76.
- §3.5 (p. 91): sum of the first n terms of AP (pairing proof) and GP (shift-subtract proof) in one section; exercises 3.77–3.102 mix both, incl. word problems and "which sequence" reasoning.
- §3.6 Infinitely decreasing geometric progression (p. 99): |q| < 1, the informal limit qⁿ → 0, sum S = b/(1−q) stated as a theorem with the Sₙ-limit argument; sums of nested squares'/triangles' areas as creative tasks; **periodic decimals** converted incl. mixed periods — 2.7(31), 0.2(3), 1.21(32) — and the condition |（1−x)/x| < 1 solved for a series to be an infinite GP. Chapter supplement (pp. 107–111): hard C problems — limits of nested radicals xₙ = √(2 + √(2 + …)) proved to have limit 2, equations whose left side is an infinite series (3.115). History spread on Gauss and the chessboard legend (pp. 97–98).

**Chapter 4 — Trigonometry (pp. 112–175).**
- §4.1 (p. 112): angles beyond 360° and negative angles via a rotating radius-vector; radian measure defined (arc = radius), 1 rad = 180°/π ≈ 57°17′45″, conversion formulas both ways, marking 0, π/6, π/4 … 2π on the *trigonometric circle*. Exercises 4.1–4.19 (incl. arc-length and clock-hand problems).
- §4.2 (p. 119): sin/cos/tg/ctg of an arbitrary angle defined as y/R, x/R, y/x, x/y for a point on a circle of radius R; domains and ranges of the four functions; the basic identity sin²x + cos²x = 1 derived; full table of values 0°–360°. Exercises 4.20–4.37.
- §4.3 Properties (p. 128): signs by quadrant; even/odd functions treated **in general** first (f(−x) = f(x) / −f(x), graph symmetry) then applied (cos even; sin, tg, ctg odd); least positive period, incl. periods of composite and fractional-part functions (worked: the period of y = {x/3} is 3). Exercises 4.38–4.60 include "for which α does sin α = 1 / 0 / −1 hold" (4.51) — the nearest the book comes to trig equations.
- §4.4 Reduction formulas (p. 137): co-function theorem for complementary angles proved on the circle, then the full table for π/2 ± α, π ± α, 3π/2 ± α, 2π − α; two memorisation rules (name changes only for odd multiples of π/2; sign by quadrant). Exercises 4.61–4.77.
- §4.5 Trigonometric formulas (p. 146): 4.5.1 basic identities (tg α = sin α/cos α, 1 + tg²α = 1/cos²α, 1 + ctg²α = 1/sin²α) used in simplification and identity proofs; 4.5.2 addition formulas — cos(α − β) **proved with the scalar (dot) product of vectors**, the rest derived, tg/ctg of a sum included; an application finds the max of cos α − √3 sin α by rewriting as 2cos(α + π/6); 4.5.3 double-angle formulas, with sin 3α and cos 3α derived as an example; 4.5.4 half-angle formulas (sin²(α/2) = (1 − cos α)/2 etc. and tg(α/2) = sin α/(1 + cos α) forms); 4.5.5 sum-to-product; 4.5.6 product-to-sum. A practice box notes the older notations sec α = 1/cos α, cosec α = 1/sin α. Exercises 4.78–4.173 — the longest run in the book — plus a chapter supplement (4.147–4.173) with max/min of trig expressions and triangle-angle identities (4.155, 4.160). History spreads: Thales, Ulugh Beg, al-Khwarizmi, Regiomontanus, Euler (pp. 148–149), and a note on ISS orientation by trig computation (p. 145).

**Chapter 5 — Elements of probability theory (pp. 176–207).**
- §5.1.1 (p. 177): elementary events and the sample space U; certain (ақиқат) and impossible (жалған) events; §5.1.2 **operations on events** — sum (union), product (intersection), difference, complement Ā — pictured with Euler–Venn diagrams, with identities proved (Ā + B = Ā·B̄ and A·B̄ = Ā + B̄, i.e. De Morgan-type laws), and shooter-hit problems expressed in event algebra (1.1–1.3 style worked examples with AB + ĀBC + … expansions). Exercises 5.1–5.23.
- §5.1.3 (p. 184): **classical definition** P(A) = m/n. §5.1.4 (p. 185): **statistical definition** — relative frequency m/n stabilising; Karl Pearson's 24,000 coin tosses (12,012 heads → 0.5005) quoted. §5.1.5 (p. 187): properties, each stated and used: 0 ≤ P(A) ≤ 1; addition rule for incompatible events; P(Ā) = 1 − P(A); **multiplication rule for independent events**; general addition rule P(A+B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(AB); **conditional probability** P_B(A) with P(A·B) = P(B)·P_B(A) proved from counts, and the three-event extension. Worked: two shooters hitting with 0.7/0.8 → P(at least one hit) = 0.94 via complements. Exercises 5.24–5.81 (A/B/C): quality-control sampling, shooters, machine/tool failure probabilities (series-system reliability flavour, e.g. 5.59–5.64), letter-frequency counts in Kazakh texts (5.28–5.31).
- §5.2 Geometric probability (p. 197): P = S_G/S (area ratio; length/volume analogues noted); worked examples: point in a square-with-inscribed figures, and the classic **meeting problem** — two workers arrive independently within an hour, each waits ≤ some minutes — solved by graphing the region |x − y| conditions as a system of inequalities in a 60×60 square (a direct application of Chapter 1's inequality systems). Exercises 5.82–5.99 incl. Buffon-style broken-stick problem 5.94 and dart/segment problems; chapter supplement 5.100–5.121 adds De Méré's dice problem retold in the history spread (pp. 205–206: Pascal, Fermat, Laplace, Kolmogorov...).

**Chapter 6 — Review exercises for the Grades VII–IX course (pp. 208–220)**, by topic: 6.1 divisibility and whole numbers; 6.2 rational/irrational numbers, square roots; 6.3 short-multiplication formulas; 6.4 quadratic equations and Vieta; 6.5 polynomials and their roots (incl. polynomial division with remainder, 6.32–6.38); 6.6 equations and systems; 6.7 proving inequalities (AM–GM style, 6.44); 6.8 solving inequalities, interval method; 6.9 integer/rational exponents; 6.10 investigating functions and graphing; 6.11 sequences and progressions (incl. prove-the-formula items 6.66–6.68); 6.12 trigonometric expressions (incl. 6.82: for which a is x = π/6 a root of a trig equation in x — again equation-flavoured but not a taught method).

**Honest negatives (W2):** no trigonometric equations or inequalities as a taught topic (only isolated "which angle gives sin α = 1" items); no graphs of the trig functions as curves (periodicity is handled on the circle and by definition, sine-wave graphs are never drawn); no inverse trig functions; no Bernoulli scheme / repeated-trials formula despite the rich probability chapter; no expected value or random variables; no logarithms. Secant/cosecant appear only as a historical-notation note.

**Copy state:** Kazakh file complete (240 pp., answers and both TOC leaves present). Russian file complete at 224 pp. No missing or duplicated leaves seen in either.

### W3 — Abylkasymova et al., *Algebra 9* in two parts (Mektep, 2019) — Kazakh + Russian editions

**As printed:** А. Е. Әбілқасымова (Абылкасымова), Т. П. Кучер, В. Е. Корчевский, З. Ә. Жұмағұлова (Жумагулова). Алматы: Мектеп, 2019.
- Part 1: KZ (file 386) 176 pp., ISBN 978-601-07-1093-1 · RU (file 433) 184 pp., ISBN 978-601-07-1079-5.
- Part 2: KZ (file 387) 152 pp., ISBN 978-601-07-1094-8 · RU (file 434) 152 pp., ISBN 978-601-07-1080-1.
Read whole in the Kazakh edition (both parts). The Russian edition is the same work: prefaces and §-by-§ content correspond, and the spot-check of §19 (degree/radian measure) showed identical text, figures (35–43) and examples in the two languages; the preface states the book has 32 параграфов in total across the parts. Page numbers below are printed pages of the Kazakh parts (Part 2 restarts its own pagination at p. 3).

**Shape:** the most classroom-scaffolded of the three G9 algebra books. Every paragraph opens with key concepts ("Түйінді ұғымдар") and what-you-will-learn, teaches through worked examples (МЫСАЛ) with boxed **АЛГОРИТМ** summaries for the main procedures, and sets exercises in three levels — A (compulsory), B (medium), C (advanced, starred items hardest) — plus practice-oriented tasks, tasks "on solutions, mixtures and alloys", and tasks for electronic resources (QR codes). Each chapter ends with a multiple-choice self-test ("Өзіңді тексер!") and the course with a full glossary and answers. A long numbered review block (items 1–69) of the Grades 7–8 course opens Part 1 (pp. 4–19), including statistics items (mean, mode, **dispersion/variance** of grade tables, bar charts of prices) and applied blocks "Maths in my life", "Maths in business", "Maths in agriculture".

#### Part 1 (Chapters I–III)

**Chapter I — Equations, inequalities in two variables and their systems (pp. 20–71).**
- §1 (p. 20): non-linear equations in two variables; degree of F(x; y) = 0 (degree of the standard-form polynomial); a solution is a pair making a true equality; the *graph* of an equation as the set of its solutions; equivalent equations.
- §2 (p. 25): systems of non-linear equations. Four named methods, each with its own printed algorithm box: **algebraic addition** (5 steps), **substitution** (5 steps), **graphical** (3 steps — circle × line worked, solutions (0;4), (4;0)), **introducing a new variable** (u, v substitution). Exercises 2.1–2.14 A/B/C include parameter systems (for which p does the system have two/one/no solutions) and "prove no solution" items.
- §3 (p. 32): a further method — term-by-term multiplication/division for **homogeneous systems**, with an explicit warning, worked through, that this can introduce extraneous roots and that checking is compulsory. Very large exercise run 3.1–3.38: systems in three variables (3.36–3.37), systems already in (u, v) form, starred parameter systems (*3.37–*3.38: find all p for which the system has exactly one solution).
- §4 (p. 44): text problems via systems, with a 6-step modelling algorithm. Worked: steamer up/down a river (distance normalised to 1), two work brigades, and a full **alloy problem** (gold–iron melts) solved with a mass/concentration table — the section defines concentration m₁/m and percentage content explicitly. Exercises 4.1–4.24: motion, joint work, mixtures/alloys (silver alloys, acid solutions, glycerin), bank deposit interest (4.18), pool-filling pipes.
- §5 (p. 54): inequalities in two variables — solution set pictured as a region (the worked example shades the half-plane for y > x + 1 by the test-point logic); strict vs non-strict boundary conventions shown in figures (xy > 1, x² + y² ≤ 4, y < x²). §6 (p. 60): systems of non-linear inequalities — intersect the regions; exercises include modulus systems and *6.19 (parameter: for which a, c does the system cut a strip in the plane). Self-test (pp. 66–71): 27 multiple-choice items.

**Chapter II — Elements of combinatorics (pp. 73–98).**
- §7 (p. 73): basic concepts via **sets**: union/intersection, n(X∪Y) = n(X) + n(Y) − n(X∩Y) for the sum rule (with the three-set version in a worked olympiad-participation example); product rule on (x; y) pairs. A historical box gives the Lo Shu magic square.
- §8 (p. 79): factorial (1! = 1, 0! = 1 stated); permutations **without repetition** Pₙ = n! proved as a theorem; arrangements Aₙᵏ = n!/(n−k)! proved; equations in Aₙᵏ (8.6, 8.9).
- §9 (p. 84): combinations Cₙᵏ = n!/(k!(n−k)!) proved; four properties, each proved: Cₙ⁰ = Cₙⁿ = 1; symmetry Cₙᵏ = Cₙⁿ⁻ᵏ; Pascal rule; and **the theorem that an n-element set has 2ⁿ subsets, proved by mathematical induction** (induction is used here before it is formally taught in §18). §10 (p. 88): using the formulas — the three-decision routine (order all n / order a k-subset / choose a k-subset), PIN-code and combination-lock worked examples. §11 (p. 92): Newton's binomial — built by multiplying out (x + a)ⁿ, coefficients identified as Cₙᵏ; seven properties listed (number of terms, symmetry, Σ coefficients = 2ⁿ, general term Tₖ₊₁); Bernoulli's inequality (1 + x)ⁿ > 1 + nx set as a proof exercise (11.11). Self-test p. 98.

**Chapter III — Sequences (pp. 99–167 of Part 1).**
- §12 (p. 99): number sequence as a function on the naturals; finite/infinite; increasing, decreasing, **non-decreasing, non-increasing, stationary**; **bounded above / below / bounded** sequences (with a worked boundedness investigation); ways of defining: analytical (formula), recurrent, graphical (isolated points), verbal. A "prepare a report on a mathematician" box features Fibonacci (with a garbled-Cyrillic rendering of the biographical text in this scan — see defects note).
- §13 (p. 108): arithmetic progression — definition, the characteristic property as a *theorem with proof* (aₙ = (aₙ₋₁ + aₙ₊₁)/2 ⇔ AP), nth-term formula, and the **graph of an AP** (points on a line y = kx + b). §14 (p. 118): sum of the first n terms — pairing proof (Gauss 1…50 story), theorem statement, Sₙ from a segment formula Sₙ = (aₖ + aₖ₊ₙ₋₁)/2 · n proved; "Maths in business" deposit items.
- §15 (p. 127): geometric progression (motivated by the Kazakh game togyzkumalak: 2, 18, 162 pebbles) — nth-term formula; the characteristic property as **two theorems** (bₙ² = bₙ₋₁bₙ₊₁ for positive-term GPs, and its converse); deposit/compound-interest applications ("Жаңа өмірдегі математика": 10% and 8% deposits compared). §16 (p. 137): GP sum formula (shift-subtract proof), incl. Sₙ = (b₁(qⁿ − 1))/(q − 1) form and q = 1 case; "business maths": depreciation and price-growth problems; repdigit sums 2 + 22 + 222 + … (16.25).
- §17 (p. 145): infinitely decreasing GP — built geometrically by repeatedly halving a segment; Sₙ = b₁(1 − qⁿ)/(1 − q) rewritten to show the qⁿ term vanishing as n grows; **S = b₁/(1 − q)**; periodic decimals incl. pure and mixed: 3.(03) and 0.2(54) converted step by step; solving 1 + x + x² + … = 3.5. §18 (p. 153): **mathematical induction** as a formal method — the principle stated as a theorem (A(n) true for n = 1 and A(k) ⇒ A(k+1) gives all n), worked proofs: Σk = n(n+1)/2; 6²ⁿ − 1 divisible by 35; 2ⁿ > n; number of diagonals of a convex n-gon = n(n−3)/2. Exercises 18.1–18.30: prove the AP/GP nth-term formulas by induction, divisibility, inequalities (3ⁿ > 2n² + n for n ≥ 5), sums of cubes. Self-test (pp. 160–161), glossary (pp. 162–167 — it spans the whole course, including the Part 2 trig/probability terms), answers from p. 168.

#### Part 2 (Chapters IV–V)

**Chapter IV — Trigonometry (printed pp. 3–99).**
- §19 (p. 3): degree and radian measure of angles and arcs; rotation angles β = α + 360°·k; marking numbers on the number circle; applied items (wheel rim speed 23 rad/s, clock hands).
- §20 (p. 12): sin, cos, tg, ctg of an arbitrary angle via a point B(x; y) on a circle of radius R: sin α = y/R, cos α = x/R, tg α = y/x, ctg α = x/y, with the similar-triangles argument that the values do not depend on R; the full table of values 0–2π (6-кесте) and the unit-circle chart (61-сурет). Exercises include "find all α with sin α = 0 / 0.5, cos α = −1" (20.6) and impossibility checks (20.10: can sin α = 1.22? can cos α = −1.2?).
- §21 (p. 24): properties — domains and ranges of the four functions; sign charts by quadrant (table and four circle diagrams); periodicity: period defined for any function, with the integer-part [x] and fractional-part {x} functions graphed as non-trig examples; even/odd (cos even; sin, tg, ctg odd) via circle symmetry; least-period exercises up to y = sin 2πx + cos 4πx (21.24).
- §22 (p. 34): basic trig identities — sin²α + cos²α = 1 proved from Pythagoras on the unit circle; tg α = sin α/cos α, ctg α = cos α/sin α, tg α·ctg α = 1, 1 + tg²α = 1/cos²α, 1 + ctg²α = 1/sin²α (numbered formulas (1)–(8), collected in a table); identity proofs and "given one function, find the others" with quadrant signs.
- §23 (p. 44): reduction formulas — derived on the circle for π/2 ± α, then π ± α, 3π/2 ± α, 2π ± α, all twelve collected in one table (9-кесте), with the two standard memorisation rules discussed through questions; note the section also *proves the 2π-periodicity of sin x* on the way.
- §24 (p. 55): addition formulas for sin and cos — **cos(α − β) proved with the scalar product of vectors** OB·OC, the rest derived (sin via co-function); §25 (p. 62): tg and ctg of a sum/difference. §26 (p. 66): double-angle formulas (sin 2α, cos 2α in three forms, tg 2α, ctg 2α) and **power-reduction** formulas sin²α = (1 − cos 2α)/2; half-angle formulas cos(α/2) = ±√((1 + cos α)/2), tg(α/2) = sin α/(1 + cos α) = (1 − cos α)/sin α, with sign-by-quadrant worked examples (tg(π/8) = √2 − 1). A C-exercise (26.20) asks to *derive* the triple-angle formulas sin 3α = 3sin α − 4sin³α etc.
- §27 (p. 75): sum-to-product formulas (derived by the (α+β)/2, (α−β)/2 substitution; tg α + tg β = sin(α+β)/(cos α cos β) form included); §28 (p. 83): product-to-sum formulas. §29 (p. 89): identity transformations of trig expressions — a consolidation paragraph with long A/B/C runs (29.1–29.34), including proving Σ sin(kα)-type closed forms (29.16) and tg α + tg β + tg γ = tg α tg β tg γ for α + β + γ = π (29.23). Self-test pp. 99–100.

**Chapter V — Elements of probability theory (printed pp. 101–121).**
- §30 (p. 101): event and its types — random / certain (ақиқат, denoted U) / impossible (мүмкін емес, denoted V), elementary vs non-elementary events, opposite event Ā, equally possible outcomes; a summary table contrasts the three event types.
- §31 (p. 107): **classical definition** P(A) = m/n, with four properties stated (certain event has probability 1; impossible event 0; 0 ≤ P(A) ≤ 1 follows; P(Ā) = 1 − P(A)) — and **statistical probability** in the same paragraph: relative frequency w(A) = m/n over repeated trials (worked: 240 girls among 500 newborns → 0.48). Exercises mix both: lottery-ticket frequencies, a shooter with statistical probability 0.3 over 160 shots, and cross-topic items (a random integer from an interval is a root of a given quadratic — 31.13). NOTE: no addition or multiplication rules for probabilities of combined events are given anywhere in the book; two-dice items are solved by direct counting.
- §32 (p. 113): **geometric probability** in all three measures — area P = S(E)/S(D), length (point on a segment), and volume (point in a parallelepiped/cube with inscribed ball), including a region defined by the inequality y < 2x in the unit square. Exercises 32.1–32.16. Self-test p. 120; course review exercises pp. 122–135 (by topic: expressions, trig transformations, equations and systems, inequalities, functions and graphs, practical tasks, construction maths, business maths, driver-profession maths, olympiad tasks 71–78, combinatorics 86–92); glossary p. 136; answers pp. 143–150.

**Honest negatives (W3):** no addition/multiplication probability rules, no independent-events product rule, no conditional probability, no Bernoulli trials, no expected value — probability stops at classical + statistical + geometric definitions with counting-based exercises. No trigonometric equations as a topic (only "find all angles with a given sin/cos value" items in §20). No graphs of y = sin x etc. as curves (periodicity is developed on the circle and through [x]/{x} examples). No logarithms or exponentials. No vectors chapter (the dot product is borrowed inside §24's proof).

**Copy state / defects:** both Kazakh parts complete (Pt 1: 176 printed pp. in 177 PDF pp.; Pt 2: 152 printed pp. in 154 PDF pp.; answers, glossaries, TOCs all present). One scan-quality defect: on Pt 1 p. 106 the "prepare a report" box on Fibonacci is rendered in mojibake-like garbled Cyrillic in the scan (the surrounding pages are clean) — the box is a biographical side-note, no maths content lost. The same garbling affects the Gauss box on Pt 1 p. 126 and the De Morgan box on Pt 1 p. 158 (among §18's exercises) — again side-boxes only. Russian parts verified as the same edition (see clustering).

### W4 — Soltan et al., *Algebra 9* (Keleshek-2030, 2019) — Kazakh + Russian editions, +CD

**As printed:** Г. Н. Солтан, А. Е. Солтан, А. Ж. Жұмадилова (Жумадилова). Көкшетау (Кокшетау): Келешек-2030, 2019, 320 pp., issued with a CD.
- Kazakh edition (file 388): ISBN 978-601-317-423-5.
- Russian edition (file 438): ISBN 978-601-317-424-2.
Read whole in the Kazakh edition; the Russian edition is the same work (TOCs align item-for-item at near-identical printed pages — both editions are 320 pp.; the trig-table appendix and closing literature list are identical). Electronic version URL printed in both: keleshek-2030.kz/books/algb_9k.php (Kazakh) and keleshek-2030.kz/books/algb_9r.php (Russian). Page numbers below are printed pages (PDF page = printed page; the file's 319 PDF pages carry printed pp. 1–319 — the final colophon page, printed p. 320, is not in the file).

**Shape:** the largest G9 algebra book in the set — over 1,050 numbered exercises in the main text (continuous numbering across the whole book) plus ~176 more in Appendix 1 "Training exercises". Structure per the preface: (1) theory with worked examples, (2) control questions, (3) exercises in three levels A, B, C. Distinctive extras: an opening review that includes a real **statistics** block (mean, **dispersion (variance)**, standard deviation σ, with grouped-data tables), Diophantine (integer-solution) work in Chapter 1, graph/tree methods in combinatorics, drawn graphs of the trig functions in Chapter 4, and simple trig inequalities solved on the circle.

**Review of the Grade 8 course (pp. 7–17):** reference tables (powers, short-multiplication formulas, quadratic-inequality sign charts by discriminant case), statistics elements (x̄, Σ notation, dispersion = mean squared deviation, standard deviation as its root, interval midpoints for grouped data), then exercises 1–52 A/B/C and five "interesting problems" (continued fractions, Newton-labelled inheritance puzzle).

**Chapter I — Equations, inequalities in two variables and their systems (pp. 18–61).**
- §1 (p. 19): non-linear equations in two variables; degree; the general second-degree form ax² + bxy + cy² + dx + ky + p = 0; solution sets pictured as graphs (line, parabola, hyperbola, circle by completing the square). Worked examples include **solving in whole/natural numbers**: factorising 9x² − 4y² = 0 over the integers, and a full investigation of when m + n = mn has natural solutions; exercises 53–78 add rectangle/perimeter optimisation investigations and a Kazakhstan-map distance problem (68).
- §2 (p. 26): systems — substitution, algebraic addition, graphical method, and **symmetric systems** via the substitution p = x + y, t = xy (worked in full, incl. recovering (x; y) from (p; t) through a quadratic), plus the ratio method for homogeneous systems. Exercises 79–102 incl. parameter systems (94: for which a does the system have solutions…) and Diophantus' ratio problem (90).
- §3 (p. 34): text problems by systems (4-step scheme stated): joint work, two-digit numbers solved *two ways* (algebraic, and a Diophantine enumeration checking (a−b)² conditions), an inscribed-circle right-triangle investigation; ancient Egyptian and Chinese problems (119–120). Exercises 103–122.
- §4 (p. 40): inequalities in two variables — solution regions (disc x² + y² ≤ 9; half-planes), incl. counting lattice points in a disc (example 5: 21 integer pairs in x² + y² ≤ 5). §5 (p. 46): systems of inequalities in two variables — common regions, incl. applied feasibility problems (a runner's speed bounds; whether a right triangle with leg-sum ≤ 10 and hypotenuse > 9 can have area > 4¾ dm², settled by adding inequalities). §6 (p. 54): chapter review 166–202, self-test (p. 59), and a history spread on Diophantine equations (Hypatia, Fermat, Diophantus; the AM ≥ GM ≥ HM ≥ QM chain set to prove).

**Chapter II — Elements of combinatorics (pp. 62–91).**
- §7 (p. 63): basic concepts through **graphs**: route problems on vertex/edge diagrams, family-tree graphs; the product rule derived from pair tables; the sum rule via unions of sets, incl. inclusion–exclusion counting (among 1–100, numbers divisible by 2 or 3: 50 + 33 − 16 = 67). §8 (p. 70): permutations without repetition, Pₙ = n!, 0! = 1 motivated ("0! = 1 деп есептеу қабылданған"). §9 (p. 74): arrangements Aₙᵏ = n!/(n−k)! (tree-diagram derivation). §10 (p. 78): combinations Cₙᵏ with the two properties (symmetry; Pascal rule) stated and proved, and an equation Cₓ² = 2Cₓ₋₁¹ solved. §11 (p. 82): Newton's binomial — expansion, binomial coefficients, Pascal's triangle table to n = 9; consequences a = b = 1 and a = 1, b = −1 (ΣC = 2ⁿ, alternating sum 0); approximate computation (1.04⁵, 0.98⁵) as an application; comparing 11¹⁰ with 10¹¹ by binomial estimate. §12 (p. 88): chapter review 293–311, self-test, history (Tartaglia, Cardano, Galileo, Fermat, Pascal, Leibniz, Euler; Omar Khayyam's triangle).

**Chapter III — Sequences (pp. 92–146).**
- §13 (p. 93): number sequence, ways of giving it (formula, table, graph of isolated points, recursion), increasing/decreasing/constant, with monotonicity *proved* from aₙ₊₁ − aₙ (example 5: aₙ = n/(n+1)). §14 (p. 100): **mathematical induction** as an "axiom" with the 4-step proof scheme; worked: Σ(2k−1) = n²; Σk·k! = (n+1)! − 1; 4ⁿ + 2 divisible by 3; 2ⁿ > n² for n ≥ 5 — and, unusually, a "find the student's error" item (342: a false induction "proving" 2k + 2019 = 2(k+1) + 2019). Exercises 342–361 include Bernoulli's inequality with a "where did the student go wrong" variant (360).
- §15 (p. 107): arithmetic progression — nth-term formula **proved by induction**, characteristic property aₙ = (aₙ₋₁ + aₙ₊₁)/2 and the equidistant-terms property aₙ = (aₙ₋ₖ + aₙ₊ₖ)/2, AP as a linear function of n (points on a line). §16 (p. 115): AP sum (pairing proof, geometric staircase figure). §17 (p. 120): geometric progression — characteristic property bₙ² = bₙ₋₁bₙ₊₁; nth-term formula proved by induction; a bank-comparison problem using Bernoulli/binomial estimates (1.005ⁿ vs 1.006ⁿ). §18 (p. 127): GP sum formula; for 0 < q < 1 the staircase figure motivates Sₙ = b₁(1−qⁿ)/(1−q). §19 (p. 134): infinitely decreasing GP — S = b₁/(1−q) with the n → ∞ argument written out (qⁿ → 0); periodic decimals 0.333…, 5.2(7) converted; a chain of tangent circles in a 60° angle summed. §20 (p. 141): chapter review 486–518, self-test, history (Archimedes, Fibonacci).

**Chapter IV — Trigonometry (pp. 147–226).**
- §21 (p. 148): rotation angles α + 360°·n; degree and radian measure (1 rad = 180°/π ≈ 57°, 1° = π/180 ≈ 0.017 rad); marking points on the coordinate circle; applied items (flywheel making 42 revolutions, compass "rumb" = 1/32 of a circle, disc at 240 rpm). §22 (p. 157): sin/cos/tg/ctg of an arbitrary angle defined on the unit circle (sin α = y, cos α = x; tg via the tangent line x = 1, ctg via y = 1); signs by quadrant; values via identities; AND **simple trig inequalities solved on the circle**: worked example 6 finds all α with sin α ≤ −√3/2 as intervals [−2π/3 + 2πn; −π/3 + 2πn], and exercises 568/584–585 ask for all angles with sin α ≤ 1/2, cos α > √2/2, ctg α ≤ √3 etc. §23 (p. 170): trig functions and their properties — full property tables for sin/cos and tg/ctg (domain, range, parity, period, zeros, sign intervals, increase/decrease intervals); least periods *proved* (sin/cos 2π; tg/ctg π; period of cos 2x is π; of ctg(x/5) is 5π); **the graphs are drawn**: y = sin x on [0; 2π] plotted from a table (the sinusoid, Fig. 67) and y = tg x on (−π/2; π/2) (the tangentoid, Fig. 68); monotonicity intervals of y = sin(x/2) found. Exercises 594–631 include "for which x is sin x = 1 / cos x = −1 / sin x = 0…" (604) and domain/range investigations.
- §24 (p. 181): basic identities (the six standard ones). §25 (p. 187): reduction formulas — the full 28-formula table for π/2 ± α, π ± α, 3π/2 ± α, 2π − α, with the two memorisation rules. §26 (p. 194): addition formulas — cos(α − β) **proved by the scalar product of vectors**, sin derived via co-functions, tg/ctg formulas; a trapezoid-area problem (680) uses the sine area formula. §27 (p. 201): double-angle formulas **including the universal-substitution forms** sin 2α = 2tg α/(1 + tg²α), cos 2α = (1 − tg²α)/(1 + tg²α), and half-angle formulas with tg(α/2) = sin α/(1 + cos α) = (1 − cos α)/sin α. §28 (p. 208): sum-to-product (all eight, tg/ctg as quotients of sines). §29 (p. 214): product-to-sum (three formulas). §30 (p. 219): chapter review 785–829 — which contains the book's nearest approach to trig equations: 789 (find all x ∈ [0; 4π] with sin x = √2/2, cos x = 1/2, sin x = −√3/2, cos x = −1), 816 (find *all* values with 2cos 2x = 1, √2 sin 2x = 1, cos(2x + π/3) = 0, tg(2x − π/6) = 0), 827 (all x ∈ [0; π] satisfying 8cos²2x − 8cos²x − cos 2x + 1 = 0) — solved from unit-circle values, with **no arcsin/arccos machinery and no general solution formulas taught**. History: Ptolemy, al-Battani, Lobachevsky, Euler.

**Chapter V — Elements of probability theory (pp. 227–246).**
- §31 (p. 228): basic concepts — random / certain / impossible / compatible-incompatible (үйлесімді/үйлесімсіз) events, opposite events; equally likely outcomes; **classical definition** P(A) = m/n; the addition rule for incompatible events *derived* (P(A∪B) = P(A) + P(B)) and the **multiplication rule for independent events** (worked: a shooter's 0.5 and another's 0.8 give 0.4 for both hitting — stated for independent trials via outcome counting with combinations, C(15,5) lottery example). Exercises 831–854.
- §32 (p. 234): **statistical probability** — relative frequency stabilising (coin series 100/200/250 tosses tabulated: 0.49, 0.505, 0.508), P(A) ≈ m/n as n → ∞, Jacob Bernoulli named; exercises are frequency-table problems (shoe sizes, part lengths, salaries, ages). §33 (p. 238): **geometric probability** — area ratio; the broken-stick problem (two random breaks form a triangle with probability 1/4, solved by graphing the region x ≤ y etc. in the unit square — again Chapter I machinery); meeting-type and target problems. §34 (p. 243): chapter review 878–891, self-test (p. 245), history (Huygens, Kolmogorov; De Méré).

**Closing matter:** review of the Grades 7–9 algebra course (pp. 247–264, exercises 893–1050 by topic, incl. a functions block and a "statistics, combinatorics and probability elements" block with a dispersion computation, 1045); answers and hints (pp. 265–293, many C-level answers are worked solutions); subject index (p. 294); **Appendix 1** (pp. 295–316): ~176 training exercises across all five chapters; **Appendix 2** (pp. 317–318): table of sin/tg/ctg/cos for every degree 0°–90°; additional literature (p. 319).

**Honest negatives (W4):** no general theory of trigonometric equations (no arcsin/arccos/arctg, no solution formulas) — equation-type items appear only as "find all angles" exercises solvable from the circle; no addition rule for *compatible* events and no conditional probability; no Bernoulli scheme; no expected value; no logarithms. Secant/cosecant never used.

**Copy state:** printed pp. 1–319 present (of the imprint's 320 pp.) — only the final colophon page is missing from the file; appendices and answers included; the CD itself is of course not in the PDF. The Russian twin file (438) is the same edition (see clustering table).

### W5 — Shynybekov et al., *Geometry 9* (Atamura, 2019) — Kazakh + Russian editions

**As printed:** Ә. Н. Шыныбеков (Шыныбеков), Д. Ә. Шыныбеков, Р. Н. Жұмабаев (Жумабаев). Алматы: Атамұра, 2019, 176 pp.
- Kazakh edition (file 389): ISBN 978-601-331-574-4.
- Russian edition (file 437): ISBN 978-601-331-603-1 (176 pp. as well; print run 10,000 RU / 20,000 KZ per the colophons).
Read whole in the Kazakh edition; Russian edition verified as the same work in Step 1 (identical TOC, imprint-matched authorship; spot-checked interiors align). Page numbers are printed pages (PDF page = printed page in this scan: printed p. 175 carries the contents, PDF p. 176 the colophon).

**Shape:** the proof-dense Shynybekov style again (same house style as W2): numbered theorems with full proofs, boxed "өздерің дәлелдеңдер" (prove-it-yourself) prompts, A/B/C exercise levels, practical tasks, a trilingual (Kazakh–Russian–English) terminology table at the end of each chapter, and closing oral-question review lists for Grades 7, 8 and 9 (pp. 163–167). Opens with a Grade 8 review (printed pp. 5–11, exercises 0.1–0.40: quadrilaterals, areas, Pythagoras, basic trig ratios of acute angles).

**Chapter 1 — Vectors in the plane (pp. 12–71).**
- §1.1: vector concept (scalar vs vector quantities, physics examples); directed segment, modulus, zero vector; collinear, codirectional/opposite, orthogonal pairs; **equality of vectors defined through parallel translation**, with theorem: vectors matchable by a parallel translation (or its mirror statement) are equal; properties of equality (parallelogram criterion: AB⃗ = CD⃗ ⇔ ABCD is a parallelogram).
- §1.2: addition and subtraction — triangle rule (Chasles AB⃗ + BC⃗ = AC⃗) and parallelogram rule; **Theorem 1: addition is commutative and associative** (proved, incl. the collinear case); polygon (chain) rule for many vectors; opposite vector, a⃗ + (−a⃗) = 0⃗; difference a⃗ − b⃗ = a⃗ + (−b⃗); decomposition of a vector along two intersecting lines (1-айырма: if a⃗ = b⃗ + c⃗ then b⃗, c⃗ are components), **Theorem 2: any vector decomposes uniquely over two non-parallel directions**.
- §1.3: multiplication by a number — definition, |k·a⃗| = |k|·|a⃗|, the three laws as **Theorem 1** ((α·β)a⃗ = α(βa⃗); (α + β)a⃗ = αa⃗ + βa⃗; α(a⃗ + b⃗) = αa⃗ + β… stated and proved in parts); **Theorem 2: collinearity criterion** (b⃗ ∥ a⃗, a⃗ ≠ 0 ⇔ ∃α: b⃗ = αa⃗) with necessary/sufficient proof; linear combinations x⃗ = αa⃗ + βb⃗.
- §1.4: angle between vectors; **scalar (dot) product** a⃗·b⃗ = |a⃗||b⃗|cos φ; properties 1–3 stated (commutativity, homogeneity, distributivity — proofs of 1–2 deferred to "from formula (1)"), a⃗² = |a⃗|²; work of a force A = |F⃗||s⃗|cos φ as the physics application; applications worked in full: the parallelogram diagonal identity AC² + BD² = AB² + AD² + AB² + CD² form (sum of squares of diagonals = sum of squares of all sides) and the trapezoid midline theorem by vectors; a methodological note defines "векторлық алгебра" (vector algebra) and a 3-step scheme for vector solutions (translate to vector form → compute → translate back).
- §1.5: coordinates — **decomposition theorem** (every vector is uniquely c⃗ = xa⃗ + yb⃗ over a non-collinear basis; proved), orthonormal basis i⃗, j⃗ (орттар), coordinates a⃗ = (x; y); equal vectors ⇔ equal coordinates; coordinate rules for sum/difference/scalar multiple; radius-vector; coordinates of AB⃗ = (x₂ − x₁; y₂ − y₁) and |AB⃗| = √((x₂−x₁)² + (y₂−y₁)²) (distance formula).
- §1.6: scalar product in coordinates — a⃗·b⃗ = x₁x₂ + y₁y₂ **derived** from |a⃗ − b⃗|² expansion; perpendicularity condition x₁x₂ + y₁y₂ = 0; collinearity condition x₁/y₁ = x₂/y₂ (proportionality); cos(a⃗, b⃗) = (x₁x₂ + y₁y₂)/(|a⃗||b⃗|) with a worked check that ∠AOB is acute.
- §1.7: applications of the vector method — **the equation of a line**: direction vector and normal vector; two-point form (x − x₀)/α = (y − y₀)/β; derivation of the general form ax + by + c = 0 and slope form y = kx + r (k = −a/b called the бұрыштық коэффициент); intercept form x/a + y/b = 1; angle between lines via tan φ = (k₂ − k₁)/(1 + k₁k₂); angle-bisector equations of two lines (1.199); **distance from a point to a line** d = |ax₀ + by₀ + c|/√(a² + b²) derived by projecting onto the normal; fully worked multi-part example (sides, angle, altitude length of a coordinate triangle) and a vector proof that the altitudes of a triangle concur (orthocentre). Exercises to 1.205 (incl. centroid formula 3OO₁ = OA₁ + OB₁ + OC₁ type results).

**Chapter 2 — Transformations of the plane (pp. 72–107).**
- §2.1: central symmetry and axial symmetry, each with a distance-preservation theorem (1-теорема: central symmetry preserves distances; 2-теорема: axial symmetry preserves distances) and coordinate rules (about the origin: (x; y) → (−x; −y); about Ox: y → −y; about Oy: x → −x, with the distance computation shown). §2.2: rotation and parallel translation — translation as a transformation with formulas x′ = x + a, y′ = y + b and **Theorem 1: translation moves points along parallel lines by equal distances**; midpoint preservation under translation.
- §2.3: motion (қозғалыс) and superposition (беттестіру) — motion defined as a distance-preserving transformation; theorems: a motion sends a segment to an equal segment (1), a line to a line (1-салдар), a ray to a ray (2-салдар), an angle to an equal angle (4-салдар); superposition as the practical test of figure equality; **Theorem 2: superposition is a motion; Theorem 3: every motion is a superposition** (proved via three non-collinear points).
- §2.4: similarity transformations — definition (all distances multiplied by one k > 0), similarity coefficient, notation F ~ F₁; properties (k = 1 gives a motion; transitivity of similarity with multiplied coefficients); **homothety** (гомотетия): centre, coefficient, theorem that a homothety is a similarity transformation (proved with the sine rule in the OA₁/OA ratio computation); its six listed properties (line → parallel line, segment → parallel segment, angle preserved, circle → circle with radii in ratio k, …); constructions (triangle homothetic to a given one; a square inscribed in a triangle by homothety — 2.6 §2.5/2.6 practical with full analysis–construction–proof–investigation stages; two circles' common tangents via homothety centres). §2.5: similarity criteria for triangles — I (two equal angles), II (two sides proportional + included angle), III (three sides proportional), each proved by transporting one triangle onto a homothetic copy; separate criteria for right triangles (acute angle; legs proportional; hypotenuse + leg proportional). §2.6: applications of similarity — **angle-bisector property** (1-теорема: a bisector divides the opposite side proportionally to the adjacent sides, m = bc/(a + b), n = ac/(a + b) formulas), and the **area ratio of similar figures = k²** (practical task: S(F₁)/S(F) = k²), with construction problems (inscribing a square in a triangle; common tangents).

**Chapter 3 — Solving triangles (pp. 108–125).**
- §3.1: **cosine rule as Theorem 1** (all three forms), proved by vectors: a² = |BC⃗|² = |AC⃗ − AB⃗|² = b² + c² − 2bc cos A; consequences (Pythagoras as a special case; comparing a² with b² + c² decides acute/right/obtuse). **Sine rule as Theorem 3**: a/sin α = b/sin β = c/sin γ = 2R, proved through S = ½bc sin α and R = abc/(4S); acute/obtuse altitude cases handled separately. Worked: altitude foot formula AD = (b² + c² − a²)/(2c). Formula collection used throughout: h_b = 2S/b, r = S/p.
- §3.2: solving triangles proper — the four standard cases worked as *algorithms with discussion* (given c, ∠A, ∠B; given a, b, ∠C; the river-width measurement problem solved by sine + cosine rules in sequence). A group "ТОПТЫҚ ЖҰМЫС" task uses S = abc/(4R) and S = pr in both directions.
- §3.3: some applications of trigonometry — an essay-style section on **how to plan a solution** (analysis of the data, choosing which unknowns to find first, checking feasibility: e.g. data sets that determine no triangle), with two long worked measurement problems (distance between two points across a river; inradius of an isosceles triangle through 2R = AB/sin α and half-angle steps).

**Chapter 4 — Circle. Polygons (pp. 126–162).**
- §4.1: **length of a circle** — the theorem that for any two circles C₁/C₂ = R₁/R₂, proved with inscribed regular n-gons and a limit statement Pₙ → C as n → ∞; π defined as C/(2R); C = 2πR; arc length l = παR/180° and **l = Rα** (radian); history (Archimedes, al-Kashi's 16 digits, Euler's π notation). §4.2: **area of a circle** — Sₙ = ½·rₙ·Pₙ for inscribed polygons and the limit Sₙ → S giving **S = πR²** as a theorem; sector area S = πR²α/360 = αR²/2; segment area = sector ∓ triangle (sign by α < or > 180°).
- §4.3: inscribed and circumscribed quadrilaterals — **inscribed-angle theorem** (an inscribed angle equals half its arc; three centre positions proved; corollary: angle on a diameter is 90°); **Theorem 2: in a cyclic quadrilateral opposite angles sum to 180°**; **Theorem 3 (converse): if opposite angles sum to 180°, a circle can be circumscribed** (contradiction proof); **Theorem 4: in a circumscribed quadrilateral the sums of lengths of opposite sides are equal** (tangent segments from one vertex); **Theorem 5 (converse)**: equal opposite-side sums ⇒ an incircle exists. Exercises include "prove: a parallelogram that is cyclic is a rectangle", bisector-intersection characterisations.
- §4.4: proportional segments in a circle — **Theorem 1: intersecting chords** AE·BE = CE·DE (via similar triangles); **Theorem 2: secant from an external point** AP·BP = CP·DP, with corollary: tangent² = secant × its external part (KP² = BP·AP); **Theorem 4: in a right triangle each leg is the geometric mean of the hypotenuse and its projection** (AC² = AB·AD), altitude-on-hypotenuse relations CD² = AD·BD as **Theorem 5**; metric relations in right triangles summarised. Applied items: Earth's radius 6370 km — distance to the horizon from 4 km altitude; a bridge-arch chord problem (4.74).
- §4.5: polygons — regular polygon defined; **Theorem 1: a regular polygon has both an incircle and a circumcircle, concentric**; **Theorem 2: side in terms of radii**, aₙ = 2R sin(180°/n) = 2r tg(180°/n), with r = R cos(180°/n); area formulas Sₙ = ½·n·aₙ·r and aₙ² = 4(R² − r²) collected in a group-work box; constructions of regular pentagon/hexagon/decagon (Gauss's 1796 construction referenced); **Theorem 3: any two regular n-gons are similar** (homothety proof), with corollaries P′/P = r′/r = R′/R. Review exercises 4.99–4.154 and a final planimetry Q&A (pp. 163–167); answers pp. 168–175.

**What this book does NOT do (honest negatives):** no area of a triangle via coordinates/shoelace; no circle equation (x − a)² + (y − b)² = r² (the coordinate method is applied to lines only); no ellipse/hyperbola equations (they are named once in the Grade-9 review questions as curves whose equations were met in algebra, not treated here); similarity of areas is only the k² ratio for figures — no separate treatment of areas of similar *triangles* beyond it (it is inside §2.6's practical task); no trigonometric *functions* beyond acute-angle ratios (right-triangle solving is formula-driven); no vectors in space.
**Copy state:** complete, 176 printed pages; answers and both appendices present (printed p. 175 is the contents page). No copy defects spotted; the scan's printed-page numbers match the PDF page numbers.

### W6 — Smirnov & Tuyakov, *Geometry 9* (Mektep, 2019) — Kazakh + Russian editions

**As printed:** В. А. Смирнов, Е. А. Тұяков (Туяков). Алматы: Мектеп, 2019, 184 pp.
- Kazakh edition (file 390): ISBN 978-601-07-1096-2.
- Russian edition (file 435): ISBN 978-601-07-1098-6 (176 pp.).
Read whole in the Kazakh edition; the Russian edition is the same work (Step 1 verification). Page numbers are printed pages. Same national-curriculum skeleton as W5 (Shynybekov) — four chapters with the same titles — but a different internal build: each motion gets its own paragraph, the circle material is split across Chapters 3 and 4, and there are two starred extras (§7* line equation is starred here too, and §24* trig functions of arbitrary angles). A/B/C exercise levels; a multiple-choice self-test closes each chapter; an approximate-values trig table is appended (p. 170).

**Grade 8 review (pp. 5–13):** numbered problem sets only (angles; lengths; right-triangle ratios; areas; the coordinate plane and the circle equation) — no theory recap.

**Chapter 1 — Vectors in the plane (pp. 14–44).**
- §1: vector concept, modulus, collinearity, equality. §2: sum (triangle and parallelogram rules), commutativity (1-қасиет) and associativity (2-қасиет) proved, polygon rule; difference. §3: multiplication by a number t — three laws listed, the third (distributivity t(a⃗ + b⃗) = ta⃗ + tb⃗) proved via the proportional-segments theorem; difference as a⃗ + (−b⃗). §4: **decomposition** — first the collinear theorem (b⃗ ∥ a⃗ ⇒ ∃! t: b⃗ = t·a⃗, proved), then the decomposition theorem over two non-collinear vectors (c⃗ = t·a⃗ + s·b⃗ unique, proved); applications: midpoint formula OC⃗ = ½(OA⃗ + OB⃗), the section formula OC⃗ = (1 − t)OA⃗ + tOB⃗, centroid XO⃗ = ⅓(XA⃗ + XB⃗ + XC⃗) (exercise 13, §4).
- §5: angle between vectors; **scalar product** a⃗·b⃗ = |a⃗||b⃗|cos φ; scalar square; properties 1–3 stated; work A = F⃗·a⃗ = |F⃗||a⃗|cos φ. §6: coordinates — unit vectors i⃗, j⃗; **theorem: a⃗ = xi⃗ + yj⃗ iff its coordinates are (x; y)**; radius-vector; sum theorem in coordinates; A₁A₂⃗ = (x₂ − x₁; y₂ − y₁); distance |A₁A₂| = √((x₂−x₁)² + (y₂−y₁)²); **scalar product in coordinates a⃗₁·a⃗₂ = x₁x₂ + y₁y₂ derived** by expanding in i⃗, j⃗. §7* (starred): **equation of a line** — normal vector; theorem: a line is given by ax + by + c = 0 with n⃗(a; b) as normal; two-point form (x − x₁)/(x₂ − x₁) = (y − y₁)/(y₂ − y₁) as a theorem; slope form; parallelism ⇔ collinear normals; angle between lines via cos φ = |n⃗₁·n⃗₂|/(|n⃗₁||n⃗₂|); **parametric equations** x = x₀ + kt, y = y₀ + lt with direction vector m⃗(k; l); **distance from a point to a line as a theorem: h = |ax₀ + by₀ + c|/√(a² + b²)**, derived through the parametric form. Self-test p. 44.

**Chapter 2 — Transformations of the plane (pp. 46–92).**
- §8: parallel translation — three properties (preserves distances, segments/rays/lines, angles), each proved or set as prove-it-yourself; Kazakh ornament (ою-өрнек) translation tasks; images of circles and lines given by equations (exercises 17–20, §8). §9: axial symmetry — properties 1–3 (distance preservation proved); symmetry axes of standard figures; ornament tasks. §10: central symmetry — properties 1–3; "a quadrilateral with a centre of symmetry is a parallelogram" (exercise 19, §10). §11: rotation; **centre of symmetry of n-th order** (360°/n rotation; the equilateral triangle's centre is 3rd order — worked). §12: **motion** — defined as a distance-preserving point correspondence; properties: motion sends line/ray/segment to themselves (1), preserves angles (2, proved), **composition of two motions is a motion (3, proved)**; equality of figures defined through motion; the theorem linking figure equality and triangle equality, with the proof that any two equal triangles are related by a motion (translation + rotation, or + axial symmetry); symmetry degree of a figure (how many self-motions it has — six cases enumerated for a triangle).
- §13: similarity of figures; homothety — **theorem: a homothety is a similarity transformation with the same coefficient** (proved: A′B′ = k·AB from OA′ = k·OA, OB′ = k·OB); property 1 (similarity sends segment/ray/line to segment/ray/line); property 2 (preserves angles); **theorem: areas of similar triangles S′ = k²S** (via S = ½ab sin A), then **theorem: for any convex polygons, S′ = k²S** (triangulation proof), with the corollary for arbitrary similar figures. §14: similarity criteria of triangles — **1st criterion** (two proportional sides + included angle), **2nd** (two angles), **3rd** (three proportional sides), each stated as a theorem and proved by the homothety-plus-motion transport; corollaries for right triangles; a long applied run (river width, Baiterek monument 97 m, pyramid shadow 93 m, mirror method) in exercises 18–50 (§14). Self-test pp. 91–92.

**Chapter 3 — Solving triangles (pp. 94–122).**
- §15: **sine rule** (theorem, proved by dropping an altitude: CH = b sin A = a sin B); the bisector property proved again here as a theorem (AD/BD = AC/BC via the sine rule in ACD/BCD); distance-to-an-inaccessible-point worked example (AC = AB·sin B/sin C ≈ 122 m from table values). §16: **cosine rule** (theorem, **proved by vectors**: AB⃗² = (AC⃗ − BC⃗)² …); the parallelogram diagonal theorem (sum of squares of diagonals = sum of squares of all sides); **median theorem: m_c = ½√(2a² + 2b² − c²)** (proved by completing to a parallelogram); **Heron's formula as a theorem** (S = √(p(p−a)(p−b)(p−c)), derived from S = ½ab sin C with cos C from the cosine rule); the bisector-length formula l_c = √(ab − c′c″) set as C-exercise 17.
- §17: inscribed angles — central angle; **theorem: an inscribed angle equals half its arc** (cases proved); the tangent–chord angle theorem (an angle with vertex on the circle, one side a chord and the other tangent, equals half its intercepted arc). §18: angles connected with a circle — **theorem: an angle with vertex inside the circle = half the sum of its arcs** (∠ACB = ½(AB + DE), proved); corollaries; **theorem: an angle with vertex outside = half the difference of its arcs**; the tangent–secant angle case. §19: segments connected with a circle — **1-theorem: intersecting chords** (AE·BE = CE·DE); **2-theorem: two secants from an external point** (AE·CE = BE·DE); **3-theorem: tangent and secant** (AE² = BE·CE) — all three proved by similar triangles; problems on common tangents of two circles. Self-test pp. 121–122.

**Chapter 4 — Circle. Polygons (pp. 124–170).**
- §20: triangles and circle — inscribed/circumscribed definitions; the two Grade 7 centre theorems recalled; **the sine rule restated as a theorem in the form a/sin A = b/sin B = c/sin C = 2R and proved** (via an inscribed angle on a diameter); **theorem: R = abc/(4S)** (proved from S = ½ab sin C); **theorem: r = 2S/(a + b + c) = S/p** (proved by splitting into three triangles); starred close: **excircles** — a circle tangent to one side and the extensions of the other two; theorem: every triangle has three excircles, with centres at the intersections of external bisectors (proved); r_a = 2S/(b + c − a) as exercise 18.
- §21: quadrilaterals and circle — **theorem: a quadrilateral has a circumcircle ⇒ opposite angles sum to 180°**, and the converse as a starred theorem (Теорема*: opposite angles sum to 180° ⇒ a circumcircle exists); tangential quadrilaterals: **theorem: an incircle exists ⇒ opposite side sums are equal** (AB + CD = AD + BC, proved by tangent segments) and its converse indicated; **theorem: r = S/p** for a tangential quadrilateral. §22: regular polygons and circle — **theorem: each angle of a regular n-gon = 180° − 360°/n**; **theorem: every regular n-gon has a circumcircle**, with **R = a/(2 sin(180°/n))**; perimeter-ratio theorem for two regular n-gons (P′/P = R′/R); **theorem: every regular n-gon has an incircle**, with **r = a/(2 tg(180°/n))** and the corollary a = 2r·tg(180°/n); **theorem: r = S/p**; circumference as the limit of inscribed-polygon perimeters (the limit statement is given verbally: as n grows the perimeter "approaches" C); Archimedes' bounds 3 10/71 < π < 3 1/7 quoted; C = 2πR; radian measure of an angle; arc length l = πRφ/180.
- §23: area of a circle and its parts — **theorem: S(circle) = ½·C·R = πR²** (argued by the polygon limit: Sₙ = ½·Pₙ·rₙ with rₙ → R, Pₙ → C); sector: S = πR²φ/360, and **theorem: sector area = ½·l·R** (l = arc length); segment: S_seg = πR²φ/360 − ½R² sin φ (worked: 60° segment of a unit circle = π/6 − √3/4); Hippocrates' lunes and the arbelos in C-exercises. §24* (starred, pp. 151–155): **trigonometric functions of arbitrary angles** — the unit circle; sin φ and cos φ as the ordinate/abscissa of the point obtained by rotating A₀(1; 0) through φ (any φ: negative, > 360°); identities: sin(φ + 360°) = sin φ, sin(φ + 180°) = −sin φ, sin(−φ) = −sin φ, sin(90° − φ) = cos φ (and cos counterparts); tg φ = sin φ/cos φ (undefined at φ = 90° + 180°k), ctg φ = cos φ/sin φ; **theorem: sin²φ + cos²φ = 1 for every φ** (proved from the point's coordinates); trig functions of a *numerical* argument introduced (sin(π/6) = sin 30° etc.), with the note that the full theory comes in Grade 10 algebra. Self-test pp. 156–157.
- Grade 9 course review (pp. 158–168); **appendix: table of approximate values of sin and tg, 0°–89°** (p. 170); subject index (p. 171); answers (pp. 173–184).

**Differences from W5 (Shynybekov G9), for the merge:** Smirnov proves the cosine rule by vectors and adds the median formula and Heron's formula as theorems in the solving-triangles chapter (Shynybekov reaches Heron only implicitly); Smirnov has the interior/exterior circle-angle theorems (§18) and excircles (§20) which Shynybekov lacks; Shynybekov has the angle-bisector *length* apparatus in §2.6 and the orthocentre/line-equation applications in greater depth, plus the trilingual glossaries; Smirnov's §24* gives the only G9 treatment of sin/cos for arbitrary (incl. negative and > 360°) angles in the band — the algebra books develop the same material independently.
**Honest negatives (W6):** no Ptolemy theorem; no Menelaus/Ceva; no constructions chapter for regular polygons (constructions appear only as exercises); the circle-area and circumference limits are stated, not epsilon-proved (the book says so itself); no coordinate equation of a circle in the main text (only in the Grade 8 review set).
**Copy state:** complete, 184 printed pages; answers, index and trig table present. PDF page = printed page in this scan.
