### W7 — Soltan et al., *Geometry 9* (Keleshek-2030, 2019) — Kazakh + Russian editions, +CD

**As printed:** Г. Н. Солтан, А. Е. Солтан, А. Ж. Жұмадилова (Жумадилова). Көкшетау: Келешек-2030, 2019, 240 pp., with CD.
- Kazakh edition (file 391): ISBN 978-601-317-429-7.
- Russian edition (file 439): ISBN 978-601-317-432-7.
Read whole in the Kazakh edition (printed pp. 7–239; PDF page = printed page). Russian edition = same work (TOCs align; both 240 pp.). Continuous exercise numbering 1–452 plus appendices. Same A/B/C three-level scheme and "өзіңді тексер" self-tests as W4.

**Opening review (pp. 7–16):** formula plates — Thales and proportional segments, midlines, the bisector property, right-triangle metric relations (h² = BH·HA, a² = c·BH…), Pythagoras, basic trig identities, the unit semicircle definitions of sin/cos/tg/ctg for 0°–180° with sin(180° − α) = sin α etc., a complete area-formula plate for all quadrilaterals and triangles (incl. S = ½d₁d₂ sin φ), midpoint and distance formulas and **the circle equation (x − a)² + (y − b)² = R²**; 24 review questions and exercises 1–29.

**Chapter I — Vectors (pp. 17–60).**
- §1: vector concept; collinear vectors; codirectional/opposite; **theorem: a vector equal to a given one can be laid off from any point, uniquely**; equality via parallelogram (AB⃗ = DC⃗ in ABCD). §2: addition (triangle and parallelogram rules) and subtraction; **theorems: commutativity and associativity of addition, both proved**; polygon rule A₁A₂ + … + Aₙ₋₁Aₙ = A₁Aₙ; opposite vector; centroid identity for medians (OA⃗ + OB⃗ + OC⃗ = 0⃗ worked). §3: multiplication by a number, its three laws proved (property 3° via non-collinear decomposition); **theorem (collinearity condition): b⃗ = x·a⃗ has a solution x iff a⃗, b⃗ are collinear**; midline and median-sum proofs by vectors. §4: decomposition over two non-collinear vectors — **uniqueness theorem proved** (via the proportional-segments/Thales theorem, which is itself §4's second theorem); worked: bisector vector AL⃗ = (3/5)AB⃗ + (2/5)AC⃗ for a 2:3 bisector split; the section formula XC⃗ = (n·XA⃗ + m·XB⃗)/(m + n) proved.
- §5: coordinates — unit vectors i⃗, j⃗; radius-vector; theorem: equal vectors have equal coordinates and conversely (proved); coordinate rules; midpoint coordinates from vectors; collinearity ⇔ proportional coordinates (with the zero-coordinate caveat). Worked: centroid coordinates M = ((x₁+x₂+x₃)/3; (y₁+y₂+y₃)/3). §6: angle between vectors; **scalar product** a⃗·b⃗ = |a⃗||b⃗|cos φ, scalar square; perpendicularity ⇔ a⃗·b⃗ = 0; **theorem: a⃗·b⃗ = x₁x₂ + y₁y₂** (proved by the |b⃗ − a⃗|² route and the collinear case); cos(a⃗, b⃗) formula; worked gems: median length MA² = ½(b² + c²) − a²/4…, perpendicular medians of an isosceles triangle giving ∠A ≈ 37°, and a proof that cos 15° = (√6 + √2)/4 by dotting two unit vectors at 45° and 30°. §7: applying vectors — resultant of two forces (√19 N), **the cosine rule proved by vectors** (c² = a² + b² − 2ab cos C from |AB⃗|²), two-point equation of a line derived from y = kx + b, perpendicular lines via direction vectors (AB⃗·CD⃗ = 0 ⇔ a ⊥ b). §8: chapter review (130–147), self-test, history (Grassmann, Hamilton, Wessel, Gibbs, Heaviside, Weyl).

**Chapter II — Transformations of the plane (pp. 61–100).**
- §9: transformations and motions — motion defined (distance-preserving bijection); **theorems: a motion sends a segment to an equal segment, an angle to an equal angle**; equal figures; central symmetry, axial symmetry, translation, rotation — *each proved to be a motion* (1-есеп…4-есеп as worked proofs); compositions named (two axial symmetries = translation or rotation — "сызықтық симметрия"). §10: using motions in problems — shortest-path (bridge) problem by translation, rotation by 90° for a two-villages problem, etc.
- §11: **homothety and similarity transformations** — OX⃗₁ = k·OX⃗ incl. k < 0; three basic properties of similarity; **theorem: a homothety is a similarity transformation** (B₁A₁ = |k|·BA proved); any two circles are similar (proved); finding the centre of a homothety mapping one segment to another. §12: similar triangles — ΔABC ~ ΔA₁B₁C₁ notation; **all three criteria as theorems, proved by homothety** (transport ΔABC by a homothety of coefficient k, then a congruence criterion); right-triangle criteria listed; worked measurement problems (tree height by shadow, trapezoid diagonals, distance to an inaccessible point OH = ab/(a + h)-type formula). §13: similar polygons — definition; **theorems: perimeters of similar n-gons are in ratio k; areas in ratio k²** (both proved, area via triangulating from one vertex with S = ½ab sin C pieces); the A4-paper √2 investigation. §14: applying homothety/similarity (inscribing a square in a triangle, etc.). §15: chapter review (209–224), self-test, history (Thales, Euclid, Klein).

**Chapter III — Solving triangles (pp. 102–135).**
- §16: **sine rule** — theorem proved from S = ½bc sin A = ½ac sin B = ½ab sin C; worked bisector length by the sine rule. §17: **cosine rule** — theorem proved by coordinates/Pythagoras (a² = (b − c cos A)² + c² sin²A route); cos A = (b² + c² − a²)/(2bc) solved forms; acute/obtuse test by comparing squares; worked: smallest angle of the 17-25-28 triangle (α ≈ 37° via cos α = 0.8), **the median formula** m_a = ½√(2b² + 2c² − a²) as exercise 242, Almaty–Shu–Taraz map problem. §18: solving triangles — the three standard cases as a scheme (two sides + included angle; three sides with table/calculator angles to 0.1°; side + two angles), incl. an ambiguous-data discussion.
- §19: **inscribed angles** — central vs inscribed; **theorem: an inscribed angle equals half the degree measure of its arc** (three centre positions); angles subtending the same arc; angle on a diameter. §20: tangent and secant properties — **theorem: the angle between a tangent and a chord equals half the arc** (i.e. the inscribed angle in the alternate segment); **tangent-secant theorem AB² = AC·AD**; AB² = d² − R² corollary; **intersecting-chords theorem MA·MB = MC·MD**; bisector of an arc / CN = √(ab) − … worked (the internal bisector segment formula CN² = ab − mn derived: l = √(ab − mn)). §21: trig in area formulas — deriving **S = abc/(4R)** (from BC/sin A = 2R), **S = pr** (stated/proved in passing), and **Heron's formula** S = √(p(p−a)(p−b)(p−c)) derived via cos C and 1 − cos²C factorisation; a "prove it is impossible" area problem (parallelogram with diagonals 1, 3 sides a, b has S ≤ 4x·sin45° argument). §22: chapter review (304–313), self-test, history (Brahmagupta, al-Biruni).

**Chapter IV — Circle. Polygons (pp. 136–184).**
- §23: cyclic quadrilaterals — **theorem: a quadrilateral is cyclic ⇒ opposite angles sum to 180°** and the **converse as a separate theorem** (proof by contradiction through a point on the circle); **Ptolemy's theorem** proved in worked example 2 (AC·BD = AB·CD + AD·BC for a cyclic quadrilateral, via similar triangles) — note: proved but not named in the text I read; maximal-area remark (diagonals must be diameters). §24: circumscribed quadrilaterals — **theorem: opposite side sums are equal** (tangent segments) and its converse (теорема (шеңберге сырттай сызылған төртбұрыштың белгісі)) with a full proof.
- §25: regular polygons — definition; **theorem: every regular polygon has a circumcircle, and only one**; **theorem: every regular polygon has an incircle, and only one** (both proved via angle-bisector concurrence); centre, central angle 360°/n; constructions with compass and ruler: hexagon, triangle, square, pentagon (the golden-ratio construction x² + Rx − R² = 0 giving the decagon side), decagon, 15-gon; doubling the number of sides; the statement that the regular 17-gon is constructible (Gauss) while 7-, 9-gons are not; parquet tilings pictured. §26: sides and areas of regular polygons — aₙ = 2R sin(180°/n), aₙ = 2r tg(180°/n), a₃ = R√3, a₄ = R√2, a₆ = R; **theorems: similar regular n-gons have perimeters ∝ R (or r); area S = ½·P·r = p·r**; chord from two radii: b² = 2R² − 2R√(R² − a²/4)… (worked example 3).
- §27: **circumference** — defined as the limit of perimeters of inscribed regular n-gons as n → ∞; **theorem: C = 2πR** (from Pₙ = 2nR sin(180°/n) and Pₙ/(2R) → π); π ≈ 3.1416 irrational (Archimedes, al-Kashi, Lindemann named in §30's history); arc length l = πRn/180 and **l = Rα** with the radian remark (α/180° = α_rad/π). §28: **area of a circle** — **theorem S = πR²** proved by squeezing between inscribed and circumscribed polygon areas (Sₙ = ½Pₙrₙ, S̄ₙ = ½P̄ₙR, both → ½·C·R); sector area S = πR²n/360 = ½R²α; segment area = sector ∓ triangle (sign by n ⋚ 180°); the **lunes of Hippocrates** worked in full (sum of the two lunes on the legs = area of the right triangle). §29: chapter review (386–409), self-test, history (Euclid, Ptolemy, Gauss, Archimedes).
- §30: Grade 9 geometry course review (pp. 174–179, exercises 411–452 A/B/C), then final self-check assignments (pp. 180–188).

**Closing matter:** answers and hints (pp. 189–207, many with full reasoning); subject index (pp. 208–209); **Appendix 1** (pp. 210–211): trigonometric tables (sin/cos and tg/ctg); **Appendix 2** (pp. 212–238): table-and-figure training tasks T1–T17 by topic (fill-in tables for sine/cosine rules, "find the unknown element" picture tasks) plus a Grade 9 review set; additional literature (p. 239).

**Honest negatives (W7):** no inscribed/circumscribed *triangles* chapter as such (R and r for triangles appear only inside §21's formulas and exercises); no Ptolemy *name* attached to the cyclic-quadrilateral diagonal theorem; no Menelaus/Ceva; no power-of-a-point *name* (the results are present as the tangent-secant and chord theorems); no coordinate equation of a circle in the main text (it appears only in the opening review plate); no ellipse etc. Otherwise the planimetry is complete for the school course.
**Copy state:** printed pp. 1–239 present (of the imprint's 240 pp.) — only the final colophon page is missing from the file; answers, index and both appendices present; CD not in the PDF.

### W8 — Smirnov & Tuyakov, *Geometry 10* — HUMANITIES TRACK textbook (Mektep, 2019), Russian

**As printed:** В. А. Смирнов, Е. А. Туяков. «Геометрия. Учебник для 10 классов общественно-гуманитарного направления общеобразовательных школ». Алматы: Мектеп, 2019, 136 pp., ISBN 978-601-07-1147-1 (file 573, Russian; a Kazakh edition of the same book exists in the series but is not in this band). Read whole; page numbers are printed pages.

**Kind and level:** a genuine textbook (theory + proofs + questions + A/B exercises + multiple-choice "Проверь себя!" self-tests after each chapter), but for the **humanities/social track**: the solids themselves are introduced only in two starred (optional) paragraphs, and the working core of the book is positional stereometry (parallelism, angles, distances) plus vectors and coordinates in space, exercised almost entirely on the cube, regular prisms, regular pyramids and the regular tetrahedron. It opens with a full **review of the Grades 7–9 planimetry course** (printed pp. 4–19): polygons and quadrilaterals with criteria, trig functions of an acute angle, sine and cosine rules (stated), areas (incl. S = ½d₁d₂·sin φ for a convex quadrilateral), plane vectors (incl. the decomposition theorem over two non-collinear vectors), and plane coordinates (distance, circle equation (x − x₀)² + (y − y₀)² = R², line equation, scalar product in coordinates, cos of the angle between lines via normals). Exercises 1–97.

**Chapter I — Axioms of stereometry; mutual position of lines and planes (pp. 20–55).**
- §1: basic concepts — point, line, plane as idealisations; notation (∈, ∩); intersecting lines; a historical essay (Egypt, Euclid's *Elements*, the five Platonic solids matched to the elements). §2: the three **axioms of stereometry** (through two points a unique line; through three non-collinear points a unique plane; two planes with a common point meet in a line), axiom 4 (there exist four points not in one plane) and 5 (all planimetry axioms hold inside each plane); **Properties 1–3 proved**: a line with two points in a plane lies in it; a unique plane through a line and an outside point; a unique plane through two intersecting lines.
- §3* (starred/optional): figures in space — polyhedron, faces/edges/vertices, diagonals; tetrahedron, cube, parallelepiped (rectangular vs oblique); **nets (развёртки)** of cube and tetrahedron; motion and similarity in space defined. §4* (starred): prism (n-gonal, right/oblique, regular) and pyramid (n-gonal, regular), with counting tasks (vertices/edges/faces from n).
- §5: parallel lines in space — transitivity of parallelism (stated as a признак: two lines parallel to a third are parallel), with a long historical essay on Euclid's fifth postulate and Lobachevsky. §6: mutual position of lines — **skew lines (скрещивающиеся)** defined; **theorem (criterion of skewness): if one line lies in a plane and the other meets that plane at a point not on the first line, the lines are skew** (proved); classification scheme diagram. §7: line and plane — **theorem (criterion of parallelism of a line and a plane)** (proved), and the theorem that a plane through a line parallel to another plane meets it in a line parallel to the given one (proved). §8: parallel planes — **theorem (criterion of parallelism of two planes)** via two intersecting lines (proved by contradiction).

**Chapter II — Angle and distance in space (pp. 56–93).**
- §9: angle between lines in space; angle between **skew** lines defined via parallel transport; perpendicular skew lines; worked: angle between AC and BC₁ in a unit cube = 60° (equilateral ACD₁); cos of the angle between AC₁ and A₁B₁ in a unit triangular prism = √2/4 — computed by the cosine rule, which is the standard tool throughout.
- §10: distance from a point to a line in space (the perpendicular length; the practical method of choosing a convenient triangle ACD with AB as altitude); worked: distance from a cube vertex to B₁D₁ = √6/2. §11: line perpendicular to a plane — **theorem (criterion)**: perpendicular to two intersecting lines of the plane ⇒ perpendicular to the plane (full proof); Properties 1–3 (unique perpendicular through an outside point; a line parallel to a perpendicular is perpendicular; two lines perpendicular to one plane are parallel — all proved). §12: distance from a point to a plane; pyramid height; space diagonal of a rectangular parallelepiped √(a² + b² + c²) worked; distance from a cube vertex to plane CDA₁ = √2/2 via the area trick (AE = AD₁·… through triangle areas). §13: distance between a parallel line and plane, and between two parallel planes — **theorem: the distance does not depend on the chosen point** (proved via the parallelogram of perpendiculars). §14: distance between two lines — parallel case; **skew case via the common perpendicular**, with the reduction: distance between skew lines = distance from one line to the parallel plane through the other (worked: AA₁ and BD₁ in a unit cube = √2/2).
- §15: **orthogonal projection** and the **theorem of three perpendiculars** (stated and proved; converse offered as an exercise); properties of projection (a non-perpendicular line projects to a line; ratios on a line preserved, midpoints to midpoints). §16: angle between a line (slant) and a plane = angle with its orthogonal projection. §17: **dihedral angle** — linear angle, independence of the chosen plane proved; angle between two planes = smallest dihedral; perpendicular planes; **theorem (criterion of perpendicularity of two planes)** (proved); worked: dihedral angle at the base of a regular quadrangular pyramid with all edges 1 (linear angle SEO = 45°); Cheops pyramid problem (17.14).

**Chapter III — Rectangular coordinate system and vectors in space (pp. 94–118).**
- §18: vectors in space (as in the plane; addition laws listed; collinearity ⇔ b⃗ = t·a⃗). §19: **complanar vectors**; **theorem: if a⃗, b⃗, c⃗ are non-complanar, any vector d⃗ decomposes uniquely as d⃗ = xa⃗ + yb⃗ + zc⃗** (proved). §20: angle between vectors; **scalar product** a⃗·b⃗ = |a⃗||b⃗|cos φ (physical meaning: work A = F⃗·a⃗); perpendicularity criterion. §21: rectangular coordinates in space (абсцисса, ордината, **аппликата**); coordinate planes; **midpoint theorem** (coordinates of the midpoint = half-sums of the three coordinates, proved by projecting onto the three coordinate planes); Descartes essay. §22: **distance between two points** √((x₂−x₁)² + (y₂−y₁)² + (z₂−z₁)²) (theorem, proved) and the **equation of a sphere** (x − x₀)² + (y − y₀)² + (z − z₀)² = R², with the ball as the corresponding inequality. §23: coordinates of a vector; **theorem: a⃗ has coordinates (x; y; z) iff a⃗ = xi⃗ + yj⃗ + zk⃗**; sum theorem; a⃗₁·a⃗₂ = x₁x₂ + y₁y₂ + z₁z₂ **derived** from the cosine rule ((a⃗₁ − a⃗₂)² expansion); cos φ formula. §24* (starred): **equation of a plane** ax + by + cz + d = 0 (theorem: (a; b; c) are the coordinates of the normal vector; proved); parallel planes ⇔ proportional normals; angle between planes via normals; **distance from a point to a plane** h = |ax₀ + by₀ + cz₀ + d|/√(a² + b² + c²) derived.
- **General review** (pp. 119–128): ~48 angle-between-lines problems, then long drill lists by topic (angle line–plane; angle between planes; point-to-line; point-to-plane; between lines) — nearly all on the unit cube, unit regular prisms/pyramids and the regular tetrahedron. Subject index (p. 129); answers (pp. 130–135); TOC (p. 135).

**Honest negatives (W8) — important for the library:** this humanities-track book contains **no surface areas and no volumes** of any solid (no prism/pyramid area or volume formulas at all); no sections (сечения) of polyhedra as a topic; no bodies of revolution (no cylinder, cone, sphere geometry beyond the sphere's *equation*); no regular-polyhedra theory (the five solids appear only in the §1 history essay); no Euler formula; no vectors cross product. The natural-science-track Grade 10 geometry (which would carry areas/volumes) is **not** in the in-hand set — see the band gap statement. Also absent here: any Grade 10 algebra (see summary).
**Copy state:** complete (136 printed pp. + TOC); no defects spotted.

---

### W9 — Soltan et al., *Учимся решать задачи по стереометрии* ("Learning to solve stereometry problems") — PROBLEM-SOLVING MANUAL, Grade 10 (Keleshek-2030, 2020), Russian

**As printed:** Г. Н. Солтан, А. Е. Солтан, А. Ж. Жумадилова, С. Ш. Алибеков. «Учимся решать задачи по стереометрии: пособие для учащихся 10 класса общеобразовательной школы». Кокшетау: ИП Келешек-2030, 2020, 112 pp. **The ISBN field on the imprint page is printed blank** (file 737). Cover adds: "для учащихся 10, 11 классов естественно-математического направления" (for Grades 10–11 of the natural-mathematics track).

**KIND VERDICT (rule (d)):** this is **not a textbook**. Its own preface states what it is: an aid for pupils who have difficulty solving stereometry problems independently — it **prints full solutions to the problems of the Soltan et al. *Geometry 10* textbook (Keleshek-2030, natural-mathematics track)**, so that a self-found solution can be compared with the one in the book. The problem numbers are the textbook's own numbers (the solutions run from №11 to №651, non-consecutively — a selection). There is no systematic theory exposition: each of the 25 numbered sections (§§1–25, mirroring the textbook's paragraph numbering) consists of fully worked problems with figures; the needed facts are invoked inside solutions, not taught. It is filed as a **worked-solutions manual / problem book**, and it is the band's only window onto the natural-science-track Grade 10 course, whose textbook itself is NOT in the in-hand set.

**Contents (by its printed TOC, p. 3):**
- Introduction — introduction to stereometry; planimetry review problems (pp. 5–6). Notably these already use **lateral and total surface area of pyramids** (№12: a triangular pyramid with all lateral edges equal, S_бок = 96√3 cm², S_полн = 112√3 cm², find the base side; №13: lateral surface of a quadrangular pyramid via the median of a lateral face) and a cube net problem (№11) — i.e. the parent textbook teaches pyramid surface areas, which W8 (humanities track) does not.
- Part I. Axioms of stereometry; parallelism of lines and planes (pp. 7–29): §1 axioms and consequences (proof problems: two planes with two common points share the line through them; points of a broken line and a plane); §2 parallel lines and their properties; §3 skew lines (incl. an equality-of-segments proof by the cosine rule in ΔBDN); §4 mutual position of a line and a plane — **sections (сечения) of tetrahedra, pyramids and cubes by planes** are built and their areas/perimeters computed (№81: section of a regular tetrahedron by a plane through the midpoint of an edge parallel to a face plane; №83: cube section through a point on an edge parallel to two diagonals, area 8√3 dm²; №84: pyramid section perimeter); §5 parallel planes (sections by parallel planes; area ratios); §6 depicting figures; parallel projection and its properties (images of medians, midlines, circles → ellipses; projection of a trapezoid divides diagonals in the same ratio); §7 review exercises for Part I (incl. National Water Sports Centre building problem №131).
- Part II. Perpendicularity of lines and planes; angles and distances in space (pp. 30–68): §8 angle between two lines; perpendicularity of two lines (answers to 0.1°/arccos precision, e.g. ∠DMH ≈ 73°); §9 perpendicularity of a line and a plane (with section constructions); §10 perpendicular and slant; **theorem of two perpendiculars** (the Russian-school form of the three-perpendiculars theorem: HM² = HN·HK tangent-secant used in №229); §11 distances between lines and planes (incl. the distance from a point of one face of a Sherkala cliff problem, №250); §12 angle between a line and a plane; §13 dihedral angle; angle between two planes (incl. the leaning towers of Madrid problem №298: given sin α − cos α·ctg 2α = 0.25, find each tower's height ≈ 114 m); §14 perpendicularity of planes; §15 rectangular parallelepiped and its properties (existence question №322 solved by a system a² + b² = 9, a² + c² = 16, b² + c² = 25 leading to a² = 0 — no such parallelepiped); §16 **orthogonal projection of a plane figure** — the area law S_пр = S·cos φ used throughout (№350: can a projection have area 0.2ab / 0.25ab / 0.3ab — answered by cos φ ≤ 1; №352–353: a student's composed problem is shown to have **no solution** because the computed cos φ > 1 — a nice self-check example); a roof-economics problem comparing 15° vs 45° roof pitches (№352, б); §17 review exercises for Part II.
- Part III. Rectangular coordinate system and vectors in space (pp. 69–105): §18 coordinates in space (building lines/triangles from coordinates; locus problems №418); §19 distance between points; midpoint coordinates (incl. least-sum-of-squares point №440 — completing the square in three variables); §20 vectors and operations (chain simplifications; **the section formula OC⃗ = (n·OA⃗ + m·OB⃗)/(m + n) proved from Thales**, №457–458; tetrahedron median concurrence: the segments joining vertices to opposite faces' median-intersection points meet in one point M with OM⃗ = ¼(OA⃗ + OB⃗ + OC⃗ + OD⃗), №480); §21 collinear and complanar vectors; decomposition over three non-complanar vectors; §22 **scalar product** (incl. proving the line–plane perpendicularity criterion by vectors, №533; area of a triangle from two vectors S = ½|AB||AC|sin A with cos A from the scalar product, №535; angle between planes via a section and normals, №542); §23 equations of a sphere and of a plane (through a point with a given normal; through three points; plane through the midpoint perpendicular to a segment; angle between planes ADC and ABC in a tetrahedron via normals: arccos(2/√29), №562); §24 **equations of a line in space** — canonical and parametric forms, intersections with planes and with the sphere x² + y² + z² = 2x − 4y − 4z (№578), mutual position of two lines incl. proving skewness from inconsistent systems (№580); §25 review exercises for Part III.
- Part IV. Review of the Grade 10 geometry course (pp. 106–110): problems №643–651 mixing everything (a section of a tetrahedron proved to be an equilateral triangle, a cube inscribed in a pyramid №648, common perpendicular of two skew lines in a regular tetrahedron №650: a√6/5). (The earlier solutions №586–642 — incl. the sphere/plane locus questions №604 — belong to §25, Part III's review.)

**What it evidences about the missing textbook:** the parent (natural-mathematics track) Soltan *Geometry 10* teaches, beyond W8's scope: construction of **sections of polyhedra**, **surface areas of pyramids** (lateral/total), and the same coordinates/vectors/equations material as W8's Chapter III plus **equations of a line in space** (which W8 lacks). It still shows **no volumes** anywhere in the solved set and no bodies of revolution — but as a *selection* of solved problems it cannot prove the parent textbook lacks them; that question stays open for the library (see summary).

**Copy state:** complete, 112 printed pages. One copy defect: on printed p. 102 a stray red annotation ("Ответ m⃗(−4; −6; 12)", completing №600's answer) is printed/stamped across the text in the scan — cosmetic, no content lost. The blank ISBN is as printed (defect of the edition itself, recorded above).
